Rev. A. J. Garvy, S.J., in grateful appreciation

For long years serious thinkers have expressed their disappointment that more progress has not been made by Catholics in bringing into action all the magnificent resources for social reconstruction with which the Church supplies them and which Catholic tradition has accumulated. The names of Villeneuve-Bargemont, of Frederick Ozanam, of Bishop von Ketteler, of Leo XIII and Pius XI, recall but a few of the score of mighty leaders who have illustrated the cause of Modern Social Catholicism. They stand out like mountain tops that catch the sunlight of God's justice and are gilded by the charity of Christ, while below, the world still lies buried in the shadows of the night.

But as Ozanam in his own day wisely said, and as we must realize in ours, the pertinent question men will ask of us is: What are we ourselves doing to aid in the solution of the social problem, what are we doing to make known to the world the teachings of our Church, and, most important of all, do we intend to carry them into effect?

Communists are active everywhere the world over. They never cease to labor, plot, and agitate to carry out their plans for the overthrow of every duly constituted government. The revolution they preach is above all meant to sound the death knell, not merely of the Catholic Church, but of all Christianity and all religion. It would mean no less than complete enslavement of all humanity to the Moscow dictatorship of espionage, violence, and bloodshed, with the utter suppression of every vestige of freedom of the press, freedom of action, and freedom of worship. 

But if it is obvious to us all that in this supreme hour of the world's need Catholic men and women must take a foremost place vii

viii 

of leadership in the social reconstruction on which the Church herself insists, it is equally clear that they cannot be expected to do so without the proper training. 

It is incumbent not merely on the heads of seminaries and Catholic universities, but on all those as well who are responsible for our Catholic secondary education, to offer systematic courses, obligatory on every student, in which the Catholic social doctrine will be taught in an intelligent, thorough, and convincing way. 

For this, the first requisite is a specially prepared and adequate text, equipped with all the most scientific modern apparatus, simple, clear, interesting, and as complete as such a book can well be made for the purpose it is to serve. 

Candidly we believe that the present volume fairly and fully meets all these conditions. With this book in the teacher's hand, there can be no further reason hereafter why every Christian high school should not carry in its curriculum a course in Christian Sociology - let us plainly use the language which the Holy See itself does not hesitate freely to employ.

And here be it borne in mind that out of every ten high-school students, one only will proceed to continue his education at college. Shall the other nine be thrust out into the world with no solid basis of training for that most important work of Social Catholicism to which the Church so solemnly summons them? 

What can be expected of them except that they in turn will be blind leaders of the blind? What knowledge can they have to face the issues of the day except what they themselves laboriously and perhaps very inaccurately acquire through their own private efforts? And how many will have the time or patience to make at least this belated endeavor to build up a sound foundation for that Catholic social leadership for which the Catholic high school was meant to prepare them, in a scientific manner, within the scope of its own proper limits?

With special gratification, therefore, we welcome this book, issued at the present crucial moment, by a writer already familiar to the public through her Survey of Sociology that is now passing

PREFACE BY THE 

ix 

GENERAL EDITOR 

through successive editions. Her new book, naturally, has been written in a far more simple and easily comprehensible manner, and according to entirely different methods, suited to the needs of high-school pupils. 

Yet practically all the problems of supreme consequence in the social question of the day will here be found clearly, plainly, and logically treated. Beginning with the nature of society and social life in general, the author passes on to the family and the presentday loosening of the domestic bond. She considers the state, its rights, duties, and limitations; occupational society, including the industrial and agricultural problems; trade unionism, strikes, and arbitration; the coöperative movement in its various phases, embracing credit coöperation, producers' and consumers' coöperative societies; ownership and man's natural right to private property, both productive and consumptive; the errors of collectivism, of socialism and communism; international society, daily becoming a more significant study, with its issues of peace and war, and the workings of the world court; the supreme educational question, and the rights of Church and State in this regard; the urgent need in our day of proper housing for all our vast population; the larger aspects of recreation with the coming of shorter working hours; dependency and relief, which have absorbed public interest to an unwonted degree during the past years; charity agencies of many kinds, with their social-service exchanges, their community drives and chests; our ever-present duties toward the blind, deaf, crippled, mental defectives, and others who else would become the derelicts of society; delinquency with its numerous causes that must be clearly understood if we would remove them, and with its modern systems of treatment, such as probation and parole; the race problem, which looms so large in our time, and especially the Negro question and "the morality of the color line"; finally, and most important of all, practical Catholic Action, with its watchwords of social justice and Christian charity for all. 

To the effective treatment of these subjects is added, by way of Appendix, an imperatively needed exposition of the postulates of

Catholic sociology, which offer the ultimate reason for its distinction from all purely secular studies in this field. As Catholics we postulate certain definite truths, which obviously must be elsewhere more fully elucidated. Such are, for instance, the existence of God, the freedom of the human will, the teachings of Christ, and the establishment by Him of an infallible Church. Our acceptance of these facts at once determines our attitude toward modern social questions. It shows us the fallacies inherent in an overwhelming proportion of the sociological literatures issued by the press for popular or educational use. For this reason a Catholic sociology text for Catholic schools becomes an absolute necessity.

Finally, present utility and timeliness are wisely consulted by a brief explanation of the National Industrial Recovery program, inaugurated in 1933, together with a listing of its numerous developments that are here brought to the notice of the student. 

Particular attention deserves to be called to the method adhered to by the author, of always giving in a brief, positive statement. boldly italicized, the fundamental thought or fact that is developed in the subsequent paragraph or short sectional treatment. 

The student has thus placed before him, in a few direct words, the entire substance of each explanation given in the book. Further educational facilities will be found in the division of the volume into units, or lessons, indicated at the end of the book, with questions attached to each, as also in the numerous illustrations and original charts throughout the work, and the bibliographies for every chapter.

The appearance of this book, then, during what has been deemed by some to be the most momentous social-economic crisis in the whole range of history, is an event of unusual consequence because of what it signifies to us. It is an earnest of a new Catholic social consciousness; of a more progressive Catholic social attitude toward the issues of our day; and finally, of a more unified, intelligent, and widespread participation of our Catholic population in striving to make actual and vital the great teachings con-

xi tained in the social encyclicals of our illustrious Pontiffs, Leo XIII and Pius XI. It is the book which, at this moment, every 

Catholic high school must feel that it needs to have. 

JOSEPH HUSSLEIN, S.J., PH.D. 

General Editor, Science and Culture Texts 

St. Louis University.

AUTHOR'S PREFACE 

Sociology calls our attention in a very special way to one of our most fundamental natural laws: that men are by creation social beings. Even from early years we begin to realize that we are seriously dependent upon the help of others, and that others are likewise benefited by what we do for them. Wishing to increase the opportunity for this mutual helpfulness, men form themselves into social groups, such as the state, the school, and the economic group in which they earn their living. These societies, in their turn, depend upon the help of other social groups, so that not only must they work together in harmony, but they must actually coöperate with one another in mutual fellowship and understanding. This coöperation is essential if we are to make intelligent use of the abundant supplies of nature, so that all men will have a sufficiency of work, and a comfortable standard of living, and no longer will people starve in the midst of plenty. 

However, unmindful of society's fundamental law, and of the seriousness of upsetting the balance between individual and social welfare, most men have worked within recent years for their own personal gain, and have neglected their social obligations. The result we all know well: our social organization has neared collapse, and is still on perilous ground; there is the danger of a socialistic era on the one hand, and of state absolutism on the other; the family, the very foundation of society, is being insidiously undermined by those who believe in the tenets of eugenism. The need of a knowledge of what is and should be is strikingly apparent. Sociology supplies this need, and fortunately the vitally human interest of the subject makes a wide appeal, so that it is becoming increasingly popular as a study by both individuals and groups.

The object of this book is to discuss the principal societies to which man belongs, to present briefly some of the problems arising from social life, and to outline some of the remedies now xiii

xiv 

AUTHOR'S PREFACE 

being considered, where abuses exist. The author wishes not only to equip students with this necessary knowledge, but also with correct viewpoint, worthy goals, broad vision, just and unselfish attitude. Care has been taken to lay a foundation in the way of 

Catholic principles and philosophies and an appreciation of them. 

Spiritual evaluations of the activities of individual social life cannot help but emerge from such training. In this light the student will see his personal responsibility for the uplift of standards, both material and spiritual, and will be motivated to participate and to contribute his part willingly and generously. 

Although there are many works on introductory sociology, most of them contain a false philosophy of life, and false notions of biology and of social origins. They ignore the supernatural, the fact that man's true social good includes not only his temporal happiness, but also the salvation of his soul. They are based on the false theory that religion, morals, and all social activities are merely the results of an evolution, and dependent upon the conditions of time and place. A Catholic introductory sociology seemed, therefore, an urgent need. It was with this idea in view that the book was written.

The author wishes to express deepest gratitude to the several friends (particularly the Reverend A. J. Garvy, S.J., and the 

General Editor of this Series) who gave many valuable criticisms and suggestions.

New edition, 1939: When last revised, a discussion of the 

Social Security Act and the Wagner-Peyser Act was introduced into Chapter VIII, and particulars regarding the National Labor Relations Act were added to Chapter IX. In this new revision the only changes made have been to bring statistical data up to date wherever possible, and to insert in Chapter VIII particulars of the Fair Labor Standards Act, which was passed a few months after the last revision was made.

XV

Chapter I  

1. We all belong to many social groups. 

It is not hard to realize that we do not live our lives alone. We are all members of a family, to which we are closely bound by ties of relationship and affection, and our feeling of intimate union with it is entirely different from our regard for other fam- 

Photo, J. Jay Hirz

Many of us are members of some society such as a boys' club. ilies. However, we do not remain isolated within the family but are members of numerous other social groups. We are enrolled in a school or study group, for example, and although this group is entirely distinct from our family circle, we nevertheless feel that we belong to this society, too, in a special manner. We have also our own particular group of companions or playmates, toward whom we likewise feel a bond of comradeship, affection, and interest. And we are members of various other societies, for example, a sodality, or a corporation for which we work, or a club or association such as the Boy Scouts, or a civic league. We all 1

belong to a religious society, our Church, and have a special regard, first for our pastors and the members of the parish, and then for the whole Church of which our parish forms but one unit. 

Again we feel in a group apart from those who belong to other religious bodies. 

Then, too, all of us live in a village, or in a small town, or in a certain district of a large city. We feel a certain loyalty toward the other residents in our neighborhood, and we have a pride in our 

Pauls' Photos 

The "jungles" near the railroad yards in any city, is usually the gathering place for vagrants. This is a type of undesirable social group. locality or a desire to improve it. We have our local town hall or municipal building, our own police court and prison, our local hospital, schools, churches, stores, factories, and workshops. This larger group is called our civic community, or "neighborhood group."

Many such groups united make up a county, and these, in turn, form a state, such as Massachusetts, California, or Illinois. These then unite to form a nation, the United States of America. The whole nation is also called a "state," and it is in this sense that the

word is generally used in sociology. We have feelings of loyalty toward our country; we are anxious to uphold its laws; and we wish others to conform to our ideal. 

Now, it must be noted that our state is but a unit in the world, where there are many states, and many peoples, all of whom are essentially like ourselves, whatever be the accidental differences of custom, race, color, or language. 

2. Sociology is the social science of human relations. 

We have shown, by various examples, that man lives in society. and is therefore a social being. Sociology is the science of society. 

It treats of man's relations with his fellow men. It studies human society, its customs and institutions and their development. Its subject matter is man's social life in general. It notes where man's relations with others may be improved, and tries to show what remedies are needed. What could be more fascinating than to study social relations as we see them around us every day? And what more useful purpose than to try to make this social world of ours a better place in which to live?

Since sociology deals with man, it is one of the social sciences. 

These social sciences all deal with man, either as an individual or in his many and complex relations with others. They include ethics, religion, economics, anthropology, history, political science, psychology, and sociology, and are called sciences because in each of them definite facts may be observed and recorded. 

3. Before beginning a study of sociology, one must take certain truths for granted.

Because sociology is a social science, the most important part of our subject matter is man. Before we can come to a consideration of man's social relations, however, we must understand clearly certain facts about him. These facts are either self-evident, or they have already been satisfactorily proved by philosophy, psychology, ethics, history, or theology. They can in no wise be considered mere theories or hypotheses.

The chief postulates of sociology, that is, the chief facts or truths which we must take for granted, are:

1. God exists, and He is the Creator of all things, man included. 

2. Man has a spiritual soul which is immortal; hence he has an eternal destiny. 

3. Man is endowed with a free will. 

4. Man is not only subject to physical laws but also to the moral law. 

5. Man has certain rights and duties which are common to all mankind. 

6. Christ is the Son of God and He established the Church, to which He gave divine authority to guide men in matters related to their supernatural destiny. 

Loading coal on an automatic belt conveyor.

For most of our needs we depend on the coöperation of others.

A brief discussion of the reason why Christian sociology postulates these facts will be found in the Appendix, and should be studied, for one cannot understand the true meaning of social life without a consideration of them. 

4. Man is social by nature and needs social life. 

When God created Adam and Eve and bade them people the

earth, He made man naturally and necessarily a social being. Man was to live with other persons, and to be dependent upon others from his birth to his grave. He was to owe his origin as well as his early care and education to his parents, or to their substitutes. 

In his mature years he was to depend upon many others for the sustenance and protection of his life. This dependence upon others, with the association it implies, is what is meant by man's social nature. Man cannot normally live alone. Common sense as well as history makes this evident. 

It is true that each person is individually responsible to his Creator for the use of that life which God gave him, for he possesses a free will and must make his own decisions, and will not Social life implies service. Here are school children acting as traffic police during the rush hour at noon. be judged by what others think or do but by his own free actions. 

He has his own separate individuality, and is made in the very likeness of God. Yet in this life he must be associated with others, and is consequently social by his very nature. Few workers use what they produce by their daily toil. They manufacture shoes or automobiles; they teach, or heal the sick, or serve behind a counter in a store; and in return for these services they obtain money with which to buy their food, clothing, housing, transportation, recreation, and all their other needs. Those who have read 

Robinson Crusoe will readily realize how much we depend upon others for even our most simple material needs. That we depend

on others for companionship, culture; and many other things will also be readily realized upon reflection. 

5. Man has a natural inclination toward social life. 

Man not only has a need of social life, but he also has an inborn inclination to live in society. To be truly happy we need to associate with others. Normal people enjoy life best when they are with congenial companions, and they are unhappy if left in isolation. Even when circumstances isolate a man, he does not live truly alone, for as a substitute for actual companionship he has the memory of former times spent with men, or he receives the ideas of others by means of books, the radio, or in other ways. It should not be hard to realize that we have indeed a propensity for social life. 40 

Ewing Galloway

Learning a form of social usefulness. First-aid class held at a Scout meeting. 

6. Man has a natural aptitude for social life. 

Besides desiring society, and actually needing social life, man also has aptitude for living with others. Evidence of this aptitude lies first in the fact that everyone, even the lowest savage, has the gift of speech, which enables him to exchange his ideas with others: a necessary condition of social life. Second, only through

social intercourse can man fully develop his intellectual, moral, and physical powers. Indeed, all the finest qualities of man, such as kindness, charity, and unselfishness, are engendered in social life. Third, man has an aptitude for love, and true love is expending one's affection on another.

Man's social nature is therefore proved by his aptitude and inclination for social life, and by his need of it. We might sum up these last three paragraphs in the words of the English writer and artist Eric Gill, who shows that man is a "social person" as follows: 

"1. Because physically (here, in this temporary place) there are two kinds of men - men and women. "2. Because the reason of having men and women is children.

Social means living in society. The first kind of society is the family (father, mother, and children). "3. Because people are not exactly like one another. Therefore some are good at one thing and some at another. Some make good fighters, some make good builders or good farmers, some make good merchants, some make good craftsmen, some make good teachers, some make good entertainers - funny men and artists. of all sorts. "Therefore men like to live in groups where there is something of everything." 

7. A society is a stable union of two or more persons uniting their efforts to obtain a common end by collective action. 

We must now distinguish between society, or group life in general, and a society, for man not only lives in society, but he belongs to different societies. To explain this in other terms: 

The social nature of man may be expressed merely by groups assembled for companionship or recreation, e.g., a crowd at a theater, or at a ball game; or it may be shown by membership in a definite society, such as a family, a parish club, a trade union. 

A society in this latter sense has certain characteristics, chiefly : 

There must be a reason for its existence, there must be some sort of a social bond, there must be a certain permanence attained 

¹Eric Gill, Unemployment, p. 3.

through common efforts, and there must be some directive authority. To take these in order:

If a society is to exist for any length of time, there must be some reason for it: It must be established to attain something which the members desire to possess. For example, a trade union is formed by a group of workers to maintain working conditions and wages at a higher level than would probably be possible if each individual worker had to bargain direct with the employer. 

A recreational society enables members to buy equipment and to 

Authority and direction are necessary even in recreational society. play games in a way which would be impossible for each member individually. A school provides individual students with the means of receiving an education which the majority of them could not obtain if they had to rely solely on their parents (their natural teachers), or if they were obliged to hire tutors for their private instruction.

Not only must there be a reason for the establishment of a society, however, but the end of the society must be attained by a community of efforts; that is, by common means. There must be some sort of social bond between the members; that is, some rights which the members may expect to receive, or some duties

and obligations which individuals, or the group as a whole, must carry out.

There must also be some stability in the association; in other words, a society must have more or less permanence of union. 

People may gather together for a common end, such as to enjoy the performance of an actor, to listen to a lecturer, or to play a chance game of football, but none of these temporary meetings are societies properly so called. Although they express the sociable nature of the men who form them, there is no stability of union. 

Ewing Galloway

Some animals obey their gregarious instincts, but they are not social beings; instinct, and not intelligence, dominates their actions. 

Similarly, there must usually be some member or members in authority, and some rules to be observed, because our ideas differ so widely that without this directing restraint there would ordinarily be no coöperation between the members, and chaos would be inevitable.

To sum up, we may define a society as a stable union of two or more persons uniting their efforts to obtain a common end by collective action. And as we have just explained, the elements common to all true societies are: plurality of persons, community of aim for the common good, stability of bond, coöperation of effort, and authority. 

8. Animals are gregarious but not sociable. They cannot form societies, because they lack reason and are dependent upon instinct.

We must by now have arrived at the definite conclusion that man is a social being. In this he differs from animals. 

Some animals have been endowed with remarkable gregarious instincts. They may hunt in packs, or congregate for greater protection against their enemies, and form families for the propagation of their kind. Naturalists have repeatedly demonstrated, however, that animals lack reason and are merely dependent upon the instinct given them by God for any gregarious organization which may appear to show intelligent planning. 

They do not know the purpose of their organization as purpose, nor can they refuse to do what their nature impels them to do. 

Bees swarm because they are endowed by nature with an instinct which impels them to act in this manner. They have no free will which would permit them to refuse to obey their instincts and refrain from swarming at the appropriate period. Animals cannot distinguish between end and means, and have no highly developed power of adaptation. Animal herdings, therefore, are not true societies. Only man is a social being, for man alone is capable of forming a society for a definite purpose and of his own free will.

Chapter II  

9. Some societies are primary societies: others are secondary or conventional. 

We stated in the preceding chapter that, in living in society, man belonged to different societies. Some of these societies are essential for our very existence, or are necessary if man is to lead a human natural life, and if ordered society is to continue in the world. Other societies are important in varying degrees, but they are not so essential. The recreational group is one of the most important of "conventional" societies.

Only five societies are necessary for man to lead a natural life as a creature, to fulfill the end for which he was created, and to maintain orderly social life. For two of these, the family and the state, man has a positive natural need that is direct and proximate, and these two have their end and means prescribed by God. For that reason they are called natural societies. There is a universal need of them, and God has so implanted this need 11

in man's nature that they cannot be eliminated from the world's social plan, nor can they be essentially changed. 

The other three primary societies are religious society, occupational society, and international society. Since man's life on earth is merely preparatory to life eternal, the religious society which has been appointed by God to help man work out his salvation is supernatural rather than natural. Occupational society and international society are required in various degrees, as we shall presently show, but their need is comparatively remote. 

All other societies, although fulfilling man's natural need of social life, and his aptitude and propensity for it, nevertheless, are not strictly necessary. They are therefore called subsidiary or conventional societies. These societies, which are almost without number, naturally vary considerably in their usefulness. Of them the recreational and school groups are the most important. 

Recreation develops body, mind, and character, and enables man to recuperate from his daily toil. The school group, or educational society, provides important physical, mental, and moral training for life. We shall discuss these two subsidiary societies in Chapters XII and XIII. 

Ewing Galloway

The family is the most important of all societies. 

10. The family, the most important of the primary societies, is also a natural society.

The family was the first society in the world, composed first of Adam and Eve, and later including their children. If this family had not existed, and if families had not continued to exist from Adam and Eve's time onward, we ourselves should not be here. Man formed domestic societies before any state or other group came into being. The family is, indeed, the most important of all societies because it is necessary for the very existence of the human race. If children were brought into the world promiscuously and without the training and loving affection of parents, if they survived at all, they would become harmful to society, and not helpful, as we shall explain in Chapter IV. The family is therefore a natural society as well as a primary one. 

Ewing Galloway

An Eskimo family. The family is a primary and natural society, and is found among men of all races and degrees of civilization. 

11. Civil society, or the state, is likewise both a primary and a natural society. 

The second of the primary societies is the state. Just as man cannot live in isolation, neither can the family. Individuals and families need the coöperation of others, and it is natural for them to congregate for protection and for the promotion of the material, intellectual, and moral development of the community group. Wherever people are congregated, however, authority is necessary for right order. There must, then, be some authority

over these community groups, and hence, civil society fills a human need.

Although the family existed before the state came into being, nevertheless the latter is also a natural society because it must have been very necessary even at an early period of the world's history. From the earliest periods of which we have any records, we learn that states existed. Just when they sprang up we do not know, since we have no record of the exact date when God created Adam and Eve, and for many ages after Adam man did not write down the history of events. These states, of course, have taken and still do take many forms. At first village communities were formed, with the civil authority vested loosely in a village council or in a tribal chief; then towns came into being, with their governing head or group of city fathers; then larger civil societies, ruled over by a king, a president, a senate, or the like. The United States, Great Britain, Germany, Iraq, Liberia, Japan — all are states within the meaning of the term given here. 71%

12. Religious society is a primary society; the Church is a supernatural society.

Even the most primitive man shows a need of expressing his dependence on his Creator by some form of outward worship in common with his fellows (religious society). 

God Himself ordained that man should belong to a religious

society, for He founded the one true Church of the Israelites, which our Lord Jesus Christ later replaced by the Catholic Church: "Thou art Peter; and upon this rock I will build My 

Church, and the gates of hell shall not prevail against it."¹ 

Quite apart from God's revelation to man, it is natural for man to belong to religious society because of his sociable nature and his inborn instinct to worship the Creator. We are all familiar with the religious societies which are most common among our civilized nations, the Catholic Church, the Episcopalian, the Jewish, the Presbyterian, the Baptist, and the rest. However, the chief religious society which may be called primary, and of which man truly has a need, is the Catholic Church to which we belong. 

Man has a need of belonging to this Church, not only to realize his social nature by worshiping God in common with other men, but also because God has made the Catholic Church the depository of truth, and the official means of salvation. The ideas of individual men often err, and man frequently misinterprets the dictates of his own conscience. In the Catholic Church, however, he may be certain that if he follows her teachings he will attain his eternal salvation and enjoy the everlasting bliss of heaven. In the

Church he will likewise secure the maximum of help, in the form of grace, to live his life as he ought. 

As Eric Gill so admirably has it: "The Church: that is the society of all men united not as fathers and mothers not as neighbours not as a nation not as workers or traders but as passengers. "Passengers on a ship or a railway train are united not as men and women, not because they are in the same cabin or compartment or deck (first class, etc.), not because they are officers or deck hands or engineers, not because they speak the same language in the dining saloon, but because they are all going the same way. . . "The Christian, and by that I mean the Catholic Church which 

¹Matt. xvi. 18.

has its visible head in Rome (I mean the Catholic Church because it is the only one which knows its own mind for certain and the only one which is not a national society or a society of special people; it is a society which contains all kinds of men, rich and poor, silly and wise, bad and good, white, black, yellow, and red, French, English, Hottentot, and Chinese. The Church of England is a very good Church, I am sure, but it is the Church of England - that is, it is mostly just English and people in English colonies; and English people are not the only passengers in the ship we call the world. The Catholic
