We might note here that from the social viewpoint, religious society is of the utmost importance, for the Church, through the sacraments and by her teaching, spurs man on to be moral, to exert a good influence in the world and not a bad one, to observe the rights of others, and to perform his duties as perfectly as possible, and thus to become truly a desirable citizen. Keystone

Agricultural workers form the most important economic group in occupational society. 

13. The church and the state are not only primary societies but perfect societies.

Both the church and the state are not only primary societies but perfect societies, for both are sovereign in their own sphere, and do not in any way depend upon any higher power than God alone. This implies that the state or civil society, whose aim is

the material and temporal welfare of its members, belongs to a sphere inferior and subordinate to that of the church or religious society, whose aim is the spiritual and eternal welfare of her members. The church, however, has no authority over the state as such, but only over the individual members who form 

Looking south on Broad Street, New York City. The building at the extreme right is the New York Stock Exchange. This is one of the leading centers of occupational society. the state. Both church and state have relations with other socie- 

ties, of course with the individuals and families which compose them, and with the other societies of which these are members. 

14. Occupational society is a primary society but not a natural one.

Work is necessary for man, under modern conditions, to main-

tain life and to attain economic well-being. Occupational society is the regulation of this labor, and its organization into groups which function within civil society. 

In earlier times it might have been denied that occupational society was primary, since it would have been possible for all except the very young, the aged, and the infirm to grow crops or to gather fruits and kill wild animals for sustenance. Nevertheless the occupational group or economic society has existed from the time man divided up labor among the various members of the family, the men obtaining the food, while the women performed domestic duties. In our own day industry is highly developed into occupational societies: corporations organized by law, trade unions, employers' associations, and the like. It would be difficult for most of us to live without the help of others; certainly impossible to maintain present standards without the division of labor and the complex system of modern economic organization. 

It must be said, however, that although agricultural occupations are the most important, since we depend upon them for our very sustenance, farming pursuits are not universally organized into the formal divisions we might expect today. Nevertheless, some organization exists even on the smallest farm, and the various men and women about the farm share the work between them. 

The marketing of certain agricultural produce is also highly complex. 

15. International society is likewise a primary but not a natural group.

By international society is meant organized harmonious coöperation among all nations. The usefulness of international society would appear to prove that it is a primary group, although this statement may be contested, since international society does not yet exist, and nations have not as yet shown any inclination to submit whole-heartedly to any central authority. Nevertheless, just as the individual cannot exist without the family, and just as the family needs group life and the state for its protection and its fuller development, so states need each other. International society should logically be the natural development of

civil society, and it may thus be considered as at least remotely a primary society. 

The interdependence of nations for the needs of life, the mutual help nations can give one another, and the terrible consequences of war, are all arguments in favor of an association of nations, such as the League of Nations strives to set up. International society, the family, the state, and also occupational society, will be the subject of special chapters later in this book. Keystone

The League of Nations in session at Geneva. A modern attempt to organize International Society. 

16. Social life is very complex and presents varied problems.

Having now discussed society in its broad outlines, it must be evident to the reader that social life is very complex. The society to which we feel most closely bound is that of our family. Yet each member of our family probably has diverse social interests and belongs to some distinct societies of which the others are not members. In certain groups, members of our own family may even conflict with each other, or the groups to which different members belong may coöperate or overlap. For example, two brothers may belong to rival baseball clubs; a mother and a

daughter may be members of different social clubs which may occasionally give joint social events; and two or more members of the family may belong to the same subsidiary groups, such as the same trade union, coöperative credit bank, or church sodality. 

In the complex social life many problems may arise. The essential element of social life is the relation of one individual to one or more other persons. This relationship gives rise to varied rights. and duties. When duties are neglected or rights violated, social problems arise. When duties or rights conflict or overlap, more problems are created. Man's principal rights are to life, liberty, justice, truth, the ownership of property, social life with directive authority, and the right to perform his duties to his Creator. His principal duties are to observe the similar rights of his neighbor; to worship God and accept His Will; and to preserve his own life and health, both physical and moral. 

A further discussion of these points will be found in the section on social environment in Chapter III, and in many special chapters later on. 

17. Summary of the first two chapters. 

We have now learned that man needs social life, and has a propensity and aptitude for it, and that he expresses his sociable nature by living in freely and intelligently organized groups. We have learned, too, that there are five primary societies for which there is a positive need in man's very nature, but that there are numberless subsidiary organizations to which man may belong, and man's social intercourse is very complex. 

The conclusions we may draw from these chapters, therefore, are as follows: (1) The most important of the societies we have enumerated merit detailed study; (2) the complexity of social life necessarily gives rise to many problems to be solved; (3) since man is a social being by nature, and since harmonious coöperation within group life is more advantageous to the individual than selfish individuality, society must be organized for the benefit of all the persons composing it, and it is our duty to become "social-minded," that is, not to look only to our own individual betterment but also to the good of others. On these

22 

conclusions the rest of this book is based, with the exception that we have omitted a separate discussion of religious society as going too deeply into theology and anthropology for the present course. 77 

Wide World Photo 

The world's oldest peace treaty. This 4,600year-old tablet bears the terms of a peace treaty between two ancient Sumerian cities. 

Even in early times some sort of international society existed.

Chapter III  ON MAN 

18. Man is influenced by a variety of complex factors. 

Since sociology deals with social relations, it is very intimately concerned with man's individual character and temperament. 

After all, man is the most important unit in group life, and his social usefulness, his moral conduct, health, and occupations, are all affected by his character and disposition. And these latter are, in turn, due to a variety of complex factors. It will be well, therefore, to study man a little, in relation to these factors. 

All men are equal in the sense that all are composed of a body and a soul made to God's own image and likeness, and that all have been redeemed by Jesus Christ; politically, too, all men are theoretically born equal with equal rights and duties, as mentioned in the first chapter. But in every other respect each individual varies from his fellow men. Just what makes the difference the exact reason why one man's conduct is good, and another's bad; why one is lazy, and another industrious; why one is a genius, and another mentally deficient — is largely a matter of conjecture, though it may be traced to some extent in individual cases. Some people explain these differences as being chiefly due to the influence of heredity; while others attribute them to the effect of environment, particularly economic and geographic. 

There is much truth in both opinions, and a consideration of these tenets is the purpose of the present chapter. It will be noted, however, that a much wider and more logical viewpoint is that which does not unduly stress any one factor but coördinates the 23

whole, recognizing that man is influenced by the physical makeup which he inherits, as likewise by his inherited emotional predispositions; that he differs from other men not only by heredity, but also by the training and the influence of all the types of environment to which he is subjected-geographic, economic, social, and psychic-and that the influence of all these factors is ultimately determined by the use which he makes of his freedom of will, and by the special graces which he receives from Almighty God and the way he accepts these. Even identical twins (that is, those born with identical heredity), brought up in exactly the same environment, may differ in character and attainment. 

19. Heredity influences man but is not all-important. 

Heredity means that which goes to make up the individual when he begins life as such. It includes the sum total of the instincts, and of the physical, emotional, and other potential characteristics which we receive from our parents at birth. 

Heredity ceases at the moment of conception. Apart from physical traits and instincts, only predispositions, that is, potential traits, are inherited; not actual characteristics. Some of these potential traits are received from the father, some from the mother, in varying degrees, which accounts for the great differences among members of the same family.

There is a class of people called the eugenists who say that not only do we inherit instincts and certain potential traits, but we definitely inherit character, mental capacity, health, and the like. They consider that heredity is the prime factor of importance in the life of the individual, and for this reason they advocate the marriage of the "fit" only. By "fit" they mean those who are strong and healthy, who have sound moral characters and good mentality.

The question of heredity involves a biological discussion which is too deep to go into here. No one denies the existence of heredity, of course, but its influence in human beings is a subject about which we know very little, and on which very little dogmatic judgment can be passed.

It is certain, of course, that we inherit our bodily structure, our nervous system, and our general capabilities. Most people have many points of physical resemblance to their parents or ancestors, that is, they inherit certain physical characteristics. 

We also inherit sexual, parental, and social instincts. These 

III 

88 AA AB AA AB AB BB AB AA AA AA AB 

An Example of Inherited Characteristics: Let BB = deafness; 

AA = good hearing; AB = good hearing with a recessive characteristic for deafness. I. Two people with good hearing marry, but one has inherited a defective gene for deafnéss, although it is not apparent. II. All their children will have good hearing but half will have a recessive characteristic for deafness hidden in their germ plasm. III. If one of the children with this recessive characteristic marries someone with a similar recessive defect, out of four offspring one will have perfect hearing and will never pass on a gene deficiency for deafness; two will have good hearing, with the hidden defect; one will be deaf. This result was shown by the monk Mendel (1822-1884) to be a law of nature; now called Mendel's Law. instincts are given us by God to insure the propagation of the race, the necessary care of children during their long period of adolescence, and our own social well-being. 

Myopia (near-sightedness), albinism, deaf-mutism, and color-

blindness are inherited defects. We may inherit a good or a poor physique or constitution. But diseases are not inherited, although we may inherit a susceptibility to certain diseases, just as we may inherit a special capacity for resisting other infections. A child born with a predisposition to tuberculosis, and having tubercular parents, will in all likelihood develop the disease if he lives with the parents from birth, whereas early separation from them might prevent this. Some people rarely catch a cold, while others seem particularly susceptible to cold-germs. 

As regards mental capacity: our mind and our will are faculties of the soul. We inherit our body from our parents, but our soul is specially created by God, and its faculties are spiritual and come directly from God and not from our parents. Since, however, our mind and will work through the instrumentality of our brain, an inherited defect in the brain may result in a person being born an idiot or feeble-minded. We must remember. however, that all those who are born feeble-minded are not necessarily such on account of the inheritance of physical defects of the brain. Their misfortune may be due to prenatal accident or some other cause. Such a person might marry and have normal children. 

It is to be noted, too, that we definitely inherit only predispositions to emotional and moral characteristics. We all have an aptitude or tendency that is, an inclination toward at least some of the virtues - humility, liberality, chastity, meekness, temperance, brotherly love, and diligence. All of us likewise inherit a predisposition toward at least some of the seven "deadly sins" of pride, covetousness, lust, anger, gluttony, envy, and sloth. Inheritance decides for us just what composition of these tendencies we are to receive. For example, some people are more covetous, envious, and lazy than others, yet their chastity, humility, and moderation may be greater than such qualities in persons who are less greedy and slothful. And, although we are born with such predispositions, we need not foster them. We can develop the good, and destroy the bad tendencies to a very large extent; or we can develop the bad and stifle our good qualities St. Francis

de Sales is accredited with having been born with a predisposition to anger, but he fought against it so hard, and tried so courageously to develop the opposite virtue of meekness, that he is now famed for his meek temperament.

It is evident that before marriage young people should consider the situation seriously if there seems a danger that they may bear children of poor inheritance, although God has so arranged it that mutual attraction, that is, love, without much consideration of anything else, is the dominant element in the choice of one's mate. The ideas of the eugenists as to the results of poor marriages certainly have an element of truth, but eugenists exaggerate the importance of heredity, and propose as means to counteract it, practices which are against the natural law. 

Environment is of much greater importance than heredity, in that it can help to foster the good physical, emotional, and other predispositions which are inherited, and mitigate the bad. Of ultimate importance, however, as we shall later explain, is the will. 

Courtesy of the Grace Line 

Cuzco, Peru. This city is in a tropical climate.

20. Environment is of several kinds. 

Environment is any external force that plays upon the individual. There is physical environment, both geographic and economic. Then there is social environment; that is, the environment provided by the various groups to which a person belongs. 

There is also mental or psychic environment: external things which affect the individual's mentality-books, motion pictures, public opinion, culture, the ideals and attitudes of those with whom he associates. 35500 

DAYTON'S STEARTS BUR COATS The Milwaukee Journal 

The downtown section of a large city in a temperate climate. 

This picture shows only a section of the office buildings, hotels, and department stores.

21. Geographic environment is important, but less important than environment of other kinds. 

There is a school of thought which places undue emphasis on the influence of geographic environment.¹ Adherents of this school are called geographic determinists. They say that man's social life is almost entirely determined by his geographical environment, and that human energy and achievement, sex relations, religion, occupations, thought, art, crime, character, are all profoundly affected by locality, climate, and other geographical factors. There is, of course, some truth in their statements. 

People living in hot climates tend to be lethargic and noninventive, for nature provides them with an easy livelihood. Very cold climates give the inhabitants of such regions a hard struggle for existence and little time or ambition for cultural pursuits. 

Those living in isolated districts, in deserts or on mountainous slopes, lack the incentive of social intercourse with others, and are rarely known for great achievements. Temperate climates are most favorable to man's development, for he has to work hard to earn a livelihood, and yet not so hard that he has no leisure for other things. Climate affects our moods and nerves. Great heat is irritating and enervating; a dull day may make us sad and discontented, a bright one fill us with joy. Geographical environment, however, in no way dominates human life, for a suitable temperament can easily enable us to rise above it, at least to a large extent. Temperature certainly does not appear to have the influence often imputed to it, for despite the torrid climate we have ample proofs of the high degree of civilization attained by the early Sumerians, Egyptians, Indians, and others; nor does climate appear to affect the achievements of those who inhabit cities in the United States with high summer temperatures and great humidity. Moreover, geographical influence is being rapidly decreased by improvements in transportation, by railroads, airplanes, wireless, ocean vessels, newspapers, and the spread of literature. 

¹Cf. Wm. F. Miller, "Geographers Astray," America, May 19, 1934.

22. Economic environment has an influence on man, but this influence is not insurmountable. 

Some people are brought up in an atmosphere of luxury. Others belong to good middle-class homes of varying degrees of comfort or luxury. Others, again, come from decent working-class homes, where there are few comforts, but where, nevertheless, there is a sufficiency of food, clothing, and recreation, and where the neighborhood is clean and decent. But many people, even in these civilized times, live in dirty tenements and hovels, with bad sanitation, lack of fresh air, and under crowded conditions which lend themselves easily to immorality and other vices, as well as to ill health and despair. The ideal economic environment is undoubtedly that of the middle-class household living in the country or in the suburbs of a city, where there are good air, sanitation, food, clothing, recreational and educational facilities, and a sufficiency of room space in the home. Children living under these conditions generally lead wholesome, healthful lives and develop good vitality and physique and keen mental ability. On the other hand, the children of the very rich are sometimes pampered, and may become selfish, lack restraint, and even spend their time in dissipation in an attempt to overcome the boredom resulting from lack of useful occupation and an excess of pleasure. 

Pauls' Photos 

Children brought up in an extremely poor economic environment are likely to suffer many physical ills. 

Those brought up in an extremely poor economic environment are also likely to be affected by it. Malnutrition and lack of

proper health facilities often result in physical ills. The depressing effects of destitution may lead a child to such evils as stealing, lying, envy, hatred, and total lack of ambition. As a result of insufficient economic resources, a person may never have the opportunity to rise out of his condition, and may be forever doomed to poorly paid positions and a life of poverty, even though his mental capacity may fit him for much better things. 

The Socialists have recognized the influence of economic environment, but they have exaggerated its significance. They attribute all the world's happenings, and all social institutions, Acme Children brought up in an economic environment such as shown in this picture are severely handicapped. even religious and moral, to the struggle to earn a living, and they say that material well-being, that is, having plenty of money and a good economic environment, is of the greatest moment in life, to the exclusion of all other factors. This theory that all events and social institutions have their rise in economic causes is called economic determinism or the materialistic concept of history. It is false, because although economic environment does indeed affect people, as we have just shown, yet poor environment

A one-family home with lawn and garden provides ideal economic environment for children. is not always detrimental, nor affluent circumstances always beneficial. Man's own nature, his good and his bad qualities, Ewing Galloway

A Negro cabin in South Carolina. Living conditions for many of our southern Negro workers have changed very little since the war between the states.

external factors such as overpopulation, famine, and the like, have been more frequent causes of social events and institutions than any economic factor.

Poverty is no disgrace. A family which is poor, but where the children are brought up with parental care and love, will be a greater asset to a nation than a careless, pleasure-loving family. 

Christ Himself was poor when He lived on earth. Poverty may act as a powerful incentive to the development of one's capacities. 

Poor men are responsible for many of the world's greatest inventions and innovations. Poverty, too, may result in the acquisition of many virtues, such as gentleness, patience, sympathy, understanding, trust in God, abandonment to His Will, and hope of future happiness in heaven. 

23. Social environment, particularly at an early age, has a great influence on man, since our habits are largely formed in youth under the influence and training of others. 

As most people spend the greater part of their days, and often the whole of their waking hours, in the company of others, it must be obvious that man is greatly influenced by these social Good companions furnish us with desirable social and psychic environment. groups with which he is associated. As the proverb puts it: "Tell me your companions, and I'll tell you what manner of man you and "Birds of a feather flock together." are";

If a worker's companions are Socialists, he will very probably come to regard their tenets as true, even though at the outset he disagreed with them. If a girl's companions regard gay parties

and the latest fashions as essential to happiness, it is very probable that she will soon come to believe as they do. Examples could be cited interminably.

The influence of environment in adult life, however, is largely determined by the habits which a person has acquired. Man's character is merely the sum of habits built upon his inherited predispositions to good and evil. Habits are formed only by repeated acts. The more often a certain act is performed, the more easily is it done, until finally it becomes almost second nature to us. For instance, when we are first learning to talk, it is only by repeatedly hearing a certain word, and then repeatedly pronouncing it, or attempting to pronounce it, that we come to use that word with facility, so that we no longer have to think of its formation, but say it automatically. If we become accustomed to going home by a certain route, we automatically turn in the direction of this route, even after we may have found that another way is more convenient or expedient. 

We are creatures of habit, and habits once acquired are only with difficulty cast aside. In our early home and school life, therefore, we should be trained in good and virtuous habits, and taught to refrain from bad actions.

The earlier that any particular social environment plays on a child, the more it will affect his habits. It is for this reason that the social influence of the family is of most importance, with the school and play groups next. If a child is brought up in a good Christian home, in an atmosphere of love and kindliness, with a normal, healthy development of his social qualities in the give-and-take of happy family life, and well-chosen companions outside the home, he will in all likelihood become a good normal citizen. If, on the other hand, a child's family life is one of constant quarreling, strife, and lack of self-restraint on the part of other members of the family, he also will probably learn to give way to all his impulses, without any attempt to control his evil propensities by being willing to do what is right and not what is easiest or most advantageous. A child who is corrected

every time he steals a penny, for example, will probably form the good habit of resisting temptation to steal, until at last the sight of a penny will no longer give him any illicit desire for it. On the other hand, a child who knows that his parents steal money from others, will in all probability develop a bad habit of disregarding other people's property rights. 

It must be clearly understood, however, that to form a habit the repeated acts must be freely made; under compulsion, actions do not become habitual, even if done continuously. Thus, although one may force an unwilling child to conform to one's wishes, it is only by convincing him of the wisdom of performing the act, or by winning his love or esteem, that one can persuade him to do it freely of his own accord. 

Yet man is imitative by nature, and social environment is of such great importance that his will naturally tends to want to conform his conduct to that of the social environment in which he lives. 

24. Of all environmental influences, mental or psychic environment is the most important.

Psychic environment is the influence exerted on our mind and will, not only by the attitudes and ideals of our social environment, but also by public opinion and convention, by the literature we read, the music we hear, by art, religious ceremonies and liturgy, and all the other indefinable and unnoticed forces which play upon our minds all day long. These forces continuously strengthen or destroy the ideals given us by our sense of duty and responsibility, and by the teaching and training in principles and the right and wrong of action, seen from both the reasonable and the supernatural points of view, which should be inculcated by both the home and the school. 

Not all the social groups to which a person belongs have the same ideals and attitudes. In adult life, a person may usually choose his cultural surroundings and the groups in which he wishes to move; or, if he is forced to live in an environment 

2Cf. Rev. V. Michel, "Social Aspects of the Liturgy," Catholic Action, May, 1934, pp. 9-11.

with ideals and attitudes different from the ones he has already formed by long-established association and habit, he may not be greatly influenced by it.

In the child, however, serious conflicts frequently occur. He has to endure moral conflicts, where his impulses and emotions are not in accord with his conscience or the standards of his environment, and he may also have added conflicts arising from the differing social groups of which he is a member. The habits which his parents try to instill in him and the psychic environment of his home may be utterly opposed to the habits and ideals of his companions at play or in the school. If he has good parents, it is not likely that they will permit him to have the wrong kind of companions, literature, or pastimes; but not all parents are as careful in these matters as might be advisable, nor are the attitudes of all parents ideal. Again, the parents may have been brought up in an environment which differs from that to which the children belong outside the home, and they may be unable to appreciate the good qualities of the latter, or to understand the changed situations of modern times. Such conflicts usually have a harmful influence on a child. 

25. Man is greatly affected by environment, but he is not determined by it. He is endowed with freedom of will, and he has God's grace to assist him to choose what is right and to persevere in the formation of good habits.

As stated in the first paragraph of this chapter, it is evident that a variety of complex factors influence man's conduct. His conduct is, of course, important in sociology, because it directly affects his relations with others; that is, it has a good or bad influence on social life.

The primary work of parents and teachers is not to crush the natural endowments of the child, which make him different from others. Their work is rather to guide nature and to train the child in habits of virtue, so that he will habitually and of his own free will choose virtuous actions in preference to evil. Mar differs from the animal precisely in that he need not be dominated by heredity and environment but is the master of his own fate.

He is a rational being; that is, he can reason about the expediency of his acts and can decide for himself. Man must build upon his instincts and his human nature, but he must see that he acts as a rational being. He must not let impulse, bad example, or convention dominate his will; rather by his will he must subjugate all his actions to reason. To help him develop a good moral character and to train his will to virtue, man has the Ten Commandments and the teaching of Christ to guide him; the Catholic, in addition, has the sacraments, the teachings of the Church, the grace of a Catholic education; and the good influence of sodalities, confraternities, parish societies, and national Catholic organizations. He will always have conflicts and struggles, either between his environment and his conscience, or between his evil tendencies and acquired bad habits, and his knowledge of what is right. His free will and the other helps just mentioned will, however, enable him to triumph over evil.

Chapter IV THE FAMILY 

26. Definition of the family.

We all know what is meant by the social group called the family, or domestic society. It consists of parents united by marriage, and their children. Sometimes other relatives are included, who may be dependent on the breadwinner and live under the same roof. In a few countries permanent servants are also considered to be members of the family for which they work, by virtue of the assistance which they give the family in carrying out its functions. 

27. The family is a natural society, and has a definite purpose or end.

There are many facts which prove that the family is a natural society. First of all, God gave man sexual instincts so that he would wish to marry and thus perpetuate the race. Now, children, unlike animals, need many years of training and care before they can earn their own livelihood. Only where there is permanent union of husband and wife in a monogamous household is this care perfectly assured to children. For the upbringing of children, therefore, and hence for the continuance of society in the world, the family is essential. Then again, since parents might weary of each other, without conjugal love to make continued companionship desirable, God in His wisdom has endowed man with an affectionate and tender nature, which craves for a more intimate companionship and love than that afforded by ordinary social life. And since the care of children is an arduous task, and parents might fail in the many sacrifices which parenthood and the rearing of children entail, God has also given parents 38

THE FAMILY 

39 a special parental love for their offspring. A mother is called upon to make many more sacrifices than a father, for the latter is usually absent from the home the greater part of the day. God therefore intensified this special love in the mother by endowing her with what we call the "maternal instinct." And to the father

God gave a more highly developed combative instinct, so that he would go forth with courage to win the daily bread for himself and his family.

Domestic society, therefore, has a definite purpose for its existence. It is essential for the continuance and well-being of our race. The primary end of the family is the propagation and education of children to perpetuate the human race. The secondary end of married life in the family is the fostering of conjugal love between husband and wife, and the mutual rendering of assistance between the members. 

28. Forms of Marriage: Monogamy, Polyandry, and 

Polygyny.

It will be well to know that there are several types of marriage. 

Strict monogamy exists where families are formed by the marriage of but one man and one woman at the same time, and where marriage with a second person is allowed only on the death of the first partner.

Where the majority of families in a community are monogamous, but where, nevertheless, it is not considered as offending against good morals if a person marries a second partner before the first one dies, then dominant monogamy prevails. Such a condition may be said to exist where a man or woman is allowed to divorce the first partner and marry another, as in most countries today.

Polygamy is a term used for both polygyny and polyandry. 

Polygyny is the marriage of one husband to several wives, as is, for example, sanctioned under the Mohammedan law. 

Polyandry is the marriage of one woman to several men. It could never have been the true form of marriage. First, a child would be uncertain as to who his father was, and many situations would become too complicated for harmonious social life. Second,

for biological reasons polyandry renders women incapable of bearing children after a few years, and the race would have disappeared had there been universal polyandry. Third, whenever polyandry has existed there has always been a specific reason for it: women were scarce; or the locality was so impoverished that one man alone could not afford the upkeep of a family, and needed to share the expenses with others; or there was a desire to preserve property undivided, as formerly in Ceylon, and among the ancient Spartans; or similar situations. Fourth, polyandry is extremely rare and now found only in Tibet and in southeastern India. Even in Tibet no strict polyandry exists, for the richer nomads are monogamous, and among the poorer classes a woman is the exclusive wife of a man for a year, after which her place is taken by her sister who is exclusively his wife for the following year, when another sister takes the place for a further year, and so on. 

29. God originally intended the family to be monogamous. 

Unlike polyandry, polygyny does not interfere with the primary end of marriage; that is, with the begetting of children and rearing them in the family. There are many considerations, however, which prove that the monogamous family is the only true natural domestic society.

First of all, whenever the sexes are more or less equal, and a woman can get one husband for herself, she would not usually wish to become the second wife of a man already married. Then the family is the most important society in the upbringing and training of a child. Habits, as we said in the preceding chapter, are formed more easily in the earliest years, and a good united family with its characteristics of mutual affection, is of the utmost importance in early training. In a harem there would be jealousies and strife, and the child would oftentimes have to submit to the varying authority of each of the separate wives. 

Again, polygyny makes the bond between child and father much less close, and the child would not normally have such affection for him as would insure that he would be properly cared for in his old age.

THE FAMILY 

41 Now, the strength of a nation depends on the strength of the families which compose it. There is little unity in a polygynous household. So the third objection to polygyny is that a state composed of polygynous families would not have the cohesion requisite for its success in the economic struggle with monogamous nations; and it would certainly be at a great disadvantage as regards those nations in times of war. 

Fourth, polygyny presupposes a certain amount of wealth on the part of the man, to be able to afford several wives and their children. At the very beginnings of the race man had probably not amassed sufficient wealth to provide for more than a monogamous family. Even in those states where polygyny is allowed at the present time, only the wealthier members practice it. 

In addition to these natural reasons, however, and despite the fact that polygyny was allowed by the Jews, we have the unquestioned authority of Jesus Christ that God intended the family to be monogamous.¹

In the Old Testament, also, it is clearly stated: "Wherefore a man shall leave father and mother, and shall cleave to his wife; and they shall be two in one flesh.""

We may say in conclusion, too, that whenever any system other than monogamy has been allowed, it is now clearly recognized by all civilized countries that it was an abuse. 

30. The family is the basic social institution. 

The very continuance of the human race is dependent upon the family, and this alone proves the prime importance of marriage and domestic society.

But the work of the parents does not end with the birth of the child. He needs many years of loving care and training before he can take his place in the world as a self-supporting citizen. 

During these years someone must support the child and his mother, so the father's continued interest in mother and child is essential. Someone must likewise look after the home for the father and the child, and this is the mother's occupation. The child's health and physical growth must be attended to. His spir- 

1Cf. Matt. xix. 4-9. "Gen. ii. 24.

itual needs must be provided for. His character must be formed, so that, his inherited predispositions to evil being gradually eradicated, he may form good habits, acquire emotional stability, and develop a sense of responsibility as a member of society. 

His mental ability and manual dexterity must also be trained, so that he may be able to earn his own living and take his place as a useful member of society. The family, therefore, is essential, not only to beget the child, but also to rear and train it as a member of society. And in this latter work both father and mother are needed.

Society, as we previously pointed out, is composed of individuals, and hence it is important that the individuals be good citizens. Whether the child becomes a poor citizen or a good one depends largely upon the training and care which he receives in the home. Indeed, the integrity of the state and of society at large depends solely upon the integrity of the family and its members.

In earlier times the family was the sole factor in the upbringing of the future citizens. There was the patriarchal family, where the father was the head not only of his immediate family, but also of the families of his grown-up sons and daughters. Abraham and other early Biblical characters provide us with an illustration of the power of the patriarchal family head. The father of this extended family was responsible for everything, and members received from him directly, or from other members at the father's command, all that was requisite for their spiritual, mental, moral, and physical growth. He was the early ancestral priest, and the older members of the family coöperated for the education and all the other needs of the younger members. 

Although the family is no longer such a self-contained unit, and although it now delegates part of its functions to the Church, the State, and the school, nevertheless it is in the family alone that the child receives his care and training in his very earliest and most important years. The family, therefore, is still the basic social institution; and although it may delegate some of its functions, the child who is reared entirely outside the family

THE FAMILY 

43 circle is sorely handicapped in later life, in competition with those who had the advantage of good parents and adequate home care. 

31. The family is a religious school. 

Although God has delegated to the Church the task of official worship and moral teaching, the family is, nevertheless, the first religious school to which the child belongs. Those whose parents do not worship God, and who have not been taught by them to say their prayers and act rightly from supernatural motives, do not usually grow up to have a true recognition of their duties to God and their moral duties toward their neighbor. The school and the Church may teach the child his spiritual and moral obligations, but if the parents misguide the child, either positively in their teaching, or indirectly by bad example, it is greatly to be feared that the child will rather adopt their attitude than follow precepts taught them outside the home. The child comes under the environmental influence of the home for a longer period than that of any other social environmental factor. Hence it must be obvious that the family exercises the greatest power in shaping the child's attitudes, ideals, and conduct: that the child's interests, his ambitions, hopes, fears, prejudices, are all likely to be strongly influenced by those with whom he is most closely related by blood and most frequently associated. 

32. The family is the primary economic unit. 

Man must work in order to live, and to live he not only needs to obtain food, clothing, and shelter, but the food must be prepared, the clothing made and kept in repair, and the home must be maintained in a clean, orderly condition. The members of the family also need special attention in sickness, childhood, and old age. From the earliest times, man divided labor among the various members of the family. The wife took care of the home and the cooking, and looked after the children. Sometimes she also tilled the soil, and wove the clothing from wool or flax. The husband hunted, or occupied himself with agriculture, or gained a livelihood in other ways. Even up to comparatively recent times, industry was carried on in the home, so that the family was united

in its mutual gaining of a livelihood and in caring for its needs. 

However, although in present times the husband usually works. away from the home, the family is still the most important economic unit of society, because it is the family which creates the demand for the goods made in industry; and likewise because it is for the upkeep of the family that the majority of men perform their daily work. There are few persons in this world who earn their livelihood for themselves alone and not as part of a family unit. 

33. Relations between members of the family must be harmonious.

The home is the inner sanctuary of all social life, and true home life should be one of mutual peace and harmony, and of love and charity between parents and children. 

The man is ruler of the family: "Let women be subject to their husbands, as to the Lord," said St. Paul. "In the family, though as human beings men and women are equal, yet as running a family then man is the head . . . we allow the father to be the head of the family (without electing him) because we think his greater freedom of movement (he is not so tied by the physical work of bearing and caring for children as the mother is), his greater freedom of movement gives him a better chance of being naturally wiser-i.e., seeing four sides to everything and the importance of other interests besides the interests of his own family."

But the wife is to be subject to her husband, not as a servant, but as a companion, and immediately following the above quotation, St, Paul adds: "Husbands, love your wives, as Christ also loved the Church, and delivered Himself up for it. . . . So also ought men to love their wives as their own bodies. He that loveth his wife, loveth himself." 195 Children must love and reverence their parents, who have sacrificed much to bring them into the world and to care and provide for them; and they must obey their parents because authority 'Eph. v. 22. "Eric Gill, Unemployment, p. 7. 'Eph. v. 25, 28.

THE FAMILY 

45 is necessary for the right conduct of society, and because the experience of parents is usually superior to the whim of the child. 

Only when grave injustice is done to them may children refuse obedience to their parents; for example, when parents wish them to break the moral law. It will be remembered that "Honor thy father and thy mother" is one of the Ten Commandments.

Parents, on the other hand, must bring up their children "in discipline and correction," but they should remember that their children have personalities as well as they, and that their natural rights must be respected. As Holy Scripture has put it: "Children, obey your parents in the Lord, for this is just. . . . And you, fathers, provoke not your children to anger; but bring them up in the discipline and correction of the Lord."

Only when parents and children recognize the mutual rights and duties of each member will family relations be harmonious. 

That the family live in harmony is the prime requisite for harmony in society at large. "Eph. vi. 1-4.

Chapter V PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 

34. Present-day conditions of family life are not ideal. 

In recent years there have been drastic moral, economic, and social changes in family life. Domestic society lacks its former unity and affectionate cohesion. There are fewer children, and often none at all, to cement the parents' love and provide a bond to preserve family unity despite the misunderstandings that inevitably occur. By many, divorce is considered the natural remedy for what might be only passing conjugal misunderstandings, as is evident when one considers the following tables:

PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 47 

Apart, however, from the increased conception of divorce as the best solution to marital differences, economic and social changes have inevitably led to a disruption of domestic harmony and coöperation. 

35. Economic changes have led to a decadence of family life.

Prior to the industrial revolution and the growth of the capitalistic régime, the economic order was known as the domestic system. The majority of people were not herded together in cities, as now, but lived by the cultivation of the soil. Even those who were engaged in industry carried on their occupation 

Ewing Galloway

Many of the women workers in this garment factory may be married and forced to leave their children during working hours. in or about the home. Thus, all day long, the family was united; parents and children worked and played together. Although there were many defects in the system, nevertheless the economic and social interests of all the members of the family were identical. 

Mutual helpfulness, coöperation, and trust were the results. 

The industrial revolution effected a change from family production to production in workshops and factories. The result was

the congregation of families in towns, to be nearer the father's business. Not only did this lead to overcrowded housing conditions and an unnatural family life, but it broke up the interdependence and unity of the family circle. The average father, at the present time, must needs spend the greater part of the day working outside the home, in order to gain a livelihood. When he returns after his day's toil, he is too tired to take full advantage of the enjoyment he might derive from the society of his family. The wife, also, frequently seeks remunerative employment outside the home, thus leaving the children for long periods to their own devices. Married women sometimes work because home life is irksome to them, but usually because the husband does not earn a living wage. Economic conditions have, therefore, made family life much less stable than in former years. 

36. Changes in religious ideals have also led to family decadence.

Side by side with economic transformations within the family, have grown up spiritual changes. Many parents no longer possess the spirit of filial piety toward God which once was usual. This loss of religious spirit has resulted in a lack of true understanding of the holiness of family life, and of the mutual duties and obligations of parents and children.

Family life entails many sacrifices, and with the waning of religious ideals has come a decline in the spirit of self-forgetfulness which is necessary on the part of all members of a family for the maintenance of family unity and love. The decadence in religious ideals has, therefore, loosened family bonds to the detriment of domestic society. The increase of selfishness, the decline in filial respect, the diminution of parental sense of responsibility toward their children as charges for which they are responsible to God, have all led to a lack of reverence toward the sacredness of family life. Many forget that the family is the primary unit of society, and that if it fails to maintain high ideals, the nation will inevitably suffer. The nation is, indeed, already suffering from the decline in family life. Children who are not brought up in a spirit of reverence toward their parents, are not inclined

PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 49 

to obey authority in other quarters, be it civil or social. Children and parents who do not exercise a sense of social responsibility and coöperation within the home circle, cannot be expected to be good and useful members of society, reverencing and recognizing the social rights of others, and fulfilling their social obligations. 

37. City conditions have had a detrimental effect on family life.

We have already stated that where men work in cities, away from the family circle, the unity of the family is affected. City life, however, has other evil effects. The moral and physical influence of overcrowded housing conditions will be treated in another chapter. It must be noted, however, that city life is unnatural to man, who was born to enjoy fresh air and spacious surroundings. Recreation is essential for man's well-being, and in city slums, and even in the apartment houses of the well-to-do, facilities for healthful recreation are limited, if not wholly absent. 

Members of the family, therefore, must spend their leisure hours largely outside the home. For the satisfaction of their recreational needs they are, indeed, for the most part at the mercy of commercial undertakings. Not only do morals frequently suffer as a consequence of this, but the interdependence of the various members of the family, already affected by the changed economic system, decreases still more.

The provision of commercialized recreation outside of the home and the lack of normal family life, have also led to a great restlessness among the population. People crave for ever-increasing pleasure and enjoyment, and the mother and other members of the family become increasingly less attached to the home. 

Family life and the moral training of the children of necessity suffer.

But city life has led to other evils, too. Social competition has resulted in a person's worth being more often judged by the wealth he possesses or appears to possess, than by his moral qualities. Thrift and frugality have become almost forgotten virtues in many city homes, and thus the family becomes less and less united, and dissensions and disputes are more frequent.

38. There is at present less preparation and training for married life due to changed industrial, housing, and social conditions.

Economic life, at present spent mostly outside the family circle, in factory, workshop, office, or store, has had an extremely harmful effect on the preparation of children by parents for marriage and child rearing. Away at work for the greater part of the day, during the years when this training would be more naturally obtained, modern boys and girls do not learn the requisites for homemaking. Domestic unions certainly have many more chances of success if the man can turn his hand to household matters when occasion demands. But this training is essential for a 

Pub. Ser. Co. of No. III. 

Working conditions for this woman in the kitchen of an up-to-date home are vastly different from those in a factory. woman, on whom homemaking more naturally devolves. An adequate knowledge of how to run a home and care for husband and children is not easily to be learned, and requires more time than is normally possible under present-day industrial conditions. 

Again, although every mother should have, of course, some time for daily recreation, nevertheless, where she is occupied around the home for the greater part of the day, she provides a family bond, and earns an appreciation from her husband and offspring, which normally will not lead to a disrupted home. It is to be noted, however, that even where a girl does not go out

PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 51 

to earn her livelihood before marriage, but stays at home, the cramped living quarters of most city dwellings, the labor-saving devices, and the other amenities of modern life obviate any thorough training in domestic duties. This means the probable elimination of the spirit of coöperation which such duties foster. It means, too, that women have more leisure time at their disposal which, to be pleasantly employed, must, under normal city conditions, be spent outside the home, thus destroying one other bond of family union.

Nor is our present school system wholly without blame. 

Although we cannot but welcome the extension of education among the masses, nevertheless the family bond was much more stable and enduring when the child was at home during a great part of the day. His presence at home from his earliest years also insured an early training in domestic needs. A remedy, of course, lies in the school giving more attention to studies which engender homemaking qualities, both moral and manual. 

39. Lack of prudence and foresight in choosing a partner is likewise a frequent cause of domestic disunion. 

In his Encyclical on Christian Marriage, issued on December 31, 1930, Pope Pius XI clearly laid down the necessity of prudence and foresight before marriage. We will quote direct from the Encyclical: 

"To the proximate preparation of a good married life belongs very specially the care in choosing a partner. . . . And so that they may not deplore for the rest of their lives the sorrows arising from an indiscreet marriage, those about to enter into wedlock should carefully deliberate in choosing the person with whom henceforward they must live continually: they should, in so deliberating, keep before their minds the thought first of God and of the true religion of Christ, then of themselves, of their partner, of the children to come, as also of human and civil society, for which wedlock is a fountainhead. Let them diligently pray for divine help, so that they make their choice in accordance with Christian prudence, not indeed led by the blind and unrestrained impulse of lust, nor by any desire of riches or other

base influence, but by a true and noble love and by a sincere affection for the future partner; and then let them strive in their married life for those ends for which the state was constituted by God. Lastly, let them not omit to ask the prudent advice of their parents with regard to the partner, and let them regard this advice in no light manner, in order that by their mature knowledge and experience of human affairs they may guard against a disastrous choice, and, on the threshold of matrimony, may receive more abundantly the divine blessing of the Fourth Commandment."³

There was a time when parents chose husbands for their daughters, and wives for their sons, without consulting their children, and the practice is still to be found in many countries. While we should not recommend this procedure whole-heartedly, it is nevertheless surprising to note that these "arranged marriages" were proportionately more successful than present-day marriages undertaken by children without parental advice. It is certain that Catholics, who cannot countenance divorce as a remedy for an unsuccessful marriage, should exercise more than ordinary prudence and foresight in their choice of a mate. 

40. Lax divorce laws and marriage legislation have contributed to the more frequent break-up of family life. 

Unhappy marriages are much more frequent when the legal ceremony is very easily arranged, without the safeguards afforded by the publication of banns and by the requirement of parental consent for those under age, and a certain minimum time of residence in the district. Lax marriage legislation leads to hasty unions, with consequent incentive to lack of prudence. Similarly, husband and wife will be tempted to give rein to their emotions and to seek divorce on the slightest pretext, when divorces are easily obtained. If it is difficult to secure divorce, there is much more likelihood that quarrels and dissensions will be patched up when time has softened the misunderstanding. Most larger communities in the United States have what are known as Courts of Domestic Relations, which are special courts where all cases 'Casti Connubii, pp. 115-116.

PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 

53 involving domestic quarrels or dissensions come up for trial. 

Before the trial the social-service department of such a court examines each case which comes before it for divorce or separation, and tries to show the husband and wife where compromise is possible. The department's efforts very often result in private reconciliations.

41. Smaller families have also caused domestic disunion. 

The decline in the number of children in the modern family is, of course, due to a variety of causes. Cramped living quarters are an inducement to limit the size of the family; working mothers frequently forego the blessings of childbirth, as do also

the selfish and pleasure-seeking. Then, again, the rush of modernday life and later marriages are responsible for fewer births. 

Children cement parental love, and oftentimes preserve unity in a family when the parents may temporarily disagree. Divorces are not so lightly undertaken if there are children to be thought of. They are much more likely to occur if there is only one child to arrange for, or none at all. 

42. On account of the importance of the family to society, there is an urgent need for a reëstablishment of higher ideals regarding the family.

It will be evident from the foregoing discussion of some of the causes for present-day decadence in family life, that a reëstablishment of higher ideals regarding the family is essential to the true progress of society. We might summarize by saying that the first requisite for reform is the restoration of religious ideals and a true comprehension of the dignity of successful married life and of parenthood. When this dignity is realized once more, preparation for marriage will become more adequate, there will be greater prudence in selecting a partner, divorce will not be considered as an honorable way out of a domestic difficulty, parents and children will come to regard the home as a true sanctuary, and will be less inclined to seek solace and amusement elsewhere. To attain all this the sure remedy would be restoration of religious principles. Such a remedy would not only affect man's aspirations and his outlook, but his family life would be improved in other ways. So far as possible all men would be paid a living wage, sufficient to maintain their wives and children in the home; housing conditions would be improved; more incentives would be provided within the home to attract the members thither. 

43. There are many natural reasons against divorce. 

Marriage is not a private contract to be left to the consent and good pleasure of both parties, and to be dissolved for any reason whatsoever. It is a social obligation freely undertaken by the parents because of the need which God has implanted in man for married life and for domestic unity and love. Just as it is plain

PRESENT-DAY DECADENCE IN FAMILY LIFE 55 

that the family can only best develop when it is monogamous, so it must be evident that where divorce can be obtained on the slightest pretext, the whole family suffers. We showed in the preceding chapter that for natural reasons alone it may be proved that God originally intended the family to be monogamous, and that wherever polygamy was permitted it was always an abuse. 

Divorce is thus against the natural law of monogamy. Strict monogamy no longer exists where frequent divorce occurs, and the present permission given by civil authority for divorce is, therefore, contrary to man's natural welfare. Divorce is, indeed, one of society's greatest evils. The children in a divorced family suffer the most, for they are deprived of loving care and help and a normal family life. Many children of divorced parents fail to receive the training necessary for useful social and economic life. Others, unable to respect their parents, develop a disrespect of the law. Only too often the cause of juvenile delinquency, crime, and poverty may be traced to the lack of a normal home life in early years. 

44. There are also positive divine reasons why divorce must not be allowed.

The Church does not sanction divorce, and it is claimed that her attitude inflicts a severe hardship on those who wish to break the marriage bonds. It is true that some innocent people may at times suffer when they obey the Church's law; nevertheless the 

Church cannot, for the sake of benefit to a few, change a precept which is grounded in the natural law, in the welfare of society, and on the solemn words of Christ Himself. In certain cases, however, to prevent worse evil, the Church does allow married people to live apart from each other, though they cannot remarry.

Chapter VI THE STATE 

45. The family needs the coöperation of others for its economic and social welfare. 

At the beginnings of the race the family was sufficient to care for human needs. As needs and inclinations grew, however, family coöperation was not sufficient. Families require the assistance of other families to satisfy their social instinct and their want of protection, mutual coöperation, and fellowship. The single family would normally be powerless to protect itself from attack and the violation of its rights, and needs the assistance of other people for protection and self-defense, and for the maintenance of peace. Similarly, if each family had to be selfsupporting and provide for its own requirements, there would be little time for those with abilities in craftsmanship or the arts or sciences to follow their true avocation. Nor could the single family provide the means of communication, roads, mail service, education, trade facilities, and many other worth-while things of life, without the assistance of others in similar need. Although the family is the basic social unit of society, it is not a perfect society in itself, for it is impossible for it to exist independently of the coöperation and help provided by other families associated with it. For mutual protection and the promotion of their well-being, therefore, families and individuals group themselves into a larger society which is known as a state. 

46. A state is a civil society, made up of four elements. 

A state is a civil society, usually characterized by a certain degree of homogeneity in race, language, social and cultural ideals, which distinguishes it from other states. It is made up of four elements: a given population or community, that is, an association of individuals and families, and all the municipalities, 56

THE STATE 

57 counties, and other groups to which they belong; a defined territory in which they live; freedom from foreign control; and a government-an agency through which the common good of the people is realized.

Although the words state and nation are now used synonymously, it might be well to note that state comes from the Latin status, meaning something fixed and established, hence primarily the organization and government of a people; whereas the word nation is derived from the Latin nascor, natus, meaning birth, and referring primarily to the common origin of a people. We keep this original sense of the word nation when we speak of one's nationality, the place of one's birth. 

47. States came into being in divers ways.

History is silent as to the way in which states first arose, although the Old Testament quite definitely suggests the state's rise in the patriarchal system — the banding together of families related by blood, under the authority of an elder, for the reasons stated in the first paragraph of this chapter. Then, for the same reasons, several groups or clans probably joined into larger communities, occupying a definite territory, speaking a common language, and thus becoming a tribe. Later some of these tribes developed into still larger societies, to form a folk or ethnic group. On the other hand, some tribes divided and developed variations in language and custom, probably not becoming closely associated with others until much later in their history. 

To trace the history of existing states is an interesting study. 

One might note the tenacity with which the Polish peoples remained a nation without being a state for any length of time. 

Then finally, despite centuries of domination by Germany on the one hand, and Russia, on the other, the state of Poland emerged at the end of the Great War. Belgium is composed of two distinct races, Walloons and Flemish, and the Flemish, although speaking the same language as the Dutch, retain their Belgian nationality. 

Numberless other examples might be cited.

History tells us how geographical environment, natural resources, unusual opportunities or lack of opportunities, have

played an important part in the later growth of states into larger or smaller territories and also in their social composition. 

Nor have all states been formed by the process of aggregation which we have described, although this is largely true of the United States of America, Canada, the South American Republics, and many others. Some states have been greatly influenced and absorbed by domination from outside nations, as witness the early history of Great Britain, and of most of the European countries.

An ethnic state is one formed by peoples of one race, as, for example, Japan. Peoples forming a state by mutual consent, or by domination, but without regard for origins or blood relationship, are said to be a demotic society, of which the United States is an example.

Whenever a group of families becomes conscious of its civic unity and submits to the rule of one man or a government, or elects a ruler or government to conduct its joint affairs, a state in the true sense of the word is formed. 

48. The state is a perfect natural society. 

The state is a natural society because in it is fully expressed man's aptitude and propensity for group life, for authority and submission to authority; and in it is also found the full expression of the fundamental human need to gather into groups for protection and for the promotion of social welfare. In civil life alone can man attain his full temporal development, further his interests, and protect his rights and liberty from the encroachment of others. To live in civil society or the state is therefore natural to man. As explained in Chapter II, the state is a perfect society because it does not depend upon any higher power, other than God alone. All other societies are dependent either upon the state or upon the Church. 

49. No state can exist without authority, but it may be organized as a monarchy, an oligarchy, or a democracy. 

Since God made men social beings, and no group of men can pursue a common end harmoniously and with advantage for any length of time without a directing hand, authority is absolutely necessary in any society. Of its very nature, then, authority is

THE STATE 

59 essential to a social group, and must come from God. "There is no power but from God." For this reason we have already stated that authority is one of the four essential elements of the state. 

But although God made it natural for man to belong to a state, by giving him a propensity, aptitude, and need for more extensive social coöperation than that provided by the family, yet He did not determine in whom or in what form of government man should vest this civil authority. As explained by St. Robert Acme Congress in session. These men represent the people of the United States in making the laws. 

Bellarmine, the authority necessary for the right conduct of the state (civil authority) is first vested by God immediately in the people. The people, however, cannot themselves exercise it, or chaos would exist. Therefore in forming civil society (the state) they transfer their authority to the ruler or rulers to whom they intrust the reins of government. The government of a state thus derives its power from God, through the people.2 This authority 

¹Rom. xiii. 1. 

2Some philosophers, however, are of the opinion that authority comes directly from God to those who are designated to exercise it, and not through the people and their natural organization into civil society. Thus in the matter of life and death (exercised by a judge, etc.) they claim that the authority must come directly from God, for He alone has the power over life, saying that no man or group of men possesses such power.

or power is conferred on the ruler or rulers in the measure and after the manner in which the people desire to confer it when they establish their particular form of government. 

We must recognize, therefore, that the state is the whole people organized for government, with the authority which God intrusted to them, now vested no longer in the multitude, but in the actual ruler or executive who succeeds to power through inheritance, through election by the people as their representative, or in any Keystone

Canada's Parliament in session. other fashion decided upon by the people in establishing a definite state. The government may have any form, provided it insures the general welfare and does not enforce laws which are against the natural law.

It must be noted, too, that man can choose the state to which he wants to belong. Most men, on reaching the age when they might decide to adopt a nationality other than that of their birth or the choice of their parents, usually continue their allegiance to the state of which they are already members without thought or hesitation. Some, however, transfer their allegiance to other nations, as do immigrants who become naturalized as citizens of the United States.

THE STATE 

61 There are many types of government, but they may be broadly classed into three main divisions: 

1. A monarchy, where the supreme political control is in the hands of one person, whose office may be elective or hereditary. 

Monarchies are of two types: (a) the absolute, where the ruler holds absolute and despotic sway, as in the time of the Roman Emperors; (b) the limited. A limited monarchy may be constitutional, such as existed in Medieval Europe, where the fundamental laws of the state limit the ruler's power, and where the ruler likewise consults the nobility, the clergy, and the representatives of the people on matters of government. It may likewise be the parliamentary type of government where the rule is in the hands of the parliament, and where the king, while nominally appointing executive ministers to rule the country, actually can select only those approved by the majority in the parliament. 

This type of government approaches an oligarchy or a democracy, despite the fact that the head of the country is a king. 

2. An oligarchy. In an oligarchy or aristocracy, the government of the state is in the hands of a few people, membership in the ruling class being determined by birth, by wealth, or by brains. 

3. A democracy is a state where the government is in the hands of the people either directly or indirectly. Direct democracy would mean that all citizens would have to pass a vote on each governmental issue. However, indirect democracy is the only existing form at present; i.e., the people elect a body of representatives to form a government, and delegate all or nearly all of their power to these deputies. Each individual state in the United 

States is a democracy, and the federation of these states into the United States is likewise a democracy; so the American citizen really belongs to two states. Democracies are of many types, some approaching an oligarchy or even a monarchy, in form. In Russia, under the Bolshevist government, only the working class is given voting rights, and unskilled artisans have the most privileges. In 

Fascist Italy control of both government and business is organized through the fourteen "Corporations" of employers and workers, under the direction of Mussolini.

THE STATE 

63 As previously stated, any one of the three above-mentioned types of government is legitimate, provided the general welfare of the members of the state is insured, and provided no laws are enforced which are against the natural law and interfere with man's natural rights and duties. If the multitude composing a state is seriously dissatisfied with the existing form of government, and there is a really lawful cause, then it is justified in 

Ewing Galloway

Where the chief business of the people of the United States is conducted. changing that government by legitimate means and in substituting another. It is necessary, however, that the succeeding government be in accordance with the natural law. And since the state is a natural society, because man has a fundamental need of it, it is perpetual by nature. Therefore, although a change of government may occur, the new form must carry out the obligations which its predecessor undertook with other states, e.g., the payment of debts previously contracted. 

50. The power of the state is limited by the rights of individuals and of lawful societies. 

Although the state is sovereign in its own sphere, and has the power to enact laws and to enforce their observance even, where necessary, by force, the authority of the state is not unlimited. 

First, the state is limited by the measure of power conferred

on it by the people. Then, too, the state itself is merely a unified body of the persons who compose it, and since all men are moral beings, the state is a moral being as well, and hence is subject to the moral law. It cannot, therefore, make laws which are contrary to the natural law and the common good. 

Again, the state exists solely for the use of man, because man has need of it to live a full and complete human life. It is therefore limited in its power by the natural rights of individuals and of groups of individuals, and by the rights of the Church. 

The individual and the family both existed before any state came into being, and it is the state's duty, not to usurp their rights, but to safeguard and promote them. Similarly, since man has a natural right to social life, he has a right to form any social group or society he chooses, provided it be not dangerous or subversive of the natural law. The state must, therefore, preserve and protect the right of any lawfully constituted society, however small.

Finally, the state must not interfere with the Church's authority in matters pertaining to faith and morals, but must protect its rights because the Church has a right to exist as a lawful, natural, and perfect society. This is true also because man's supernatural interests have been intrusted exclusively to the Church, and the state, existing for the good of man, must in nowise hinder his eternal welfare, for this is of more importance than anything else in the world. 

51. The function of the state is to protect the rights of the individuals who compose it, and to promote their well-being. 

It is the duty of the state to do whatever is necessary to carry out these functions.

The state's whole reason for existence is that it can provide more completely than the individual or smaller group for man's needs, and foster the temporal happiness of its members. 

1. The state must protect the rights of individuals and groups which compose it, and in doing this it must protect the rights of everyone, not merely those of a majority, or of the rich or influential.

THE 



STATE 

2. The state must promote the well-being of its members. Whenever any work that is not primarily an affair of the government can be satisfactorily accomplished by private initiative and smaller societies, the state should not undertake that work. If the state undertakes unnecessary tasks, it is in no way promoting social well-being. Man should be free to undertake that which he thinks best, provided it be lawful; and if the state leaves smaller groups to do what they can, it will then have greater freedom to carry out its other duties.

U.S. Treas. Dept. 

Public health officers at work.

The chief functions of the state are as follows: 

1. Religion: It should foster and protect religion by its laws, and it should not enact any measure to compromise its safety. 

Firemen at work. 

2. Protective services: The state should exercise its "police" powers for the maintenance of internal peace, order, and safety, and for the preservation of the community from external encroachments that would overthrow its security. It should provide an army, a navy, a police force, and judicial service. It should have adequate legislation for the protection of rights and the enforcement of duties, and should provide preventive and reformative measures for prisoners and juvenile delin-

quents. It should preserve health by the provision of sanitation and general health laws, such as for street cleaning, quarantine of infectious diseases, and against adulteration of foods; by the provision of sanitary inspectors, health measures for workers and school children; by the maintenance, where public aid is necessary, of hospitals for the care of the insane, feeble-minded, defectives, and all dependent incurables; and by health and
