A court scene. A witness is on the stand at the right of the judge. Just in front of the judge are the clerk of court and the court reporter (at table). In the foreground are the lawyers and their clients. On the right, standing at the rail, is the bailiff. A mixed jury of 12 is in the box. accident insurance where satisfactory provisions cannot be privately made. 

3. Preservation of a high standard of morality: The state should suppress vice, and should pass laws against immoral conduct, and incitements to immorality, such as pornographic literature and plays. 

4. The operation of public works and the conservation of

THE STATE 

67 natural resources: Provision must be made for roads, bridges, canals, post offices, and public utilities; also for the upkeep of national parks, the preservation of beauty spots, afforestation schemes, and so forth, where these cannot be satisfactorily maintained by private efforts. 

5. Education: The state must foster education and supply the defects of private educational systems. It may thus establish universities, museums, libraries, art galleries, botanical gardens, and other educative facilities. The state must not put itself in the place of the family, however, but merely supply its deficiencies and perform part of the disciplinary powers formerly exerted by the parents alone. 

6. The enactment of social legislation to regulate economic life: All men should have an opportunity to participate in the public prosperity. The state should take such positive action as the regulation of imports and exports, coinage, banks, corporations, privately owned public utilities, such as electric-light and power companies, and street-car corporations. It should safeguard the worker by providing legislation to insure Sunday rest, hygienic working conditions, security of employment, the payment of a living wage, the protection of trade unions and other private but legitimate social groups. The state should also combat unjust speculation, protect consumers against fraud, conduct research, and provide commercial information which cannot be readily obtained from private sources. It should encourage industries (a) negatively, by putting no obstacle in the way of legitimate, wholesome employment and its profit; (b) positively, by the protection of patents, the encouragement of private associations, and by giving rewards to real benefactors of mankind. 

7. The relief of destitution and care for all dependents and defectives: The state must provide hospitals for the sick, homes for the dependent poor who cannot be cared for in other ways, financial assistance to the needy, old-age pensions, and so forth, insofar as such measures may be necessary. The needs of married people and their dependents must be given special care, for the welfare of the state depends ultimately on the welfare of the families which compose it.

8. Administrative and representative services: The state must provide municipal and governmental administrative departments, and also a diplomatic corps for its dealings with other states. 

Ewing Galloway

Dining room in an old men's home. In homes such as this the state cares for those dependent on it. 

52. The rights of the state are to demand anything which is necessary for it to carry out its functions in an efficient manner.

In order to prevent abuse on the part of the officials who form the governing body, and who represent the state in the carrying out of its functions, the rights of the rulers of the state must be limited by the authority committed to them in the constitution, and by constitutional enactments of the state. Within these limitations, however, the state as a whole has the following rights: 

1. The right to do anything necessary for the common good. 

2. The right to expect citizens to coöperate with it fully in the exercise of its duties toward the common good, for this is an end which is the very raison d'être of civil society.

THE STATE 

69 3. The right to expect citizens to obey its laws; and to punish transgressions, for the common good. 

4. The right to conscript men for military service, when necessary for the defense of the country. 

5. The right to exact taxes, to secure the requisite finances to carry on its work.

Since citizens need the coöperation of the state to protect their rights and to promote their well-being, they in turn must coöperate with the state in its efforts to fulfill its duties to other citizens.

Since rights involve corresponding duties; since man is a social being and must coöperate in any effort to help his fellow men; since also the state is a natural society, deriving its authority Based on figures taken from The World Almanac, 1941, p. 756.

2. He must respect public authority, both public officials and the laws they enact.

54. There are many problems connected with the state at the present time.

Human nature being fallible, it is obvious that neither the state nor the citizens fulfill their duties perfectly. Charity and justice are not clearly recognized in the world, by either citizens or all the governing body of officials. There is much political corruption, graft, favoritism in tariffs, secret agreements and treaties with individuals and other states. This condition of affairs naturally militates against fair and equitable service being rendered by the state to all its citizens and to all those who have dealings with it. It likewise makes it difficult for the citizen to maintain a fitting respect toward officials whose dishonesty is known to him, and to obey laws which he knows are unjust. 

The inhabitants of the state themselves are frequently indifferent to their duties as citizens: they do not keep themselves informed on public affairs, and they either vote haphazardly, or even neglect to use their vote at all. Individuals and groups likewise often carry out their functions imperfectly, so that the state must step in to fill the gap. From this arises a danger of officials assuming too much power, and a further danger that they will abuse that power, as we stated in the paragraph describing the function of the state. The direction of group life in all its multiform phases should, wherever possible, be left to the initiative of individuals or private groups. If all citizens did but carry out the dictates of social justice and charity, the state could then confine its activities to those tasks which private initiative is unable to accomplish satisfactorily. Relieved of a multiplicity of unnecessary duties, the state could be more efficient, and come nearer the ideal than any which exist at the present time.

Chapter VII  

55. Man has many needs, both spiritual and material. 

It is clear that man has many needs in life: he needs the family to bring him into being and to provide him with affection, training, and care in early youth; the state, to provide him with the protection and assistance which the family cannot supply, or which he himself is unable to furnish; and the Church, to direct and insure his spiritual welfare. These three, the family, the 

Church, and the state, are the most important primary societies, W-3899 

Pauls' Photos 

This picture illustrates the many operations necessary in automobile manufacture. The material has gone through many hands too before it can be used here. 72

but they are not all-sufficing. A host of subsidiary social groups, for recreation, education, economic and social wants, are necessary for man's well-being-some in a greater and some in a less degree. It is considered that for man's full material development and comfort, two other societies are so fundamentally necessary that they, too, may be called primary groups: international society, to be outlined in a later chapter, and occupational society, which we shall discuss now. 

U.S. Dept. of Labor 

Workers in a candy factory. Working conditions for these girls are quite good. 

56. Material goods are necessary to maintain life, and are supplied by nature: but man must work to obtain them. 

The food and clothing which man needs in order to live, are furnished in abundance by nature. However, despite the abundance of the fruits of the earth, man would soon die if he did not seek his food and provide himself with shelter. To lead even the simplest life man must hunt, fish, gather in fruits, and labor in many other ways to avail himself of nature's supplies. Many of nature's goods need complicated services to make them of use; for example, iron must not only be taken from the earth, but must be smelted into steel, rolled into bars, hardened and shaped, before finally being made into an automobile spring, let us say; wheat seed must be stored up, the

fields must be ploughed, the grain sown, the harvest reaped and ground, before the flour is ready, and that again must be kneaded and made into dough and baked into bread, before we eat it. 

Many of man's requirements, therefore, can be provided for only by work. Before Adam's fall, seeking the wherewithal to live was a pleasurable occupation, but since that time the earth has yielded man life only "in the sweat of his face." Acme 

Pouring metal in a steel mill. 

57. Man has a fourfold obligation to work. Although the rich have a right to use their possessions to provide themselves and their dependents with material needs, no one is exempt from undertaking some form of honest labor. 

Man is bound to work for four main reasons: (1) He has a duty to himself to maintain his own life. (2) He has a duty to 

¹Gen. iii. 19.

provide for those dependent upon him. (3) He has a duty to contribute to the general welfare of society. (4) He has a duty to God, not only to preserve his own life, but also to use the gifts of nature and to develop his faculties conformably to the divine will.

Despite man's obligation to work, however, not all men are bound to provide for their own sustenance or that of their dependents. The rich, for example, have a right to live on the 

International News

Coal miners at work. This is one of the most difficult and dangerous of all occupations. proceeds of their riches. Neither is there an obligation to work hard during all one's waking hours: A certain amount of recreation and rest is essential; social intercourse or the pursuit of the arts may afford us great pieasure and yet be our duty; and it is at least advisable to have leisure time for personal development. 

But no one may be idle, for, as we have just stated, man has a duty to lead an honest and useful life; he has a duty, too, to use the gifts of nature and to develop his personal gifts and faculties. Work, indeed, develops not only physical and mental

76. 

powers, but moral qualities also, and all labor, if done for a good purpose, has a high moral value. 

58. Man can best supply his needs by coöperating with others in the division of labor.

Man's economic needs are better provided for by a mutual division of labor - that is, by coöperating with others in the necessary work - than by striving to provide for all the require- 

AUTOSERVE CONCERN 

Acme This new rotoserve market is one of the many labor-saving devices being used in industry. The customer sits at ease and selects her articles from the shelf which revolves. ments of himself and his family by his own labor. Men have varying gifts and aptitudes for different kinds of work, and only by coöperating with each other can they more fully develop them. 

Some people are fitted for leadership and for positions of responsibility and authority; others prefer working in capacities which do not necessitate independent action on their part. Some men are happiest as artists, others as bookkeepers, engineers, tailors, blacksmiths, or in domestic service. A man contributes more to the world's wealth if he confines himself to the work he does

best than if he performs many varied duties. He can receive a reward for his specialized services in the form of goods, or money which can be exchanged into the goods he needs. Moreover, some countries, or even some districts, can produce certain kinds of goods to greater advantage than others, and it is best that their inhabitants should specialize in these products, exchanging their surplus for other commodities. 

59. Economic coöperation has led to the various economic systems described in history. These systems may be termed occupational society. Occupational society is the regulation of man's labor, and its organization into groups which function within civil society. 

We have just defined occupational society as the regulation of man's labor, and its organization into groups which function within civil society, that is, within the state. Occupational society is a primary society because man has not only an aptitude and propensity for it, but also a fundamental need of it. As we have just shown, work is a duty and a necessity; and although man could exist without the division of labor, this coöperation enables a man to specialize in a certain task and thus to be more useful in the world, to produce more wealth, and to enjoy more goods. 

Occupational society must, therefore, have been preordained in the divine order of the universe. 

Man has coöperated with others from the earliest ages, although the organization was extremely simple at the outset, and is most complicated at the present time. 

We have many records that even in primitive times the men confined themselves to hunting, fishing, and looking after the cattle; while the women attended to household duties, made the clothes, and sometimes tilled the soil, although quite frequently this latter labor, too, fell to the lot of the men. This was a simple family division of labor, or a friendly coöperation within the tribe. Later, specialized trades grew up, and while the majority of families still cared for their own needs, others, with special aptitudes for certain tasks, devoted their labor to the different arts and crafts, receiving goods or money in return.

At various times within the history of man slave systems have regulated this labor, when men and women were, for sundry reasons, forced to work for their employer on whatever terms he chose to lay down. These systems have always been, of course, against man's natural and inherent right of liberty. It is interesting to note that in the early Egyptian, Greek, and Roman times there were even well-organized trade unions of workers, although these in some measure perpetuated a slave system, for often a worker's children were obliged to remain within the trade of the father, and could only marry children of those who practised the same trade. 

60. The manorial and the gild systems of England are the best known of the early economic institutions. 

Under the manorial system of the early middle ages in Europe, the lord of the manor acquired his lands by birth or conquest, and divided them among the serfs. These serfs were obliged to work for him, but in return they received the right to a portion of the soil and its products, and the privilege of grazing their cattle on the common land. Every man had a sufficiency, although each was "addicted" to the soil on which he lived; that is, he was not free ever to leave it permanently. 

Then, as a result of the land inclosures which followed on the growth of the continental wool trade, and the increased knowledge which travelers brought them of conditions elsewhere, many of the serfs, or villeins, on manorial lands went to the towns and there established themselves in trades, and with the money they earned they satisfied their needs by buying at the local markets. 

The majority of men still worked on the soil, but in the towns the gild system arose. Every boy who wished to learn a trade, had to be accepted into the gild of that particular occupation. He then became an apprentice, and lived with his master's family, and was treated as the latter's son. After several years of apprenticeship he would be advanced to the status of journeyman. In this position he might travel from town to town to learn of new methods in other districts; he might continue to reside with his master, to become as a younger brother to him; or he

might marry and establish his own home, but still work for his master. Later, with enough money saved from his earnings, he could set up a business of his own. There was plenty of opportunity for him to do this, for the gilds limited the number of apprentices and journeymen each master might employ. The gilds also regulated working conditions and wages for employees, and the price which might be charged to the consumer, and there were severe penalties for an infringement of the gild laws. 

Competition between masters was not on the price of goods, but on design and workmanship, so that a man took a pride in his work, and insisted on good work being done by his employees. 

Despite the advantages of the gild organization, however, the gilds lost their power, partly due to government action, and partly on account of abuses which had arisen within them. While the majority of Englishmen were still agricultural laborers, and while industries were still carried on in the home, rich men employed many workers and paid them what they chose. Nevertheless, the worker still had his family about him, could be with them all day long, and he still had fresh air and good wholesome food, and no mechanical devices to set the pace of his work; he was also in close touch with his employer. 

61. The industrial revolution was, in large measure, the beginning of modern economic evils.

With the invention and widespread adoption of machinery, the industrial revolution began in the second half of the eighteenth century in Europe and the United States. Domestic labor could at this time no longer compete with the cheaper machinemade goods; men had to leave their homes to work in the factories where rich men had placed machinery. The worker could no longer own his implements of production, as he did under the domestic system, because they were too expensive and complicated to be worked by the few. He, therefore, had no other choice than acceptance of the employer's terms, and the latter gave him long hours of labor, under wretched conditions, and poor pay. 

Because of the increase of simple machine processes, women and children were employed and paid less wages than the men, so

that the men had to compete with them for lower wages, and no working-class family could do otherwise than have all the members working at the earliest possible age, to enable them to obtain at least a bare existence. Family life was thus impaired. 

The greater division of labor led to the increased dependence of a man on his employment, for he was usually engaged in doing but one small operation. He learned no skilled trade with which he might find employment elsewhere; and the monotony and fatigue attending his work made him unfit for another type of labor, and prevented the full development of his character and of his mental and physical capacities. Pauper children were placed in factories at the age of eight or nine, or even earlier. 

The philosophy of laissez-faire was fashionable at the time, according to which it was thought that the worker should be left to make a free bargain with his employer, and that the liberty of both employer and worker would be interfered with unjustly if the government put an end to the terrible abuse of labor. The organization of labor into trade unions was forbidden for the same reason. Unlimited free competition and cheap labor led to a vast accumulation of wealth and power in the hands of the few, and the weak and the poor were exploited by the rich and the strong. The result was a complete transition from the family spirit which existed among employers and employed under the gild system, to an ever-widening breach between capitalistic employers and the workers who lived a hand-to-mouth existence on the meager wages they received. 

62. Legislation, trade unionism, and saner views led to an improvement of the workers' condition. 

Conditions such we have just described could not long endure without remedy. For the past hundred years legislation has been gradually introduced to give the workers better wages, shorter working hours, more sanitary working conditions, and to protect woman and child labor. The workers have greatly helped their own progress by the formation of trade unions, although these, in turn, often increased the breach between employer and employee. The employer has also realized that when his workers

are healthy and happy they produce much more and therefore help him to earn larger profits, than when they are half starved and discontented, when they are forced to be absent from work for long intervals on account of ill health, or when they go from place to place in an effort to improve their condition. Although workers are now frequently regarded almost as machines, expected to turn out a certain amount of work of a fixed standard and at a fixed pace; although they must now contend with the increased insecurity of modern industry, must bear the risk of prolonged unemployment, and are subject to all the other evils we shall discuss later on, nevertheless they enjoy many comforts formerly 

U.S. Dept. of Labor 

A library scene. Working conditions in such a place as this are very good and the work itself is pleasant. beyond their reach; wider educational opportunities are available to them; they have the possibility of rising to managerial positions in a manner before unknown; and they have sometimes been able to make a fortune by speculation, although, of course, many people hope that the modern large-scale speculative possibilities will soon be curbed. 

63. Rural workers are frequently better off than those employed in other industries, but these, too, have their problems.

Because the greater proportion of mankind now works in industries other than agricultural, we have confined our discussion chiefly to industrial problems, and will continue to limit ourselves in this manner in other chapters on occupational society. Ever since the industrial revolution, the trend of population in all civilized countries has been cityward, and the United States is no exception, as the following figures clearly indicate:2 Urban

Although many farmers now conduct their business on capitalistic large-scale methods, most farms are still small units worked by the owner or tenant farmer and his laborers. Life on the farm is certainly a more natural thing than spending the larger portion of one's day in a noisy workshop or in an office, and the farmer and his family get the benefit of fresh air and good food. Many would-be reformers of our social system are anxious to see more

Whether or not it is possible for town dwellers to make such a change and be happy is still a matter of conjecture. Town life provides many attractions in the form of greater facilities for social intercourse, for recreation, education, and a choice of many occupations. On the other hand, rural life could be greatly improved by an increase in village social activities, by traveling libraries, better travel facilities, well-planned education, and other ameliorations. 

Agriculture, however, is "industry's poor relation." Prices at the time of writing are so low that the farmer can make little

or no profit on his goods. This situation is due to two main causes: First, taxation on farm land has vastly and speedily increased within recent years, to such an extent that all profits sometimes go to pay taxes. This taxation has come about by the rapid building of expensive roads suitable for automobile traffic, with heavy state assessments on the adjacent farms. Then, too, rural districts often have been forced to share with city taxpayers in the costly public buildings and other civic improvements which have been erected during the course of this century, with their necessary officials, employees, and cost of upkeep. Second, the

The traveling library is a great help to the social life of the farmer. farmer sells his produce to the wholesale merchants, who in turn sell them to other wholesalers, or to the retailer or consumer direct at greatly increased prices. This development of the urban middleman and his organizations has so regulated prices that the profits have been diverted from the producing farmer to the distributing middleman, and the farmer has been reduced almost to destitution.

A reorganization of the whole agricultural industry seems essential, but just what form that reorganization should take is at present impossible to say. Certainly it appears that the farmers would benefit by definite plans of organized marketing, such as the coöperative societies in Denmark, Ireland, and certain parts of the United States. Coöperative movements would also help the

small holder to have the use of machinery now available only to those having large amounts of capital at their disposal; and ampler facilities for money credit might enable more people to settle happily on the soil. 

64. The modern capital istic system came into being to provide the large sums of money needed for the purchase of equipment and for the manufacture and sale of products on a large scale. 100% 90% 80% 70% 60% 50% 40% 30% 20% 10%

RURAL POPULATION 

Ever since the industrial revolution the invention of machinery and labor-saving devices has increased, and large sums of money have been needed to purchase and maintain factories and their equipment, and to carry on large-scale business with the idea of making greater profits.

Manufacturers were unable to provide this capital themselves, so the stock-exchange system arose. Under this system, if a proposition appears marketable, an appeal is made to the public for the necessary funds, usually through the medium of investment banking houses.*

The trend of population from the country to the cities. Note that in 

1890 only 35.4% of the population was urban, while in 1940, 56.6%, over half of the population. lived in cities. or Lenders of money at a fixed interest rate, on the security of land, buildings, or other assets, receive certificates called bonds, which are usually repayable at the end of a certain period. If a "Investment banks are banks which specialize in financing business, by undertaking the public issue of stocks and bonds on behalf of various corporations, or by making large private loans to industrial concerns.

capitalist wishes to become a part owner of the business, sharing in the profits yearly by receiving dividends in accordance with the amount of his investment, the company gives him stock certificates. These bonds and stocks then become commodities, as it were, which can be bought and sold at a special market called a "stock exchange," through dealers who are called "stock brokers." If a holder wishes to sell part or all of his holdings, he puts them up for sale on the stock exchange through his banker or broker. If a capitalist wishes to buy some stocks or Acme 

The New York Curb Market in operation. 

bonds, he purchases them on the stock exchange in the same manner. The price of these capitalistic holdings in business is dependent not only on earnings, but also on the supply and demand of the particular securities. If a company makes large profits, its dividend payments per share of stock may become very high, which means that the stocks are much sought after, and the price rises. This situation makes the bonds rise somewhat in price as well, for the security behind them will generally be all the greater. Speculation as to supply and demand and future dividend yields, leads to great fluctuation in the prices

of stocks and bonds, and many fortunes are made and lost in stock-exchange operations, which are matters quite apart from the business itself. This speculative trading is often called "stock gambling"; and, in fact, it does not differ greatly from gambling on races or in games of chance. 

65. The capitalistic organization of occupational society has led to many grave problems.

By the capitalistic organization of occupational society, which we have just described, not only is there in most businesses a marked breach between the capitalist employer and the worker, but there is also a complete divorce between the ownership of a business and its management and control. The owners of bonds and stocks know little of the methods by which their companies are conducted. They are represented in the management by a board of directors, who may or may not conduct the business fairly, and who often have but a nominal capital interest in the companies they manage. Sometimes even the directors themselves are merely figureheads in the direction of the concern, which is left to a well-paid manager, sometimes called the "organizer," "entrepreneur," or "enterpriser."

Those who actually manage the business have control of large sums of capital, and the capital owners are entirely dependent upon them for the safe-keeping of their capital, for the interest it yields, and for the right treatment of employees, and the charging of a just price to the consumer. The interests of the consumers and employees, however, are always subordinated to the interests of the capitalists. Stock-exchange prices are a ruling factor in the acquisition of any future capital needs, and the chief concern of directors and managers is that good profits are made.

The ever-growing desire for larger profits has led to cut-throat competition between companies selling the same products, with the hope that increased sales at the lower prices will make greater aggregate monetary returns. To meet the competition in prices, companies frequently amalgamate with others, so that large trusts, combines, and monopolies practically govern the world's economic life. It is becoming increasingly difficult for small

businesses to compete with these larger ones, and a great number of people are yearly withdrawing from the small-employer class to that of the paid employee. This means that an ever-increasing number of people are becoming entirely dependent upon the business policies of the comparatively few "captains of industry." 

In 1927 Professor W. I. King computed that 2 per cent of the population of the United States at that time owned between them 40 per cent of the total wealth of the country; 33 per cent of the population were middle-class people, owning from $3,500 to $50,000 each, or a total of 45 per cent of the total wealth; the remaining 65 per cent of the population owned only 15 per cent of the wealth between them they were the poor whose fortunes 

ranged from nothing to a maximum of $3,500. 

Greed and overconfidence in the business future leads to overproduction. Surplus stocks must then be sold off at lower prices, to get rid of them before newer goods are made, and when overcapitalization is present, the resultant smaller profits must be divided among many more capital owners than are necessary. 

Unemployment is the result, and the unemployed buy fewer goods and so in their turn help to increase unemployment for others.

It is evident that occupational society needs replanning, so that the capitalist will take his place as a coöperator with the worker, but not as being more important; and so that the vast differences in the ownership of the world's wealth will be at least somewhat diminished. Depressions must inevitably occur at frequent intervals, until such time as this replanning is successfully achieved. 

The N.R.A. program of 1933, which was an effort to bring about this transformation, will be discussed in Appendix II. 

66. Present-day occupational groups provide many social relations.

In providing for man's material needs of food, clothing, shelter, and various services, a new set of social relations comes into being, which may be termed "economic relations," and which it will be well to discuss before closing the chapter. Since all $Journal of the American Statistical Association, June, 1927.

social intercourse implies duties and obligations on the one side, and rights on the other, these universal needs lead to many complicated problems. Man's economic life may, indeed, be associated with others in any of the following ways: 

1. He has relations with persons or organizations which supply him directly with his economic needs; in connection with the goods provided by stores, farmers, land or house owners, railroads and other transportation agencies, servants, doctors, tailors, teachers, plumbers, to cite but a few examples. These relations refer to man in his capacity as a "consumer," that is, as needing goods, and satisfying these needs. Every man alive is, of course, a consumer, and in this capacity he has a duty not to cheat the seller in any way, but to pay him a fair price for what he has received, whether the goods "consumed" have been commodities or services. 

2. If a man supplies economic goods or services to consumers, one must regard his economic relations from a different angle. 

He is called a producer, and there comes his duty to provide the purchaser with satisfactory commodities or services, not to charge him more than a just price, and to adhere by the terms of any just bargain which may have been made. 

3. The worker has relations with his employer. He must be loyal to him and work faithfully and to the best of his ability. 

4. The employer, on the other hand, has duties toward his employees. He must treat his workers with dignity and justice. 

He must give them a just, living wage, provide good working conditions, and see that their working hours are not excessive. but that they have time for recreation, Sunday rest, and religious observance. He must also refrain from employing women and children in unsuitable occupations. And he must not imitate present frequent injustices to labor, such as the terrible abuse of house-service at salaries wholly inadequate to the living requirements of servants, and to the quantity and responsible nature of the service required; and the payment of less than a living wage to women and to Negroes, whereas the wage paid "The term "goods" in economic language signifies all commodities and services which satisfy human wants.

to men or white people doing the same work is a living wage, or at least falls less short of it than theirs. 

5. The worker also has relations with his fellow workmen, toward whom he must exercise charity and justice, and for whom he must help make working conditions more pleasurable. 

6. The capitalist must do all in his power to insure that he is not unfairly taking more profits from industry than are his due, and that the workers in the companies in which he has financial interests are being justly treated. 

7. The business organizer or entrepreneur must see to it that all the interests in industry receive their fair portion of its products. He is entitled to a just remuneration for his services as organizer, and he must insure just treatment to the worker, to the capitalist, and to the consumer.

Women workers registering at a public employment bureau. Bureaus of this kind, under government direction, could do much to help the unemployed. (See next chapter.)

Chapter VIII WORKING CONDITIONS AND WORKERS' RISKS 

67. As the majority of workers are dependent on their daily work to gain a livelihood, it is important that working conditions be favorable.

The worker's happiness naturally depends very greatly on the conditions under which he is employed. Much has already been done to remedy bad working conditions, and the International Labor Office, established in connection with the League of Nations, is striving to bring about further improvements. 

Nevertheless, we shall have to consider the chief disabilities under which the worker may still have to labor. These are: (1) the payment of inadequate wages; (2) unhygienic or dangerous factory conditions; (3) excessive hours of labor; (4) the employment of women and children at tasks unsuited to their sex, age, or strength. We shall consider each of these needs in turn. 

68. Every normally employed man should be paid a living wage; that is, a sufficient wage to maintain himself, his wife, and his children, in reasonable comfort. 

Because a man must work in order to live, and is a rational human being, possessing a human dignity, he has a strict right to an adequate wage to live as a human being. Because he is also a social being, and the majority of men are destined to live in the married state, he should receive a wage sufficient not only to maintain himself alone, but also a wife and children. It is now generally recognized that a man who has honorably done a day's labor, and who has faithfully fulfilled his duties toward his employer, is entitled to what may be termed a "family wage"; and that a woman who must work has a right to a sufficient 92

income to maintain herself away from home in an honorable and decent manner. 

An adequate living wage might be termed one which would enable the worker - and for the married man, his wife and his children also to live in reasonable comfort. By "reasonable comfort" is meant, not luxury, but a moderate attainment of the requisites of life. It means food sufficient in quality and quantity to maintain the efficiency of the individual members of the family; sufficient clothes to provide warmth and decency; adequate housing to provide a healthful, moral environment; sufficient fuel to supply warmth; sufficient light for reading purposes; sufficient funds to provide for a reasonable amount of recreation, social intercourse, medical attention, insurance against life's contingencies, a befitting education, and church membership. 

Of course the value of the work done may be much more than a living wage, for workers differ in physical strength, in skill, intelligence, and education. Some occupations are unusually hazardous; some demand exceptional intelligence and skill; some men put forth greater efforts and work more quickly than others. Moreover, wages should be fixed not only with regard to the capacity of the worker, his general welfare, and that of his family, and the danger of unemployment, but also with regard to the welfare of the nation as a whole, including the worker, the capitalist, and the consuming public. 

Not all employers give a living wage to their employees. In some states, an attempted solution to the wage problem for women and children has been made by laws which set up a fixed minimum wage for them, making it illegal for the employer to pay a smaller amount. The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 has established minimum wage legislation for workers in all industries connected with interstate commerce, with some exceptions. 

It states that minimum wages are to be: For the first year, 25 cents an hour for a maximum 44-hour week; for the second year, 30 cents for a maximum 42-hour week; for the third year, 30 cents an hour for a 40-hour week; within seven years 40 cents an hour for a 40-hour week, with certain exceptions. Overtime

must be paid at least one and one half times the normal rate. 

It is illegal to lower wages which are higher than the minimum set by the Act. The Act has been much criticized and probably will be changed.

Industrial profit-sharing and copartnership plans, whereby the employer shares with the worker a small fixed percentage of the profits, have long raised the wages of employees in the comparatively few instances where such schemes are in operation. 

The family allowance systems which have been undertaken by some private groups of employers, or made compulsory by certain foreign governments, are interesting attempts to solve the wage problem. The systems vary, but they are all based on a plan such as this: The employers in the group pay weekly into a central fund a definite fixed sum of money for each worker in their employ. Each is pledged to pay a standard wage to every employee, which is considered sufficient not only to keep the worker but also a wife and perhaps a small family. Whenever a worker has more than the specified number of dependents, for each additional dependent he receives an allowance over and above his standard wage. This additional sum is paid by the employer, but the employer reimburses himself by making a claim on the central fund for the extra amount paid out to the worker. 

69. Unhygienic or dangerous occupations, uncongenial surroundings, or environment unbecoming to modesty are inimical to the worker's health, morals, and efficiency, and should be improved.

State legislation usually now insures that working conditions are sanitary and clean; that adequate lighting is provided; that dangerous machinery is carefully protected with guards; that noxious fumes, gases, dust from grinding and polishing metals are removed as far as possible by the provision of flues and other devices; that adequate and separate toilet facilities are provided for workers of both sexes; that washing facilities and a separate room for eating are provided for those working with lead, arsenic, phosphorous, or other poisonous chemicals. We

should all promote and uphold laws of this nature, for they are most important for the health, morals, efficiency, and happiness of the worker. 

70. The worker should have adequate rest and time for religious observance. 

Man's intelligence demands that he should not be a slave, but should have time for rest and recreation, and for the fulfillment of his duties toward his Creator. A reasonably short working day reduces fatigue and hence accidents; it improves the worker's health, physique, and morale as a result of a more human existence; it increases interest in the home and family life, gives time for physical development, recreation, mental improvement, and sociability, and prevents the moral degeneration so often caused by overfatigue; it also leads to increased and better production for the less tired and the more healthy a man is, the more efficient he becomes. 

Trade unions and a certain amount of legislation gradually led to a reduction of working hours to an average of about eight a day, but the Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 (mentioned on p. 93) gave still greater benefits. It lays down the special conditions under which an employer may require overtime. It must be noted, however, that this Act concerns only industries connected with interstate commerce, and does not apply to certain types of employees. 

71. The employment of children should be especially safeguarded, and they should not be allowed to work at too early an age.

A certain amount of work around the home is good for the child, because it develops in him good habits of work at a time when habits are easily formed, and it gives him a correct idea of the dignity of labor, and of his duty to coöperate with other members of the family. Child labor, however - the employment of children away from home, or for long hours, at tasks unsuited to their age or strength is certainly detrimental to their physical health and to their mental development. Employing children when too young restricts their education, deprives them of the 

---

aid to health of care-free play, dulls their intelligence, and usually leads them to blind-alley occupations where they have no opportunity of fitting themselves for the better positions obtainable in later life by the more healthy and the better educated. When girls are employed away from home at too early an age, not only These boys are learning the use of tools. 

Both boys and girls should have some training in practical work. is their health often seriously affected, but they may receive little training in caring for a home, and may make poor wives and mothers in later life. In a word, the early employment of children deprives them to a large extent of the opportunity of becoming valuable citizens of the state. 

Child labor is due to a variety of causes: to the industrial system providing for it, to the selfishness or the poverty of parents, or to children being dissatisfied with school life and anxious to be free and to earn their own living. 

The National Child Labor Committee has done much to forward existing state legislation regarding the child worker; and the special division of the United States Children's Bureau which is concerned with investigating the working conditions of children in industry has also done invaluable work. All states now have laws compelling attendance at school until a certain age, or until a certain grade of education has been reached; specifying the

age limit below which even part-time gainful employment is prohibited; restricting the number of hours a day which children under a certain age are allowed to work; and requiring certificates testifying as to health, age, and education before a child can A group of new Americans studying the language and customs of their adopted country. secure a position. The Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 prohibits any producer, manufacturer, or dealer from shipping or delivering for shipment in interstate commerce any goods "produced in an establishment situated in the United States in or about which within thirty days prior to the removal of such goods therefrom any oppressive child labor has been employed." The Act excepts certain classes, and defines "oppressive child labor." The provision of mothers' pensions to widows with children, and the enforcement of workmen's compensation acts, to be described in paragraph 77 and 79, have alleviated the necessity for child labor to a certain extent; but the universal payment of a living wage to adult workers would greatly help to eradicate the evil. 

It is interesting to note¹ that of the 14,300,576 children in the ¹U. S. Department of Commerce, Bureau of Census, Report on Population, 1930 Census, Vol. 5, p. 345.

United States between 10 and 15 years of age, only 667,118, or 4.7 per cent, were gainfully employed at the time of the 1930 census. 

In addition, however, 587,817 young people of the age of 16 were gainfully employed, and 891,024 of the age of 17, out of a total of 4,663,140 young people between the ages of 15 and 17. 

Of the 18,963,713 children between the ages of 10 and 17, therefore, we find that 2,145,959, or 11.3 per cent, were gainfully employed. These figures are less than previous census showings.

An older sister trying to take mother's place. If the mother were not obliged to engage in industry, this meal would look more inviting than it does. 

72. Women in industry should also be specially safeguarded, and wherever possible it should be made unnecessary for married women to work. 

For health reasons, practically all the states now have special legislation for women workers, limiting the number of hours daily during which a woman may work, and prohibiting women from night work, and from mining and other dangerous occupations. 

Farm labor and domestic service, however, are generally specifically exempt from regulation-a condition which should be remedied.

Married women workers present the greatest social problem because of the effects on family life, and on the health and moral education of the children resulting from the fact that the mother

is employed in some occupation. It is almost impossible for a mother, occupied all day away from home and tired from her day's industrial work, adequately to perform her family duties, to prepare nourishing meals, to keep the home attractive, and to give due attention to her husband and to the care and supervision of her children. The result is only too often that a house is used by the entire family, not as a home, but merely as a place in which to eat and sleep.

Most married women who work do so because of the insufficiency of their husband's wages, or because for some other reason the family is wholly or in part dependent on their earnings. The necessity would be greatly diminished with the universal payment of a living wage. It would also be further curtailed if all states paid widows with children a mother's pension, and if other social insurance systems were in universal operation. 

73. Sweated labor combines bad working conditions with inadequate wages, long working hours, and child and woman labor.

Sweated labor is a disgrace to a civilized community, yet it is to be found in all states and countries. Under this system, the maximum amount of work is extracted from workers for payment which is less than a just minimum wage, and often under conditions of insanitation and discomfort. It is usually simple work, capable of being done by children or by the sick; for example, sewing buttons on cards or on garments, making artificial flowers, putting cords on pencils, stringing beads, shelling nuts, and making cigars or cigarettes, and the routine work connected with dressmaking and tailoring.

Most sweated labor is carried on in the workers' own homes, so that it is difficult for the state to enforce legislation as regards working conditions. The work is done in poverty-stricken, unhygienic homes, often by those suffering from contagious illnesses or diseases, and leads not only to the ill health of the worker, but it also endangers the health of those who buy the goods. 

Usually the class of goods produced is of a kind which could not 2See paragraph 79 for explanation of the term "mother's pension.” "The term "social insurance" explained in paragraph 75 below.

be sold other than at a low price, and the low price charged the consumer is only possible if the work involved in the manufacture is poorly paid. While it is only natural that consumers should buy goods as cheaply as they can be obtained, it is really the consumer's duty to pay a just price for his needs, and to refuse to take goods made by sweated labor. The National Consumers' 

League was organized as far back as 1890 to examine working conditions, and any articles are certified not to have been made by sweated labor if they bear the special League label. 

74. The worker is subject to many vicissitudes in life, though he can often foresee contingencies and make provision for them.

In addition to the risk of working under some of the adverse conditions just described, which can only be improved by legislation and by the employer realizing his responsibility more fully, the worker is also subject to many other risks, chief of which are: (1) an unprovided old age; (2) industrial accidents and industrial diseases; (3) nonindustrial accidents, ill health, and the cost of maternity; (4) the death, incapacity, or desertion of the breadwinner, leaving dependents unprovided for; and (5) unemployment.

The ideal to be aimed at is the payment of a living wage to all workers, to enable them to provide for these contingencies, either by saving money against "a rainy day," by insurance, or some other method. All workers, however, do not take care of future emergencies, in some cases through want of foresight, or because of thriftlessness, but usually on account of lack of resources. 

75. Social insurance is one method of providing for some of these special contingencies to which the worker is subject. 

The principle of insurance is as follows: A certain number of people are subject to a specified risk, which will not come to all of them at the same time, and to some of them may never come. 

They, therefore, pay a small amount each into a common fund. 

These small payments together provide a sufficiently large sum to meet the demands of those contributors on whom the risk

falls. By social insurance is meant group insurance for workers, to which the worker is compelled by law to contribute either the whole or a part of the cost. Usually the employer or the state pays part of the contributions. This insurance entitles the worker to a certain sum of money (or goods and services, such as medical attention and necessary drugs and surgical appliances) as compensation for losses arising from the five contingencies specified in the previous paragraph. 

Until a living wage is universally paid to the worker, it would appear that he needs the protection of social insurance, with the possible exception of old age (which might be provided for by a straight pension from the state) and industrial accident and disease (which is probably better cared for by the present employers' liability laws). 

76. Unprovided old age is a risk which many workers dread. The economic needs of the aged are now taken care of by state laws and by the Federal Social Security 

Act of 1935.

Most workers earning insufficient salaries to make provision for old age, worry as to what the future holds in store for them when they no longer will be able to earn a living. Even savings may be lost in bank failures or corporation bankruptcies. And children, if there are any, cannot be depended upon for support in later years, for they may die beforehand, or may marry and not earn sufficient to maintain themselves and their own families. 

Some employers provide pensions for their aged employees, but until recent years the majority of low-wage earners have been dependent in their old age on the charity of relatives, friends, or relief agencies; and many have had to enter almshouses or private charitable institutions.

Realizing these facts, the American Association for Labor Legislation, the American Association for Old Age Security, and other societies, have long been insisting on the state's duty to provide for the aged. For various reasons it has been considered best by many governments to cover this risk, not by social insurance but by giving pensions either to all people over a certain

age, or at least to those with low incomes: a system which has been in operation for many years in Germany, Great Britain, Denmark, France, and elsewhere. All the states of the Union have now passed old-age security laws of various kinds, the pension not being considered as charity, but as an honorable reward for a long period spent in the service of the state. The Federal scheme inaugurated by the Federal Social Security 

Act of 1935 will be outlined below. Many aged couples are welcome in their children's homes, provided they are no financial burden, and the giving of a small pension or annuity to the aged is no more costly than housing them in institutions, or providing outdoor relief. In many cases, too, workers who have a fixed sum of money to which they may look forward, are encouraged to save enough additional money to be able to stay in the surroundings to which they have been accustomed. Where it may be

National Safety Council OPERATORS TAKE NOTICE KEEP SAFETY GUARDS IN PLACE WARNEX 

Some of the safety devices used in industrial plants. This picture shows the saw guard on a hand-fed ripsaw; also the guarded electric light and the safety warning. necessary for an old person to be cared for in an institution, the pension or annuity will enable him to be self-supporting and not to feel that he is being given charity. 

The Federal Social Security Act of 1935 contained two provisions for the aged. First, an annuities system was established, by which equal contributions by employers and employed are to be made to a central Old Age Reserve Account for all workers other than agricultural workers, domestic servants, casual work-

ers, ship crews, government employees, and employees in nonprofit-making institutions. These joint contributions, beginning at one per cent of yearly earnings in 1937, will be increased by one-half of one per cent every three years, until from 1949 onwards both employer and employee will pay three per cent per annum into the fund. Contributions must be paid for every employee, regardless of his total income, but on salaries over $3,000 only the first $3,000 is taxable. Beginning in 1942, an annuity of from $10 to $85 per month will be paid to everyone over the age of sixty-five years who ceases work at that time, regardless of his income or property holdings. The amount paid depends upon the total income and contributions made from the time the system came into effect until he reached the age of sixty-five. It is seen that only certain classes of workers are eligible for this old age security help; that the annuities paid are a tax upon worker and employer alone, so that the wealthy do not contribute anything from the general taxation funds of the government; and that for many years, until sufficient reserves are built up, the younger people of the country and their employers, paying current old age security taxes, will bear a large part of the expense of the annuities to older people who in the meantime reach the age of sixty-five.

The second part of the Federal Social Security Act, relating to the aged, made provision for financial assistance to states who have passed or who will pass laws to care for the dependent aged. Federal assistance is limited to half the amount of the pension received by a dependent aged person from the state, and is not to exceed $15 per person per month. State pension schemes, however, must conform to certain requirements to be eligible for this Federal aid, but undoubtedly many of the states will revise their existing laws or inaugurate schemes which will make them eligible for the Federal grant. Several states have, indeed, already done this. 

77. Industrial accidents and industrial diseases are risks to which all workers are subject. 

Industrial accidents and diseases are those injuries and diseases

suffered by the worker as a direct result of his industrial employment, resulting in his death or in permanent or temporary incapacity for work. The increased use of machinery and largescale production has led to a yearly increase in the number of workers incapacitated in this manner. Since, therefore, industry is responsible for such contingencies, it has generally been considered fair that the employer should bear the loss, and that the risk should not be covered by social insurance. When the employer is made responsible, he will not only take precautions to remove unnecessary dangers, but he will instruct the worker in the right use of machinery, and will often be farsighted enough to realize that fewer working hours, higher wages, and good working conditions produce a less fatigued and a less harassed worker, and therefore one less likely to meet with accidents. 

Since employers have not always sufficient funds to meet their workmen's compensation liabilities, the state usually safeguards the workers by making it compulsory for employers to insure against this risk. All the states with the exception of Arkansas, Mississippi, Florida, and South Carolina have compensation legislation for accidents, but not more than about twelve of the states insist on compulsory insurance of employers' liability for occupational diseases. The worker is thus much better provided for in some states than in others, and there is a great need of uniformity of legislation. 

78. Workers and their families run the risk of incurring losses and expenses due to nonindustrial accidents, ill health, and maternity.

Not all invalidism is due to industrial causes. The worker may become ill, or have an accident outside working hours; his wife and children may need medical care; and the coming of each additional child in the family involves a considerable outlay of money.

Free hospitals and clinics cannot always cope with the demand; sometimes they are inconveniently situated for the worker or his family; and there is, too, the stigma of pauperism attaching to attendance at these places. Public health education

and the health care of school children do much toward the cure and prevention of sickness, but do not embrace all the needs of the worker's family. In many localities "visiting nurses" visit the sick in their own homes, but they do not provide financial relief. Usually the benefit features of certain trade unions and fraternal societies take care only of skilled workers and tradesmen who would have been able to care for their needs in other ways. Some employers pay the medical expenses of their workers, but they rarely provide for the medical needs of their employees' families. Certain hospitals have inaugurated private group insurance with some measure of success. Until the worker gets a living wage, therefore, there is reason for social insurance to meet his needs. Of all the industrial nations, the United States is the only country which thus far has made no provision at all for social health insurance of any kind. The Social Security Act of 

1935, however, authorized the Federal Government to spend $10,000,000 a year for preventive public health work, including grants for the care of crippled children, the blind, and others with physical handicaps. Many of the States, of course, have set aside special funds for these health needs. 79. In a working-class family the death, incapacity, or desertion of the breadwinner usually reduces the wife and children to destitution. On account of the importance of the child as a future citizen, special provision should be made by the state.

Through poverty a mother may be unable to look after her children, if she is the widow of a man who died without savings, or who was not privately insured for a sufficiently large sum; if she is the wife of a man who finds it impossible through sickness or some other cause to provide for her and her children; or if she is deserted by her husband. She may be able to secure work, but she will rarely earn a sufficient wage to enable her to keep her home together and pay someone to look after the children during her working hours. Relatives may take care of her children, or otherwise help her; or there may be a day nursery in the vicinity, but these aids are not always available or adequate for all needs.

In the United States, widows with children are now given pensions, called "mothers' aid," in all states except Alabama, Georgia, New Mexico, and South Carolina. Mothers who have been deserted or divorced from the husband, or whose husbands are in prison, or are mentally or physically incapacitated, are also helped in this way in most states. Many people believe that mothers' pensions in nearly all cases could be supplemented more appropriately by social insurance. Nevertheless, this form of aid is not charity. The mother who brings up her child to be a good future citizen is performing a service for the country, which an institution could but imperfectly supply. Only where the mother is deficient in some respect should public money be spent in bringing up the child away from her." 

80. Unemployment, that is, to be unable to obtain work suited to his capacity, is the greatest and most disastrous risk to which the worker, under the present economic system, is exposed.

Unemployment, and the discharge of men from industry in early middle age, which so frequently happens nowadays, seriously affect not only the individual worker, but also his family and the nation. The lack of nourishing food and worry often impair the health of all the members of the family. When the mother or the younger children find employment in an effort to provide for the family's needs, there are the bad social effects of a mother working outside the home and of child labor. The longer the period of unemployment, the greater will be the worker's loss of skill and efficiency, due to lack of practice, impaired physical strength, and the physical effects of worry and discouragement. A man who is out of work for any length of time develops a sense of failure; he becomes listless, loses his selfconfidence, initiative, and sense of responsibility, and often degenerates in the ranks of the unemployed to the state of being unemployable. Particularly are these effects of unemployment seen in the young. Indeed, without religious convictions, supernatural faith and hope it is not to be wondered at that many of the unemployed reach such a state of despair and social demoralization that they become bitter and rebellious against law and order,

and develop a hatred of the wealthy and the employing classes. 

81. Unemployment may be due to natural causes, to personal deficiencies, to social causes, or to such industrial causes as seasonal work, the recurrence of the trade cycle, or technological improvements. 

The causes of unemployment vary, of course, with each individual case. Natural causes, such as fires, earthquakes, crop failures, floods, and other disasters provide the least problem, because they are accidental and do not arise from a defective educational or industrial system. Relief measures are usually the only remedy for the unemployment which these contingencies occasion, although man's ingenuity can at times foresee and prevent them. 

Sometimes the causes of unemployment are personal, that is, a man may be unemployed on account of lack of education, or because of inadequate preparation for industry, physical disabilities, illness, or accidents, or such moral deficiencies as laziness, untruthfulness, or inability to mix well with other workers. 

Bad social conditions or systems, such as the lack of a spirit of brotherhood and social coöperation in the world, the maldistribution of wealth, defective public education, sweated labor, and bad housing also cause unemployment.

Industry, however, is responsible for the present widespread unemployment evil. The chief form of industrial unemployment are three seasonal, cyclical, and technological. 

Seasonal unemployment: Men in trades or professions depend ing on the season or on the weather are unemployed for certain periods of the year; for example, those engaged in the building or painting trade, the summer tourist business, fruit or vegetable picking and canning, the manufacture of wholesale clothing, or the making of novelties and favors used only about Thanksgiving and Christmas.

Cyclical unemployment: Up to the present time, business has moved in cycles. In a period of prosperity, manufacturers overstock their goods, and eventually they are forced to employ less men and to reduce prices until the surplus is disposed of. To meet the competition, newly manufactured goods must be sold at lower prices also, and wages are reduced to enable a profit

to be made. The greatest number of the world's consumers are the workers themselves; and those who receive reduced wages, or who are unemployed, purchase less goods. In an endeavor to induce consumers to buy, prices are still further reduced, and wages again lowered, until a time arrives when prices are so low that those with money on hand undertake extensive alterations and repairs to their buildings, have new buildings erected, and buy goods in anticipation of future needs. These business activities lead to increased employment and make prices rise somewhat. The reëmployed workers purchase more goods than when they were unemployed, and others likewise make more purchases, fearing further advances in price. These additional purchases again lead to higher prices, and more employment, and thus the upward movement of what is known as the "trade cycle" begins, and a boom period is once more in the making, inevitably to be followed later by another depression time. 

Technological unemployment is that due to the great advances which have been made in technology, that is, in the invention of labor-saving devices, so that one man, with the aid of a machine, can now do the work formerly performed by many workers. 

82. Social insurance against unemployment was provided in the United States by the Federal Social Security Act of 1935. Systematic planning for more regular employment is the only possible way to remedy the unemployment evil. 

Since a certain amount of unemployment will always be inevitable, every worker should be insured against this risk; until he receives a living wage, this should be done by social insurance. 

Wisconsin set up a state social insurance scheme for unemployment in 1932, and in 1935 the Federal Social Security Act was passed. This Social Security Act provides that all employers of eight or more persons are to pay a tax of 1 per cent of their payrolls in 1936, 2 per cent in 1937, and 3 per cent thereafter. 

Workers employed in agriculture, in domestic service, as seamen, and by the government, by relatives, or by religious, charitable and educational institutions, are exempt. Since the government wishes to promote state provision for unemployment, the act has made no provision for the Federal distribution of funds.

Instead, states are urged to provide for a certain type of unemployment insurance, to be financed by a pay-roll tax equal or greater than the Federal tax. If this is done, they may be credited with 90 per cent of the Federal pay-roll tax for their own scheme. Moreover, the Federal Government will provide funds for the administration of the state unemployment system. 

Benefits are not to be paid to workers for a period of two years. 

They vary in the different states from $5 to about $15 weekly. 

Although Congress is authorized to appropriate annual financial subsidies for the management of the program, through the Social Security Board, obviously most of the expenses of the scheme come from the direct tax on employers, and there is much discussion as to whether this additional tax will not hamper industry, and eventually lower wages, and lead to further economic distress. Whatever the merits or demerits of this particular scheme, however, it seems that until the widespread insecurity of employment is overcome and until all workers receive a living wage, some form of unemployment insurance is essential to national well being. All of the states of the union have now passed unemployment insurance acts, which provide for a 2.7 per cent pay-roll tax plus a 0.3 per cent federal tax, a total of 3 per cent, which equals the Federal tax which would be imposed in the absence of a state law. 

In addition to provisions for social insurance, the Federal Government passed the Wagner-Peyser Act in 1933, which inaugurated a nation-wide scheme for the conduct of state employment bureaus, connected with the United States Employment Service. Many such bureaus were established during the 

World War, but because of lack of Federal appropriations they were not very useful from 1919 onwards. If all the unemployed will register at these bureaus, and if all employers likewise register vacant jobs there, those needing work will have a very much greater chance of hearing about all available employment than before.

To help provide work during periods of unemployment, public bodies should likewise defer until slack seasons, or periods of depression, such undertakings as the improvement and construc-

tion of roads and public buildings, slum clearance, reforestation, reclamation of desert lands, irrigation schemes, and the construction of parks and recreation grounds.

Personal and social causes of unemployment would be very largely eliminated by a more widespread introduction of industrial education into the school system, an increase in vocational guidance, the complete abolition of child labor, improved housing and working conditions, proper home and school training in early life, and a more universal adherence to Christian principles of morality.

Seasonal and cyclical unemployment would be greatly reduced by forecasting and preparing for rush periods in advance; by research as to further uses to which a product can be put; by the deferment of all construction and repair work to slack periods; by stimulating demand in off seasons by increasing advertising, or by special discounts for purchases made in those times. For example, a large food-packing company which formerly prepared only bacon for market, now handles a variety of products, such as biscuits, candy, and other canned goods, and provides year-round work for its employees. A motor-car manufacturing firm also makes domestic food coolers and cash registers, and transfers its employees from one branch of its business to another, according as demand warrants. A New England concern, which originally manufactured jewelry boxes only, now makes all types of paper goods. 

As regards improvements in technology: The financial benefits of these inventions should not go wholly to the capital owners in the form of dividends, nor wholly to the consumers by way of reduced prices. The workers should share in the benefit by reduced working hours, without reduced pay; and if work similar to his occupation is not available elsewhere, every man who is supplanted by machinery should receive special vocational training for a new line of employment, or he might be paid a lump sum of money to enable him to have sufficient finances to adjust himself to a necessary change. A complete reorganization of occupational society, however, seems the only permanent way out of the present chaos in the industrial system.

Chapter IX TRADE UNIONISM AND COÖPERATION 

83. The fact that the employer controls three of the four factors of production gives him a semi-monopolistic power over labor.

Ever since the industrial revolution, men have tended to congregate in cities, near the centers of industry, so that as we saw in Chapter VII, even in the United States with its vast tracts of open country, 56.6 per cent of the total population in 1940 was urban. Over two fifths of these town-dwellers lived in cities of more than 250,000 inhabitants. This percentage is higher in most other industrial countries, and it means that, although the majority of men are dependent upon their labor for the maintenance of themselves and their families, nevertheless only a very
