small proportion are able to grow their own food. Of the remainder, comparatively few are employers of labor, own their own implements of production, or work directly for the consumer, as do professional men, small artisans, and storekeepers. The general situation is one in which either the employer personally owns the land, buildings, money, machinery, office appliances, and all the intricate organization necessary for the conduct of modern industry; or he manages these factors of production for the stockholders, and is in almost as strong a position as if he himself were the owner. If he fulfilled his moral duties toward his employees, an obligation seldom realized although it is strictly binding in conscience, and if he had a high regard for human dignity, this quasi-monopolistic position would in no way adversely affect the worker's living conditions. Few employers, however, carry out their moral duties, pay their workers a living wage, or give them good working conditions, without some outside compulsion. The vast improvement in the worker's status since the industrial revolution, although he is still in a position very far from ideal, is due, as we mentioned in Chapter VII, partly to independent legislation, such as we outlined in the discussion of working conditions; partly to the foresight of employers who realized the advantages of providing a humane existence for their employees; partly to the competition between employers for good workers; and very largely to the action of the workers themselves through the medium of trade unions. 

LAND ORGANIZATION LABOR CAPITAL 

84. By forming trade unions, workers can turn their labor into a monopoly almost equally as powerful as that of the employer. 

Despite the employer's monopoly of land, capital, and organization, as three important factors of production, essential factors of proand despite the weakness of the workers' position, due to their need of earning a livelihood, the workers themselves provide the fourth necessary factor of 

Land, labor, capital, and the four organization: duction in modern times.

TRADE UNIONISM AND COÖPERATION 

113 production - labor. Alone, their bargaining power may be much weaker than that of the employer, but since their labor is necessary for him to make use of the factors he controls, they can attain an almost equally strong bargaining position by organizing trade unions, and thus turning the provision of their skill into a monopoly. Of the 48,829,920 people gainfully employed in the United States, as reported in the census of 1930, the American Federation of Labor and the Committee for Industrial Organization are each said to include some four million members, and the Industrial Workers of the World claim about 130,000. 

Two important independent unions at the present time are the 

Big Four Brotherhood of the railroads and the International

Ladies' Garment Workers. The American Federation of Labor 

(A. F. of L.) is a federation of trade and industrial unions, founded in 1881. It is based upon voluntary coöperation of the individual members belonging to it, and the rights of constituent units are preserved intact. In 1935 a number of the union delegates at the annual meeting of the A. F. of L. criticized the fact that the federation was not furthering the unionization of such massproduction industries as steel, rubber, automobile manufacturing, so that its bargaining strength was declining with the increased mechanization of industry and the decreased employment of skilled craftsmen. A proposed program to enlarge the policy of the A. F. of L. and promote industrial unions in the United 

States was defeated. In the same year, however, John L. Lewis, president of the United Mine Workers Union and seven other industrial union presidents formed the Congress for Industrial Organization (C.I.O.) to hasten the unionization of the unorganized workers in mass production and other industries upon an industrial basis. In 1936, the A. F. of L. suspended from membership those unions which had joined the C.I.O. while still maintaining membership within the A. F. of L. ranks, and since that time the C.I.O. has become an independent federation of industrial unions. Industrial Workers of the World (I.W.W.), founded in 1905, is a loosely bound group of any workers who subscribe to its revolutionary ideas and apply for membership.

85. A trade union is an occupational group formed by workers for the maintenance of working conditions. 

A trade union may be described as an association of workmen who band together to maintain or improve their working conditions. It may comprise workers engaged in the same trade or occupation, such as an association of bricklayers, or of bakers, or printers; or it may be composed of all workers engaged in the same industry, for example, a trade union of builders, miners, railroad employees, or those who are in the clothing industry. 

Each member of the trade union pays a small fee annually, in weekly or monthly installments, to provide for necessary expenses, for payment to the workers to enable them to subsist during a strike, and for the salaries of representatives called "business agents." Some trade unions also have mutual insurance associations, and have set up an education fund for members. 

The union conducts its negotiations through the business agents. These are specialized representatives who can usually deal more competently with employers than could individual workers, for they generally have an expert knowledge of the labor market, and of wages and conditions within the industry in all parts of the country. Also, since they are employed by the trade union, they are independent of the employers with whom they negotiate, and so they can state the workers' case fearlessly, without the risk of losing their own employment. 

The Wagner National Labor Relations Act of 1935, designed to take the place of the defeated N.R.A. legislation in protecting the rights of organized labor, specifically states in Section 7 that "Employees shall have the right to self-organization, to form, join, or assist labor organizations, to bargain collectively through representatives of their own choosing, and to engage in concerted activities, for the purpose of mutual aid or protection." Section 8 of the same Act declares that employers must not, among other practices, interfere with, restrain or coerce employees in the exercise of the rights guaranteed in Section 7; discriminate in regard to the hire of union members, or make terms or conditions of employment to encourage or discourage

TRADE UNIONISM AND COOPERATION 

115 membership in any national labor organization; refuse to bargain. collectively with the representatives of employees. This Act was, of course, a distinct victory for labor. 

86. Disputes between the employer and a trade union are best settled by amicable agreement or by arbitration. 

It is the business agent's duty not only to make terms with employers on behalf of the trade unionists he represents, but also to discuss with them any disputes which may have arisen regarding the treatment of individual members, working conditions, or wages. If the dispute is serious, and neither the employer nor the workers will come to a compromise or accede to the demands of the opposite side, the best solution of the difficulty is for both parties to have recourse to arbitration. Arbitration means an agreement by both parties to submit their case to an independent judge, called an arbitrator, who will weigh the merits of the respective arguments and pass an impartial judgment by which both parties will agree to abide. The Department of Labor has a special Division of Conciliation, and the Secretary of Labor is authorized to act as mediator or to appoint commissioners of conciliation whenever in his judgment the interests of industrial peace demanded it. Among other provisions, the Wagner National 

Labor Relations Act of 1935 established the National Labor 

Relations Board, composed of three members to be "appointed by the President, by and with the advice of the Senate." The purpose of the Act was to make the bargaining power of the worker more nearly equal to the employer, and to protect the worker from "unfair labor policies." The duty of the Board is to watch over the operation of the Act, and if any unfair labor practices as set forth in the Act are perpetrated, in interstate industries, then it is the duty of the Board to set a time for the compulsory hearing of evidence on both sides. The decision of the Board is binding upon both workers and employers. 

Special boards were created by the Railroad Labor Disputes Act of 1934 to settle railroad disputes. Several states have provided for permanent state boards of conciliation and arbitration for industrial disputes. These facilities, however,

merely provide the machinery for the settlement of disputes by arbitration, for neither party to a dispute is obliged to submit the case to these federal or state arbitrators, or to accept their decisions unwillingly. 

87. The chief weapon of the trade union is the strike, and in certain circumstances a strike may be both just and necessary.

Just as the employer has the weapon of discharge to use under legitimate circumstances, so the worker, under certain conditions, has the right to refuse to work and to declare a strike. 

A strike is a simultaneous and concerted cessation of work by a group of wage earners, to enforce a demand upon an employer, the strikers taking measures to retain the positions they have temporarily vacated. Section 13 of the Wagner National Labor 

Relations Act, after enumerating the procedure to be taken to prevent unfair labor practices, specifically states that nothing in the Act is to be "construed so as to interfere with or impede or diminish in any way the right to strike." 

Since a strike may lead to very grave consequences to the worker himself, to his family, to the general public, and to the employer, workers may justly make use of their monopolistic power in this manner only on approximately the same conditions as regulate a just war: 

1. The strike must have been called on account of a just grievance, and there must be no malicious intent. 

2. Peaceful means of settling the grievance by arbitration, and all other possible means, must have failed. 

3. It must not involve violence or destruction of property. 

4. There must be a reasonable hope of success or at least a hope of ultimate advantage to the worker. 

5. The benefits resulting from it must exceed the damage caused, both to the employer and to the workers. 

88. The lockout is the employer's chief weapon against trade unionism.

A lockout takes place when an employer refuses to continue in his employ laborers who will not work on his conditions. As the strike is often imperative to obtain justice from an employer,

TRADE UNIONISM AND COOPERATION 

117 so a lockout is sometimes necessary to force employees to come to reasonable terms. A just lockout has the same characteristics as a just strike. 

89. Collective bargaining by means of trade unionism has many advantages. 

1. As we have just explained, workers, whose individual bargaining power is inherently weaker than that of the rich employer, are effectively enabled to meet the employer on more equal ground, and are thus protected from unjust treatment. 

2. The workers have the advantage of the business agent's ability to drive a better bargain than would have been possible individually. 

3. All employers must pay trade unionists the same wage, and give them approximately the same working conditions, at least as to hours of labor. The just employer is thus protected from the competition of those who, without the compulsion of trade unionism, would give their workers lower wages, and make them work longer hours, and thus be able to sell their manufactured goods cheaper. 

4. Trade unionism, by eliminating competition among workers as regards their wages, makes skill and efficiency become the determining factors in a man's employment, to the advantage of the employer. 

5. A trade union pledges itself to keep all agreements made with the employer on behalf of its members, which is another advantage to the employer. 

6. Trade unionism furnishes the machinery through which the employer and the employee may cultivate friendly relations and obtain a clearer insight into their individual problems. 

90. There are also some disadvantages to trade unionism. 

These, however, are outweighed by the advantages. 

Trade unions were at first declared illegal, but although the workers' need of mutual protection has now been recognized by law for many years, nevertheless trade unions, as they now exist, have not been an unmixed blessing. The chief disadvantages of trade unionism have been as follows: 

1. It has often caused increased hostility between employer

and employed, instead of working toward that coöperation which is necessary for increased production. 

2. In certain instances it has led to a labor monopoly equally as obnoxious as any monopoly of capital, and has resulted in unjust and unreasonable demands by workers from employers, and in disastrous strikes. 

3. There is a tendency for trade unionism to benefit skilled workers rather than the unskilled and poorly paid, who are in greater need of protection. 

4. The interests of the consumer and the general public are often disregarded when unionists are seeking to enforce their claims. 

5. It has sometimes led to "racketeering," where a man has been able to get control over a union, and has dictated his terms to both employer and worker, getting large sums of money from both parties; from the one, by threatening a strike if payments are not forthcoming, and from the other by refusing permission to belong to the union without large fees for the privilege. 

6. Some trade unions have abused their semi-monopolistic power to restrict the output of workers. Others have charged excessive entrance fees to members, so that many men were unable to join. Others, again, have demanded equal wages for all men in the union, without sufficient attention to individual differences in efficiency and skill. And some unions have limited the number of apprentices received, without just cause, to create an artificial shortage of labor in a particular occupation. The rise of "jurisdictional disputes" has also been serious, where work has come to a complete standstill while two unions of men doing similar yet different work have disputes as to which of the two should allow its members to perform a certain operation. 

Special legislation has, on occasion, been necessary to curtail the power of the unions, but despite these adverse aspects, the advantages of trade unionism appear to outweigh the disadvantages. In the next chapter we shall show that the universal organization of workers into trade unions on the one side, and of employers into employers' associations, the two to be bound

TRADE UNIONISM AND COOPERATION 

119 together by the mutual formation of a vocational group, might possibly provide the machinery for the more efficient conduct of occupational society. For such a plan to become operative, however, trade unionism must not be confined to the portion of those workers who are in skilled occupations, but must be extended to all those engaged in industry and working for a wage, unskilled laborers as well as skilled, and also to office workers. 

The workers, too, must have a greater realization of their duty to serve their employers efficiently and well, to exercise charity and justice in their dealings with their fellow employees, and to consider the rights of the consumer. 

91. Consumers' coöperation was initiated by a group of workers, and of the three forms of coöperation has done the most to improve the worker's condition. 

In addition to the benefits conferred on them by trade unionism, certain workers have also benefited by the three systems of mutual benefit, known technically as consumers' or distributors' coöperation; credit or bankers' coöperation; and producers' coöperation.

Consumers' coöperation means an association of consumers who seek to eliminate the middleman and his profits by arranging to supply themselves with goods instead of buying their needs from private retailers.

This type of coöperation originated with a small group of weavers in Rochdale, England, in 1844, who called themselves the Rochdale Pioneers. Coöperative stores on the Rochdale plan are now to be found throughout the British Isles, and the movement is spreading in the United States, although thus far it has not been so successful here as in Great Britain. 

Consumers' coöperative societies are usually operated in a locality in this way: Anyone who wishes to become a member may do so upon taking up one share in the enterprise at a cost of about $5, payable, if necessary, in installments of approximately 25 cents. Further capital investment is permitted, if desired, up to a maximum which is usually fixed at $1,000 to prevent the danger of possible future capitalistic control. Interest

is paid on capital shares at the same rate as that on deposits in savings accounts at the local banks, but the organization is strictly democratic, for each member has but one vote, regardless of his capital investment. The organization has conveniently located stores run by hired employees, under the direction of managers who are appointed by vote. Members buy goods at these stores at current market prices. Profits are computed quarterly, after a deduction of all expenses, such as salaries to managers and employees, rental charges, interest on capital shares, and cost of goods sold. A percentage of these profits is placed on reserve for depreciation and unforeseen contingencies, or for the development of the business, and the balance is divided among the members, not in proportion to capital holdings, but in proportion to the amount of goods purchased during the quarter. 

The money which members receive back in this manner is called "savings returns" or "dividends," and usually amounts to about 10 per cent of the money spent in purchases during the quarter. 

It has been objected that consumers' coöperation eliminates the small tradesman. This is true, to some extent, in poorer districts, although even here many of the poor cannot take advantage of coöperation because they rely on their credit at the local stores, and all the business of the coöperative organization is done on a strictly cash basis. In wealthier districts, however, people usually prefer the greater choice of goods, and the greater attention given them in privately owned concerns. And in any event the chain store has, in many cases, taken the place of the small tradesman.

Coöperative stores are a great boon to the working classes. 

Members reduce their cost of living very considerably, because by coöperating in this manner with other consumers, they can take advantage of large-scale buying and obtain the usual profits of the middleman for themselves. Habits of thrift are also promoted, for not only must members pay for their purchases in cash, but they automatically accumulate savings in the form of "savings returns" on purchases, which, being paid them quarterly, are often invested. Coöperative stores can also successfully compete

TRADE UNIONISM AND COÖPERATION 

121 against the quasi-monopolies held in some places by the capitalistic chain stores. 

92. Credit coöperation is more widespread in the United 

States, and also renders material assistance to the worker. 

Credit coöperation is that whereby individuals who have small amounts of capital to save, pool their resources and eliminate the profits of the banker or money lender by lending to members in need of ready funds. It originated in Germany, and the first credit coöperative society in the United States was founded in St. Mary's 

Catholic parish in Manchester, New Hampshire, in 1909. 

Credit unions, as these societies are usually called, restrict their membership to persons of good character, who have a community of interests. For example, a credit union may be formed by employees of a particular factory, by the postal employees of a certain city, or by the members of a parish. The Credit Union 

National Extension Bureau of Boston is always ready to assist groups to establish a union, free of charge, and the promotion of credit unions in Catholic parishes is being particularly advocated by the National Catholic Welfare Conference, whose Social Action Department has now organized a Parish Credit Union National Committee, to whom application may be made for free information and advice.

Every member of the credit union pledges to save a certain amount each week, however small, a method which encourages thrift and provides an easy, safe, and convenient system of saving small sums of money. Interest is paid annually on these savings at the usual rate given on deposits in savings accounts at the local bank.

To provide the money necessary to allot this interest, the credit union lends out these savings deposits to members who are in need of a loan, at a rate of interest which is slightly higher than that given as interest on savings, but which is, nevertheless, much lower than would be charged by banks. The difference between the two rates provides a reserve to cover bad debts, but any surplus profits that may be made are divided among members either in proportion to their borrowings or, as is more usual, in

proportion to their capital savings. Bad debts are very rare, however; first because loans are only made to members, and no member is admitted who is not of good character; second, collateral is required wherever possible, although loans are also made on character and needs; third, loans are made only for constructive or productive purposes, for example, to enable a man to buy a tool which will help him make larger profits in his business; to provide a washing machine for a poor mother who could then earn a living by doing laundry work; to give a man a holiday, necessary to maintain his earning capacity; to purchase an outfit of clothing for a girl or boy just leaving school and needing to present a neater appearance in order to obtain employment.

As just stated, loans are made only to members, but any member, no matter how small his capital investment, may obtain a necessary loan. This enables a person who lacks the financial standing necessary to secure a bank loan, to obtain credit he needs; it makes it possible to secure loans which might be too small to be handled by the local bank; it prevents a man in need of money from entering into the clutches of the loan shark or money lender, which is too often the fate of the poor; and it maintains the independence and self-respect of members who, without the credit facilities thus provided, might be forced to resort to charity. 

93. Producers' coöperation is another form of coöperation of benefit to the worker, but is not capable of such universal adoption as consumers' and credit coöperation.

By producers' coöperation is meant a voluntary association of workers who seek to eliminate the employer, and to provide both capital, labor, and management for their enterprises. Usually the workers appoint a committee of management. For remuneration they either depend solely upon a division of profits, or each worker is paid an agreed wage, and then receives also his share of any surplus.

There are several important disadvantages in producers' coöperation on account of which the plan probably never will

TRADE UNIONISM AND COÖPERATION 

123 become very widespread nor benefit many workers. These are: 

1. Workers cannot normally provide the large outlay of capital necessary in large-scale production. 

2. They cannot afford to run the risk of losing their capital during depression times, whereas a similar loss in a normal capitalistic enterprise would be spread over many stockholders. 

3. Workers usually dislike taking necessary orders from foremen and others in command, and frequently they cannot find men in their ranks to fill these offices, and are forced to hire experts to organize, buy, and market, and then they often resent the payment of higher wages to those who fill these offices. 

Jealousies of this nature can only lead to failure. 

4. In times of prosperity more capital is needed, yet if more workers are taken into partnership, profits have to be divided among a greater number, which may mean very little money for each individual partner if a depression follows. The tendency, therefore, is to hire workmen under ordinary conditions of wage payment during prosperous periods, and under these circumstances true coöperation in the technical sense is no longer present, and the enterprise becomes a form of capitalistic partnership. 1 Producers' coöperation would appear to be most successful in the farming industry. It enables the small farmer to buy seed and fertilizers in wholesale quantities, and to share the expenses of labor-saving machinery and other equipment, such as tractors, motor, trucks for taking produce into market, grain elevators, and up-to-date dairies. Here, however, the farm laborers themselves do not usually participate in the profits, but remain as hired workers, and it is the small capitalist who benefits. Nevertheless, the small farmer is thus able to benefit, and to withstand competition from large-scale farming methods, and an increase in this form of coöperation would seem highly desirable.

Chapter X  

94. A reorganization of occupational society is urgently needed.

Despite the improvement of the worker's status, due to legislation, trade unionism, "scientific management," and various forms of coöperation, the world's organization for work or, as we have called it, occupational society, is so defective that it is a matter of much discussion and concern the world over. That millions should be out of work in the world, with little hope of reëmployment; and that many thousands should have lost the savings of a lifetime in bank failures, stock-exchange crashes, corporation bankruptcies, or adverse foreign exchanges, are conditions which civilized peoples cannot tolerate without at least striving to provide a remedy. Proposals to remedy the evil have been too numerous to outline here, but two in particular merit our earnest attention, and we shall refer to them very briefly in this chapter. The first comprises the doctrines of socialists and communists: theories which have led to many grave abuses in Russia and elsewhere. The second is the scheme which 

His Holiness, Pope Pius XI, outlined in the encyclical Quadra gesimo Anno: On the Reconstruction of the Social Order. 

95. Wealth or property in the economic sense not only has value in use but also value in exchange; it may take the form either of consumption goods, called "consumptive property," or of capital goods, that is, productive property. 

For a right understanding of the socialistic arguments, we must first understand the exact distinction between the various classes of wealth. Wealth is any thing or service which has value in use; it may be tangible, for example, bread, money, land, cattle; or intangible, such as the services of a doctor, a lawyer, a servant : 124

titles and claims to copyrights and trade-marks; moral goods, such as the love and affection of a friend.

Although all goods have value in use, wealth or property in the economic sense consists only of economic goods. Economic goods are those services and material goods which in addition to 

ALL GOODS HAVE VALUE IN USE SOME GOODS HAVE VALUE IN EXCHANGE 

(E.G.) 

FOOO CLOTHING SHELTER TEACHERS SERVICES LETTER OF FAMOUS MAN OTHER GOODS HAVE NO EXCHANGE VALUE BECAUSE THEY HAVE NO SCARCITY VALUE (E.G.AIR, SUNLIGHT, FRIEND'S LETTER). 

IF USED FOR DIRECT ENJOYMENT CONSUMPTION GOODS (E.G. HOUSE WE LIVE IN, CLOTHES WE WEAR, COAL BURNED IN FURNACE AT HOME). 

IF USED ONLY INDI- RECTLY-TO PRODUCE OTHER GOOOS PRODUCTION GOODS (CAPITAL GOODS) (EG. COAL USEO IN A FACTORY, FARM MACHINERY, POWER USEO IN FACTORY). 

All goods have value in use. Some have value also in exchange; that is, they can be bought and sold at a price. possessing value in use are also marketable, that is, have value in exchange. The value in exchange of a good is the amount of goods which others will give us in exchange for it. This exchange is usually expressed in a common denominator of money. The words economic wealth, therefore, exclude moral goods, and all those goods which are free and common to all, such as the air and the sea; they exclude also such things as a photograph, which may be of great value to the owner, but of no service to anyone else.

The economic goods which a man has for his direct use and enjoyment, whether these be necessities, comforts, or luxuries, are called consumption goods or consumptive property. Such are, for example, the house in which one lives, the clothes one wears, the books one buys to read, the furniture one uses, and one's food and adornment.

Man, however, does not make direct use of all the goods he produces. Some he employs indirectly, as it were, in the production of other goods, or he may exhange them into money with which to buy the means of production, or to provide wages for workers, or loans to those needing funds to carry on some form of economic activity. Goods which are used for the production of other goods, are called capital goods or productive property. 

They are the product of past industry and the result of saving. 

Money which is used to earn an income by investment in industry, for the purchase of capital goods is called capital, and no goods can be produced under modern conditions of perfection and quantity output without capital not only to provide material goods in the form of buildings, machinery, and other equipment, but also to pay for workmen, executives, advertising, and the numberless services which production now demands. Even in agriculture, one cannot raise grain without seed and farming implements. And so it is with all other production. Capital goods are essential for the production of other goods, and they are one of the four factors of production, performing a function equally as necessary as that of the other three factors-land, labor, and organization. 

96. There are many proofs that man has a natural right both to productive and consumptive property. 

1. There is God's sanction for private property in the two commandments which presuppose its existence: "Thou shalt not steal"; "Thou shalt not covet thy neighbor's house nor his field . . . nor anything that is his." 

2. Because man has a right to life, God must have intended him to obtain the goods necessary for his sustenance, and to store up goods in the form of productive property to make other goods.

3. Man is by nature a social being, and the foremost social relationship into which he enters is the family. He was given by God the blessing to "increase and multiply," and he has therefore a right to marry and to raise a family. Fittingly to provide for his family, in case he should be unable to work for their sustenance, he must be able to transmit property to his wife and children by inheritance. 

4. Man is subject to many vicissitudes in life. He has many contingencies to meet, such as accidents, ill health, old age, death, and the leaving of dependents. These cannot be provided for unless a man saves up goods for the future. To economize for "a rainy day," however, he must have the right to amass not merely consumptive property, but also productive. If he had not this right and he suffered misfortune, he and his dependents would become wholly dependent on the will and charity of others. 

5. Man is endowed with intelligence, and unlike the animal he does not live merely for the present. He has the right, therefore, to use his intelligence to foresee the contingencies just mentioned and to provide for them. He has the right to use his intelligence to provide himself not only with consumptive but also with productive property which is not consumed in the using. And since intelligence and other immaterial goods of heart and mind are perfectible, he must also have the right to own property to enable himself or his dependents to spend what time is necessary to perfect these gifts by study, travel, and in other ways. This is impossible without a store of goods with which to maintain life while devoting one's attention to pursuits other than the gaining of a livelihood. Man's nature demands this improvement of his higher faculties. 

6. In exercising his social nature man must live in harmony with his fellow men. Yet by nature man wants to acquire goods, for acquisitiveness is an innate instinct in him. To avoid quarrels, to maintain peace among men, and to assure a wise administration of goods and production, stable rights of private productive property ownership seem very necessary. Man is more careful with his own goods than with the goods of others, and is

more content with individual ownership than when possessions are held jointly and must be shared with others. Universal collective ownership would necessarily lead to disorder, and disorder is contrary to nature. 

7. To put forth his best efforts in labor a man needs more than a bare daily sustenance. By nature man needs an incentive to work, a motive power to give him courage to continue his labors. Self-interest is the only incentive which will make him industrious for any long period of time. 

8. Finally, we must note that there is clear evidence that all primitive peoples not only have private ownership of consumptive goods, such as clothing, but they also have at least private ownership of such productive property as canoes for fishing, hunting spears, and the like. Many primitive tribes, indeed, have as complete a system of private property rights in all productive goods as we ourselves have at the present time, and they acknowledge private ownership not only of material goods and land, but also of rights to songs, names, special medicines, and all other goods. 

97. Man's right to the ownership of private property is a general right only, and the possession of property imposes duties on the owner which, if universally recognized, would largely solve the world's economic problems. 

Although man has a natural right to own property, one must not confuse this fact with the idea that he must therefore actually possess goods or suffer an injustice. Man's right to own property is undeniable, but it is a general right and must be made definite by purchase, inheritance, or other just acquisition, or by what is called "first occupancy"; that is, by settling on land which belongs to no definite person, and thus taking possession of it. This latter method of acquiring property is still possible in some parts of the United States. 

Even though a man has exclusive right to his own property, this does not mean that his possession is unlimited and without control. The state has the right to purchase property, even though the owner may not wish to part with it, provided a just price

is paid and the purchase is necessary for the general good of the citizens. Similarly, the state has the right to tax property to provide itself with the necessary funds to carry out its duties, and it may grade taxation so that larger property owners pay a proportionately larger tax.¹ Again, not only must property be justly acquired by the owner, but it must be put to proper use. 

This obligation imposes two duties on the rich: the duty of justice and the duty of charity. Man has to account to God for the right use of the property which he possesses, and charity suggests that at least some of the surplus over and above that necessary to maintain himself and his family according to his state of life should be devoted to the benefit of those less fortunate than he. 

Property may be shared with the needy either by outright donation or by investment, provided in the latter case it serves the common good through adequate wage payments and suitable labor conditions. 

98. A reasonable amount of interest is permissible in return for the loan of capital. 

If a man has saved up capital for future use but does not need it in the present, he usually "invests" it. This he may do chiefly in two ways. He may become a partner in a business, nowadays generally in the form of being a stockholder in a corporation. If he does this, he receives a share in the profits, called dividends on stocks, and the question of interest does not enter in. The other way is to lend capital, either to persons who are temporarily unable to provide themselves with necessary consumptive goods, or to those who desire productive goods to carry on a business but who do not own sufficient capital to conduct their business without help from others. Capital invested in this latter way, in bonds, mortgages, etc., receives interest. 

Various complicated arguments have been put forth as to whether or not interest on capital lent to others is permissible. 

There are some who think that no interest should be paid on borrowed capital, but it would seem that a moderate interest is quite just for these reasons: First, the owner is entitled to some 'For a brief discussion of taxation, see Haas, Man and Society, pp. 77-81.

remuneration for the sacrifice he suffers in foregoing the investment of capital in stocks or otherwise becoming a partner in a business with a view to receiving profits; and also for foregoing the personal use of his money during the period it is lent, if he did not wish to invest it. Second, the owner, in entrusting his wealth to the borrower, runs the risk of losing it. Third, since capital is today a necessary factor of production, and helps man to produce more wealth, it would seem to be entitled to a share of the wealth it produces. 

99. Collectivism is a general term which may be used to describe the theories of all those who are in any way opposed to the private ownership of productive property. 

So many different philosophies are represented by the terms socialism and communism that it will be best for our purpose to group them under one common term of collectivism, and to use this word in the most general sense possible to signify all those who are opposed to the private ownership of the means of production. In general, collectivists are of the opinion that while a man should be allowed to hold and to acquire consumptive property, at least some of the forms of productive property which is a source of interest, profit, or rent, should be owned by the state, as representing the whole body of the people, for the common good of all.

To effect this change, the productive property now in private hands would have to be acquired by the state, either by purchase or by appropriation. To purchase all or even part of existent productive property would require more capital than the state possesses in its treasury. To appropriate it would inflict a grave injustice on the present owners because, whatever the deficiencies of the present order may be, and whatever may be the benefits which it is thought would be the ultimate outcome of the injustice, man has a natural right to the private ownership of both consumptive and productive property, and this right may not be taken away from him unjustly. 

100. Benefits which collectivists claim would be the result of the adoption of their system.

Collectivists claim that if their philosophy is carried into

practice, class distinctions and the great discrepancies between the rich and the poor, the capitalist-employer and the penurious wage earner will be abolished, and that hatred, envy, bitterness, selfishness, greed, and the spirit of commercialism will likewise disappear. Man will then belong to a universal brotherhood. He will love activity for its own sake, not for the acquisition of wealth as he does so often today. The present unnecessary duplication of work, wasteful competition, unscrupulous exploitation, and overproduction will be eliminated. Under a socialistic era industry will be more scientifically organized, production will be carefully estimated to avoid unnecessary expenditure of energy and wealth. The result will be an entirely reformed world, universal contentment, greater leisure for the masses, and a larger total of wealth than at present to be divided among all. . 

101. Collectivism which denies man's natural right to the ownership of productive property is founded on a false philosophy, but all collectivism would, in general, give too much power to the men in charge of the government of the state, and would in no wise produce the benefits which are claimed for it.

The fundamental error in all the theories of the socialists and communists lies in the denial of man's natural right to the private ownership of productive property. Apart from this, collectivism is a dangerous theory for several reasons: 

1. All productive property is the result of past industry and also of saving, and the adoption of any scheme of property confiscation would necessarily inflict an injustice on the owners of productive property who had acquired their ownership by their own labor, by inheritance, or in any other honest way. 

2. As we learned in the chapter on the state, the state should not undertake any work which is not primarily governmental, and which can be satisfactorily accomplished by private initiative and smaller societies. The state exists merely for the well-being of its members, and it must protect the rights of the individuals and groups which compose it, and it must not deprive its members of their natural rights. Moreover, were the state to have the ownership of all productive property, a tremendous amount of

power would be in the hands of the body of politicians who happened to form the government at any particular time. If this ruling body were unscrupulous or tyrannical, the well-being of the people would certainly be in danger. 

3. The advantages claimed by collectivism for the adoption of its theories can be proved to be false:

First of all, collectivism presupposes an ideal man who is ready to share equally with others, forego his own advantages, and show the same concern for the common goods that he would if they belonged personally to himself. Yet a mere change in the economic system will not alter man's nature. If he be rapacious or lazy now, he will never be different under a system of socialism. Man, left to his purely natural impulses, will never live in perfect peace and equality with all. The brotherhood of man can exist only when motivated by the lofty ideals of religion: were man a perfect being, he would conform his life even now to his ideals, and to his duties of charity and justice- and socialism would be superfluous. Monks and nuns share their goods in common within the community to which they belong, but there is this fundamental difference between their system and communism: they voluntarily give up the private use of their property from religious motives. Moreover, their communities are small, and they have no complicated industrial system to manage as there would be in a collectivist state. 

Then, too, even under the most ideal system of property distribution, there would still be inequalities, for nature does not seem to have intended that strict equality should prevail. 

Men differ in their capacity for work, in their capacity for acquiring wealth and keeping it when once it is acquired; some men have more money-spending propensities than others, and some have more demands upon their resources. 

Collectivists also presuppose in man an urge to work which will be sufficient incentive even when he has been deprived of his natural right to acquire personal ownership. But such is not man's nature. Under the more extreme forms of socialism he would have no inducements at all to save for future needs, for

the young, aged, and infirm would be cared for by the state. 

This would mean a total lack of development of filial love and care, which is nurtured principally by a desire to care for weak, ailing, or aged parents; children would have no duties to their parents; similarly parents would have no duties to their offspring, for the state would provide for all. Inevitably there would be a break-up of family life. Yet family life is the basis of all social life, and the state exists primarily for the protection and well-being of the family. Moreover, even though the particular form of collectivism in sway were to have no definite grudge against the family, there would be a danger of a development against family life, as in Russia against Bolshevism today. 

102. Pope Pius XI has given us an admirable plan for the reconstruction of economic society. 

In his "Reconstruction Encyclical" issued in May, 1931, to 

WORKERS 

IN EACH 

FACTORY TRADE UNIONS (TO UNITE WORKERS) EMPLOYERS 

ANO 

MANAGERS EMPLOYERS' ASSOCIATIONS (TO UNITE EMPLOYERS) JOINT BOARD (REPRESENTATIVES OF WORKERS ANO EMPLOYERS) 

of commemorate the fortieth anniversary of the Rerum Novarum, Pope Pius XI 

worked out an admirable plan for the reorganization of occupational society. In this encyclical he shows that the individualistic spirit of cutthroat competition which has existed since the industrial revolution, and the sharp class distinctions between employer and employed, cannot much longer endure without the complete breakdown of the existing oranization of economic society. "Just as the unity of occupational human society cannot be built upon class warfare," he says, "so the proper order- 

The organization society according to the Rerum 

Novarum of Leo XIII.

ing of human affairs cannot be left to free competition. . . . Free competition . . . though within certain limits just and productive of good results, cannot be the guiding principle of the economic world."

The Pope wishes, therefore, that all social groups within each state should become imbued with the spirit of justice and social charity, and that individualism should be replaced by the idea that society is one organic whole, all the members of which should work together for mutual good. To this end he advocates that all workers, without exception, should band together into trade unions; and that employers should also join employers' Acme

A convention of the American Federation of Labor. Such meetings bring together representatives of all the organized trades. associations within each trade and industry. For each trade or industry, therefore, there would be a trade union and an employers' association. His Holiness would have representatives of these separate associations for employers and employees meet together in local joint committees, so that "brains, capital, and labor combine together for common effort," and both parties can discuss their problems and their needs. Such a combined organization would obviously not countenance employees who did not fulfill their duties to the employer, nor employers who took

advantage of the weaker position of their employees. The Pope wishes, in fact, that not only should the worker receive a living wage but that the "wage contract should, when possible, be modified somewhat by a contract of partnership in this way wage earners are made sharers in some sort in the ownership, or the management, or the profits." Business, however, is not to be organized merely into local groups. His Holiness would have representatives of the local associations meeting in national councils (vocational groups) for each trade or industry, to decide all matters of major importance. 

But he does not want employers and employed to band together solely for their own selfish ends. He is of the opinion that not only must there be a reasonable relationship between wages and profits, but that a reasonable relationship between these and "the prices obtained for the products of the various economic groups, agrarian, industrial, etc.," should also be taken into account. 

He would have the state leave the settlement of all business of minor importance to these small groups, so that it will "carry out with greater freedom, power, and success the tasks belonging to it, because it alone can effectively accomplish these, directing, watching, stimulating, and restraining, as circumstances suggest or necessity demands." Finally, in order to safeguard the general interests of all concerned, he would have national conferences of employers, workers, capitalists, consumers, and representatives of the government. And since, as he so wisely says, nations "are largely dependent one upon the other, and need one another's help," he foresees international "economic coöperation by prudent pacts and institutions."

In this way harmful competition would be eliminated, and employers, capitalists, workers, consumers would all be united, so that society would become a truly harmonious organism working for the common good. To quote the Pope's own words: "True and genuine social order demands various members of society joined together by a common bond. Such a bond of union is provided on the one hand by the common effort of employers and employees of one and the same group joining forces to produce goods and give service; and on the other hand, by the

common good which all groups should unite to promote, each in its own sphere, with friendly harmony. Where this harmonious proportion is kept, man's various economic activities combine and unite into one single organism and become members of a common body, lending each other mutual help and service. For then only will the economic and social organism be soundly established and attain its end, when it secures for all and each those goods which the wealth and resources of nature, technical achievement, and the social organization of economic affairs can give. These goods should be sufficient to supply all needs and an honest livelihood, and to uplift men to that higher level of prosperity and culture which, provided it be used with prudence, is not only no hindrance but is of singular help to virtue." 

103. All citizens have a duty to strive for the betterment of economic society.

Since the occupational group is one to which nearly all men belong, and with which all men have dealings, since also it is in such grave need of reconstruction as social beings with a duty toward society, all citizens must do whatever is within their power to remedy the present evils. The following are some of the ways by which citizens may help toward this end: 

First, they can study the problem, that is, both the causes, and the plans which have been proposed to remedy it. Second, they can work for the moral betterment of the world, and for an elimination of the present individualistic spirit of greed, of desire for ever-increasing income and profits, without regard to the social aspects of economic life. With this end in view, they can strive in every possible way to bring about a more universal recognition of social justice and charity, and of the respective rights and duties of producers and consumers, of employers and employees, of capitalists, business organizers, and of all property owners. Third, they can promote good social legislation, particularly with reference to the worker, and they can work for the extension of trade unions with high ideals of justice, and for the development of the three kinds of coöperative societies discussed in the previous chapter.

-- 
Chapter XI INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

104. Nationalism is natural to man and should be preserved.

In Chapter VI the state was shown to be a natural society, that is, a harmonious coöperation of individuals and families within definite territorial divisions, fixed by natural boundaries, by custom, or by conquest. For harmonious relations to exist between the peoples within each state, however, it is obvious that there must be some uniformity of race, of language, or of interests and institutions. It is equally evident that in these latter particulars the nations of the world differ very widely. The world is too large and too varied in its needs, its mentality, its customs, and its interests, for all national or local societies to be destroyed and reunited in one large cosmopolitan state. Such a fusing of interests of all the nations of the world is neither conceivable nor advisable. Nationality or civil society has existed from the earliest times, and patriotism, the true and genuine love of a man for the country of his birth, is a highly desirable and admirable trait, and should not be abolished by a world state. 

Says Pope Leo XIII: "The natural law enjoins us to love devotedly and to defend the country in which we had birth, and in which we were brought up, so that every good citizen hesitates not to face death for his native land." "91

And the Catholic Union of International Studies pronounced as follows at the end of 1931: "The national sphere is for man a natural one, which in its 

¹Pope Leo XIII, Sapientiae Christianae. 

137

complete form, offers him at the same time an extension of the domestic sphere on a spiritual, cultural, ethnical, and territorial basis. It exercises on him an educative and stabilizing influence which is of great value in the development of the individual. 

The natural rights enjoyed by nationals and national groups are founded on this service rendered to the individual." 

French Line 

A French ship on the way to New York. Our country needs the things that these foreign ships bring us, and the other nations need our materials and products. 

105. Excessive nationalism is, however, undesirable, because nations are interdependent.

The state is a natural society, and patriotism is a noble virtue. 

Despite the beauty, and the utility of patriotism, however, false and excessive nationalism (called "jingoism" and "chauvinism") takes its root in envy, national pride, and egotism. It breeds "Nationality: Its Place in the Law of Nations," Month, January, 1932, p. 59; Catholic 

Mind, February 22, 1932, p. 86.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

139 

international hatred and injustice, unjust competition for markets, raw materials, and other advantages, and leads to ill feeling and wars. Just as individuals and families need the coöperation of others, and unite into varied social groups and into the state groupso also no state can exist as an isolated unit in the world. Each state is but one of many, and none are selfsufficing. The higher the civilization, the greater the number of needs, and the more do men within the state depend upon the product of other states. Moreover, from the social, scientific, and cultural viewpoint, even with the economic phase excluded, each nation has its own individual quota to contribute to the common needs of man, and any nation which refused this coöperation would hinder the development of other states and be blameworthy. Imagine the world's loss if no one could take advantage of the benefits to be derived from travel and intercourse with those of other races, mentality, and training. Imagine conditions, too, if each nation maintained a strict monopoly on the inventions of its citizens. To name but a few examples: if the United States refused to allow any other nation to use the inventions of Edison; if France had kept the inventions of Pasteur a close secret from the rest of us; if Canada had retained exclusive use of insulin; if Italy had denied the rest of the world the benefits of her art and her music or Marconi's brains; if England had monopolized the many engineering and electrical contributions of her citizens; if Germany had kept strict guard over the products of the brains of her musicians, chemists, and writers. 

106. International society is a primary society because of man's aptitude and propensity for it and his need of it. 

We have shown that cosmopolitanism or the fusing of the interests of all nations in the world, is not a natural thing. International society, however, is a primary society. International society means an amicable federation of the nations of the world, each maintaining its entity, but each working not only for its own good but for the good of others. It means a society of states, not a fusion of all existing states into one world state

as would exist in a cosmopolitan society. Just as man's family life should not suffer by the fact that he belongs to civil society or the national state, so neither should the national state suffer if it belongs to a federation of states. The state exists for the good of the families and the individuals which compose it, as we have many times stated. So a society of states should exist for the good of the member states. That nations are inter-

God intended all men to be united in brotherhood and mutual understanding in the "Mystical body of Christ," which is the Church, in the enjoyment of the common "Fatherhood of God." 

This fact is affirmed by the teachings of the prophets of old, of Christ, and of His Church. Addressing the Church, St. Augustine said: "Thou joinest together, not in society only, but in a sort of brotherhood, citizen with citizen, nation with nation, and the whole race of men, by reminding them of their common parentage." 973

141 b) The oneness of the human race, and the obligation of nations as well as individuals to abide by the one moral law, shows man's aptitude for, and need of, international society. 

The whole human race is descended from Adam and Eve, and all human beings from every part of the globe are essentially the same in bodily formation, in reasoning powers, in ability to exchange ideas by means of speech, and in certain fundamental character traits. We must remember, too, that the whole human 

Underwood & Underwood

The world would lose many beautiful national customs if cosmopolitanism ever came into existence. race was redeemed by Jesus Christ. All peoples are likewise bound by the common law of morality, and the same laws of justice and charity which regulate the actions of individuals must also govern their conduct when, gathered together collectively into a state, they have dealings with people grouped together in other states. All the offenses against the natural law which result from war injustices, hatred, envy, and distrust would be done away with, were harmonious world coöperation to exist. 

c) Man has a social, cultural, scientific, and economic need of mutual helpfulness among nations. 

We can easily see that man's need of social life extends beyond the narrow circle of his family, his occupational group, and other local groups; and that it extends, too, beyond the state. As we mentioned in an earlier paragraph, no state is self-sufficing, but

gains many cultural and other advantages, as well as economic help, by sharing its resources with others. With improved methods of transportation each state is enabled to share its economic advantages with others, and to participate in the preëminence of other states. Despite the sometimes almost impassable tariff barriers erected to impede international trade and commerce, as a consequence of the world's excessive nationalism, fear, and governmental greed, or as a result of the avarice of heads of business who make use of political corruption to achieve their ends, the value of international trade and specialization is indeed clearly recognized. World improvement in health and sanitation also requires coöperation between nations by international agreement as to laws regarding health, the sale and use of narcotics, etc., to stamp out disease and vice. d) Man has an aptitude for international society, and for at least the past several hundred years has shown a propensity for it.

For centuries nations have maintained treaty relations with other powers, and we can perceive the tendency toward federation of states in the nations of North and South America, and in the efforts of the League of Nations to maintain harmonious relations among world powers. 

107. Lack of world federation leads to wars, the direct causes of which may be many and varied. 

War is the most important result of the lack of harmonious relations between nations. The causes of each war naturally vary, but wars are usually attributable to one or several of the following reasons: 

1. Belief in a mistaken philosophy that the state is an end in itself and is practically free from the moral law; that it can commit acts (for example, the seizure of the property of other nations, or the ruthless invasion of another nation's territory) which would be wrong if done by an individual but which, by reason of the state's supposed sovereignty, is entirely permissible to the government of a people. It is obvious that the rights of no nation may be violated in the name of sovereignty, since the

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

143 fact that men are grouped together in a state does not mean that the ruler or rulers who govern in their name can in their name commit acts which are immoral. 

2. The pride of excessive nationalism, which makes a nation intolerant of others, regards them as inferior, and breeds a hatred of other states and an excessive sensibility to supposed hurt to national honor. Such a spirit in one nation leads to the engendering of like errors in others, and it further conduces to discontent among the peoples whom that nation may control. 

3. The materialistic doctrine which has now been rampant in the world for many years, which sets excessive store on worldly gains and riches, and leads to the many economic causes of war. 

4. Imperialism and greed, which are an outgrowth of the excessive nationalism and materialism just referred to, and the desire of nations to extend their authority over peoples and territories for the resulting prestige or economic advantages. 

The economic struggle offers several reasons for war: a) The desire for territorial expansion for the sake of the wealth to be acquired thereby, or the potential markets to be gained for the conquering nation's excess production. b) The struggle for the control of raw materials, since the more civilized a nation becomes the more raw materials it finds necessary for its well-being, and no nation possesses all the varieties sufficient for its needs. This is naturally a source of much friction and antagonism. c) The struggle for markets as an outlet for excess production consequent upon the lack of agreement among nations regarding foreign trade. This leads not only to a desire to possess other territory but to the erection of unreasonable trade and tariff barriers. d) The excessive trade and tariff barriers just mentioned, erected by nations to foster their home industries. Some goods are denied entrance to a country, others are allowed only upon payment of an exorbitant import duty, thus hindering other countries from finding an outlet for goods which, for one reason or another, they may be able to manufacture more cheaply than

the opposing nation. Excess in this regard creates much animosity and interracial hatred and distrust, particularly where these barriers are directed against some nations, but do not exclude others, or admit the goods of others on more favorable terms. 

Some are in favor of the removal of all tariff barriers. e) The importance to a country of controlling international shipping, railroad, and cable facilities, and the like, and the consequent struggle for possession of means of communication. f) Financial investment of one country in another, whether the loans be made directly by the government of that country, or to manufacturers, transportation companies, public utilities, and the like. This gives the lending nation an excessive interest in the welfare of the other. It likewise affords the latter an undue incentive to declare war in the hope of the cancellation of its debts in the event of victory. 

5. The possession of excessive armaments by all the nations of the world. Each nation has the right and the duty to protect its citizens against unjust aggression from the outside and from internal disorders, and in times of just warfare it may even conscript men for military service. Nevertheless, international rivalry in this respect has reached unprecedented and unwarranted proportions. Each year expenditure for armaments increases instead of being curtailed. National pride leads to this

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

145 increase, and the increase results in other nations fearing lest one of them become stronger, and hence leads again to further investment in arms. Thus a vicious circle is formed, for which there would appear to be no remedy but universal and mutual consent among the nations for proportionate reduction of arms. 

The expenditure of public money in this rivalry is appalling, and it is of no tangible benefit to the citizens from whom it is raised by taxation. This money could be employed for the welfare of the taxpayers, and thus could be put to much nobler and more useful ends. 

108. The effects of war are always disastrous and farreaching: future wars must be prevented.

There have been some who have glorified war, who talk of its "romance," who extol the strength and the bravery, the unselfishness and self-sacrifice which it engenders, saying that war brings to the fore all the finer and nobler qualities of man. It needs very little thought, however, to envisage the disastrous effects of war, particularly when we consider the methods employed toward the end of the World War of 1914-1918. When we think, moreover, of the perfection in aircraft and poison gases and long-range guns since those dreadful days, it is evident that the next large conflict would simply mean extermination for the attacked nation: extermination before even a chance for reprisals would be had. A visit to one of the War Veterans' hospitals will confirm our thoughts, that although many men did return home safe and well after the last war, nevertheless many who escaped with their lives are maimed or crippled; without limbs, perhaps, or deprived of their sight, or diseased through poison gas; probably incapable of earning a livelihood for the rest of their days-to mention nothing of the mental and physical pain which they endure. Then one must remember the millions of men who were killed, leaving behind them parents, wives, and children, to mourn their loss and often to suffer untold misery. One must remember, too, those men who returned from the war in apparent good health, but who are nevertheless deprived of the physical and mental endurance they once had.

Added to these effects of war, is the damage done to property in the invaded areas, and the irreparable loss of many of the world's choicest buildings, libraries, and art treasures. The moral consequences of war must also be considered: In war time man's animal passions are given freer play; they are, indeed, fostered in order to make men put all their energy in the fight; the 

Underwood & Underwood

A collecting station behind the British front during the late World War. Scenes of suffering such as this take much of the "glory" out of war. result is seen in violent national hatreds, in lack of restraint, in excitement and emotionalism, which leads to brutality, and often to sexual excesses as well. It is perhaps wise to remember, too, the weakened constitutions of many nonfighting citizens of the belligerent countries, particularly those who were children during the war, who played no active part in it, but were seriously affected by the lack of nutrition alone. Further, there is the lack of regular school training and education of children in areas near the scene of action. Then, again, depressions have been largely traced as the inevitable aftermath of wars. The cause of the universal depression at the time of writing has been ascribed largely to the World War.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

147 For self-protection, if for no loftier motives, therefore, it is incumbent on the nations of the world to devise a means whereby future wars will be avoided. Nor would permanent peace measures insure the saving of life and money at war time only. To protect itself from possible encroachment, each nation at the present time has its standing armies, navies, air fleets, and the rest. As has already been stated, the cost of upkeep is appalling, and the money thus spent could be employed in many ways more useful to the taxpayer. 

109. A war is only just in certain restricted circumstances.

There will always be a danger of war until nations have come to a universal agreement for the reduction of armaments to a minimum; and until man has set aside his selfish pride and greed and has come to a sane realization of the peace and prosperity to be attained by a true community of mind and spirit with his fellow men: each individual citizen working for the good of his state; each state looking beyond its petty nationalistic aims to the well-being of the community of nations. It is well, therefore, to set down the conditions of a just war: 

1. There must be a just cause — an actual and morally certain violation of right by another state. 

2. All peaceful means to avoid war must have failed or be impossible. 

3. There must be a reasonable hope of success that the people may not be made to suffer from the war without any prospect of deriving good from it, whether present or future. 

4. The evils involved in the war must not be greater than the evils sought to be averted. 

5. The sovereign authority must declare the war, and he must have a right intention. However just the cause, if a nation had subsidiary ideas of unjust aggrandizement or other wrongful benefit accruing to the victor, the war would, in this particular aspect, be immoral.

It may be mentioned, too, that at the termination of a war the victor may exact compensation for losses he has unjustly

suffered, but he should be moderate in the prosecution of his rights. He may not aim at destroying the opposing nation, and he may take punitive measures to subjugate the enemy only insofar as they are clearly necessary as a precaution against future assault where, for instance, the enemy clearly shows an attitude of permanent hostility and a resolve to renew attack at a future. time. 

110. Many efforts have been made to promote peace. 

Many efforts have been made to promote peace throughout the world since the time of the 1914-18 World War. The Nobel and the Bok peace prizes, the 

Woodrow Wilson Foundation, the Rhodes scholarships, the

Carnegie Association of In ternational Peace, all have helped in this regard. American Catholics have reason to be proud of the Catholic Association for International

Peace which is affiliated with the National Catholic Wel fare Conference in Washington. Most important of all was the League of Nations, with its World Court, established on January 10, 1920.

Erected in 1892, to commemorate the settlement of a bitter boundary dispute between the two countries, it bears on its pedestal the inscription: "Sooner shall these mountains crumble into dust, than Argentina and Chile shall violate the peace they have pledged at the feet of Christ their Savior."

Pope Pius XII has pointed out that the two root causes of the international friction which culminated in the second World War of 1939 were these:5 first, forgetfulness of the law of human solidarity and charity, and second, the error of the totalitarian state.

INTERNATIONAL SOCIETY 

149 The same Pope gave us five fundamental conditions of a just peace in a Christmas message to the world in 1939:6 

1. “To assure the right to life and independence of all nations, large and small, strong and weak." 

2. "Nations must be liberated from the heavy slavery of the race for armaments, and from the danger that material force, instead of serving to protect rights, become the tyrannical violater of them." 

3. "To examine the consequences of the gaps and deficiencies of the past," and "the establishment of juridical institutions, which serve to guarantee the loyal and faithful fulfillment of terms and, in case of recognized need, to revise and correct them." 

4. To observe "the real needs and just demands of nations, and of peoples, as well as of ethnical minorities." 

5. That rulers and peoples should be "penetrated always more and more by that spirit from which alone can arise life," namely, justice and charity.

Chapter XII  

111. Man has a right to an education which will take care of his physical, mental, technical, moral, and religious needs. 

Among the natural rights which man holds in common with all other men, as postulated in Chapter I, are the rights to life, to truth, to live socially, and to perform his duties to his Creator. 

These rights imply, among other things, that man has a right to a physical, mental, technical, moral, and religious education sufficient for his welfare in this world and in the next. 

If man is to live in a sufficiency of comfort as regards material goods, his physical and mental growth must be adequately cared for, and he must receive the technical training and instruction necessary to enable him to gain a livelihood-a training for domestic life, for science, industry, or one of the professions. 

If he is to live socially, his moral character needs training, so that he shall be aware of his obligation to respect authority, shall recognize his rights and his duties, and shall not always seek his own selfish ends, but learn to think of others. Man likewise needs an opportunity to acquire the necessary ability to set up a domestic society of his own in later years, and to take his place in the occupational group. In addition to this more practical education, he must also have some form of cultural instruction, to enable him to appreciate the best and more wholesome things of life. Given these opportunities he becomes a true social being, and not antisocial; that is, he becomes a benefit to society and not a hindrance or detriment to it.

If man is to perform faithfully his duties to his Creator, and thus insure not only his eternal welfare but also his true temporal well-being, his character not only needs the training 150

mentioned above, but he must also learn to face difficulties squarely, to make wise choices of his own free will, and to shun evil, whatever may be the temporal consequences of his good actions. Finally, in addition to this moral education, his mind and will must be nourished with truth about Almighty God, about his eternal destiny, and about the glories of the true Church and the achievements of its members.

Man therefore needs not only a secular, technical, and moral education which will fit him for life here below, but also a religious education which will direct him to his supernatural end, will give him a noble goal to strive for, as also a motive power for a high ideal of living, and the faithful recognition of the rights of others and of his duties in their regard. 

112. It is the parents' duty to provide for the adequate education of their children; but since they themselves have rarely the time, knowledge, or training, to give their children a complete education, they must delegate part of their function to others.

Education is a necessity for man and his natural right. Parents, therefore, have the strict duty of doing their best to provide their children with fitting instruction and training. 

The first and most important source of moral instruction is the home. This education is, indeed, begun immediately after birth, and modern psychologists insist that the first five years of a child's life are the most important from the educational viewpoint. 

Moreover, since this early training comes from those whom the child loves, it has a peculiar power. It has the additional advantage that in good homes, where parents have unwearying patience in the training of their children, the children, as it were, imbibe habits of virtue from their social environment, that is, from the constant good example of the parents and other members of the household. It is for this reason that good Catholic parents perform such a valuable service to society, in the training of their children as good citizens of the state, and it is for this reason, too, that children brought up in institutions lack the advantages of the child who enjoys good home life, affection, and care.

Yet the majority of parents have neither the time, ability, nor opportunity to give their children a complete education in all its branches without the assistance of others. Even moral training in the home may be more or less haphazard and lacking in the results achieved by expert educators. And since few can afford adequate instruction for their children by private tutors in the WASH,
