113. Educational society is next in importance to the five primary societies.

The society which provides for the more complete education of man may be called educational society or the school group. It consists of those who are especially trained to perform the important function of education, and a group of pupils who are intrusted to these teachers by their parents. Since man has such a fundamental need of education, and comes under the influence of the school at an early age, frequently to continue in it until attaining mature years, the groups which supply this want rank next in importance

to the primary societies. In 1938 there were 28,779,841 students in elementary and secondary schools in the United States, with an additional 1,350,905 students in institutions of higher education. 

114. It is the right and the duty of the state to promote education, and if the state is to prosper, it is essential that it have a good educational system. 

U.S. Dept. of Agr. 

A 4-H Club in session. Groups of this kind are doing much to improve methods in agriculture.

The state, as has been mentioned, exists for the common good of its citizens, and has the duty to protect its members and to supply whatever they cannot provide sufficiently by their own unaided effort. The state, therefore, must foster and aid education in every possible way: first, because the citizen himself needs an education and has a natural right to it; second, because prosperity, security, and economic and cultural advancement all rest on the education which young citizens receive, and no state can prosper unless its citizens are law-abiding, hard-working, frugal, possessing a high degree of mental, technical, and cultural achievement. Hence, it is the right and the duty of the state to insure to every citizen an opportunity to acquire at least a minimum education suited to his position in life, and to set down certain minimum requirements which the school must provide. And particularly is it necessary in a democracy that the citizens be educated.

Only persons with trained minds and a wide knowledge can properly deal with any difficulty which may arise, and regard a situation from all possible angles. In a democracy, therefore, where each man has a vote, and women, too, of late years, it is important that all should be given an education to enable them to vote intelligently. A high-school library. A well-equipped library is a necessity in any modern school. 

115. The educational facilities in the state must be complete, and must embrace the needs of all classes of citizens.

We said that it is the duty of the state to propose the minimum educational facilities available to citizens. These facilities must take care of the "whole man," and not merely provide for the physical, mental, and vocational education necessary to enable every child to take his place as an economically independent citizen in later life, with the requisite strength, mentality, and ability to provide for material progress. We might include the grade and high schools in this general need. 

Education must not be too theoretical and bookish, and entirely divorced from life; on the contrary, it must be "alive," and adequate for all the varied phases of social life, as well as for all cultural needs. Most important, however, education must primarily aim at the development of character. Law and order,

habits of thrift and good workmanship, and true "social-mindedness" within the state, all depend on the character of the citizens. 

A good character is formed only by long-established habits of virtue, based on high principles, noble ideals, well-trained strength of will. Only prolonged education of the best type can endow citizens with characters of this nature, and it is therefore to the

Hall of the Birds of the World, showing the Flying

Bird Dome, in the American Museum of Natural History, New York. Provision of museums is part of the state's duty in the general education of the people. advantage of the state, as well as its duty, to see that the educational system of the country gives its citizens a true moral training.

Finally, since religion and education are inseparable, and religion provides the child with a motive power for his good actions, the state must not neglect to provide its citizens with a program of education which includes time for the acquisition of religious knowledge, and it must do nothing to hinder such an education. 

Only in a training which makes him realize his allegiance to

Almighty God will the child become a true citizen and not fail in his duty to himself, to his fellow men, and to the state. 

In its educational program, however, the state must not supply merely a minimum education for the ordinary citizens. Special schools or other aids must be provided for the handicapped, the defective, and for those adults who have met with accident or

Within the state there should be museums and art galleries, to promote the cultural education of the people; evening and other part-time continuation schools should be available and free public libraries should be within easy access of all, for books are an important means of education. Private initiative must provide colleges, universities, summer schools, lectures, conferences, study clubs, correspondence courses, and so forth. There must be a good daily press and suitable, inexpensive weekly, monthly, and quarterly publications and pamphlets which will provide the citizen with good educational material untainted by undesirable features. 

In this latter connection it is desirable that the state should guard, by legislation, against objectionable periodicals and other literature. Since the radio, and the motion-picture theater are so rapidly becoming a cheap and universal means of education for the

masses, their programs should also be watched over by the state so as to be agents of good and not of evil, and their educational facilities should be fostered.

116. The educational facilities provided by the state are financed by taxation. Only when private educational opportunities are not available should state provision be made. 

There is an extensive system of free education in the United States, supported in the usual manner by taxation. Libraries, museums, art galleries are financed by local taxation, except such institutions as are under state control or privately endowed. The elementary schools and public high schools also depend for their upkeep on taxes levied in the particular city or district in which they are situated, but these schools invariably receive a state grant also. All the states have state universities, normal schools, and agricultural colleges, to which they allot yearly financial aid, and all the states have legislation regarding educational requirements and the school-leaving age. The Federal Government supports education in a variety of ways - by laws against the transmission

of immoral literature and plays through the mails; by publications; and, under the provisions of the Smith Hughes Vocational 

Education Act of 1916, by financial aid to those states which help to support high schools giving vocational courses. 

It must be noted, however, that if the members of a state provide an adequate system of education of their own initiative, which satisfies the requirements of the state, then the latter should in no wise interfere. It is the state's duty to see that each child within its realm receives a befitting education, and that adults have adequate facilities for educational development, yet only when good private education is not available is it the state's duty to supply the deficiencies. Again, although it is important that there should be as many educated citizens as possible within the state, nevertheless only a "minimum" education need be supplied free of charge to all. The proportion of state and municipal revenue allotted to higher education in this country has been severely criticized by many citizens as being unduly lavish expenditure of public funds for the benefit of the comparatively prosperous few. Instead of widespread provision of public education in the higher branches, the state might best be served if poorer citizens with the requisite mental ability were assisted in prolonging their educational training by state loans and scholarships.

In providing an educational system the state must bear in mind that however poor parents may be, nevertheless they have a natural right to educate their children, and if they do not wish their children to attend a particular school, they are at liberty to send them elsewhere. The school is merely an aid to the family, to supplement and not to supersede it, and therefore, despite the importance of the school group, teachers possess only so much authority and jurisdiction over their charges as the parents wish to delegate to them. For their own protection, however, the school may bargain with the parents and impose conditions on which pupils will or will not be accepted. But even where free education is concerned, strict attention must be paid to the parents' natural right to demand that the education given their children shall include at least the minimum requirements, and thus provide for training in morals, and instruction in whatever religion they choose to designate. Only where parents are morally or mentally incapable of educating their children, and no other persons provide what is lacking, must the state supply for their incapacity by taking complete charge of their nonreligious education. In every case, of course, the rights of the Church must be respected, for the reasons given in the next paragraph. 

118. The Church is the divinely appointed educator and has a duty and a right as regards education. 

The Church has rights over the Catholic child, and a duty to establish Catholic schools for two reasons: First, Christ conferred on the Church authority to teach and govern all the faithful when 

He gave the divine command: "Going, therefore, teach ye all nations... teaching them to observe all things whatsoever I have commanded you." Second, as mentioned before, the child has a natural right to a complete education, and this includes a right to an education which not only insures that he is not learning wrong principles and habits, but which provides him with positive moral and religious instructions and environmental influence. The 

Catholic child brought up in non-Catholic environment during his school hours forms non-Catholic friendships and imbibes non- Catholic ideas, and thus can rarely grow up with a true Catholic "Matt. xxviii. 19-20.

spirit and a complete knowledge of his religion and of how to live it every moment of his waking hours. The right of the Catholic child to be sent to a Catholic school when one is available must therefore be observed by his parents and by the state. Catholic Parish Building. This building, in Green Bay, Wis., includes church, school, rectory, and convent. 

This view shows the church and rectory. Rear view of above, showing convent and school. In splendid schools like this the church is carrying out her duty to give her children a complete education. 

119. There is an admirable Catholic educational system in the United States, but Catholics are almost wholly neglected in educational tax allotments. 

In accordance with the above statements, while Catholics recognize the work of the public schools in secular education, and contribute to their support by means of tax payments, nevertheless they claim that they are entitled to give their children a Catholic education, because of the lack of religious training in the public system.

There is an admirable Catholic educational system in the United States, entirely supported by voluntary contributions. In private institutions, of course, the students pay fees which usually cover the cost of their tuition. The elementary schools, however, are mainly supported by the parish, although students are usually charged a small fee in part payment of tuition and textbooks, and the teachers, mainly Religious, work for a minimum salary. In some parishes the school is able to accept children from needy families without payment, but in poorer districts, where there are A class in a Catholic parish school. The school supplements the family in education. large numbers who cannot afford the small cost of Catholic schooling, parents are often obliged to send their children to the local public school, to the danger of their religious training. It does not seem unreasonable to expect, where education is paid for out of the taxation, as in the United States, that those Catholic schools which meet the ordinary demands of the country should receive their share of tax payments allotted for educational purposes. 

Failing this, it seems that taxpayers should be allowed to decide for which class of school their taxes must be allotted, and Catholics could designate Catholic schools for this purpose. And if this is not allowed, surely where health service and standard textbooks are supplied gratis to public-school children out of public

funds, it would be but just and to the advantage of the nation if such service were likewise given to Catholic students. Textbooks are rarely, if ever, provided to Catholic schools by the public authorities, and in many localities Catholic children attending Catholic schools receive no public health service of any kind.

Chapter XIII  

121. The home is the inner sanctuary of the family, and it is essential that the family's physical surroundings should be desirable if the life of the nation is not to suffer. 

We now come to a discussion of two of the family's most important needs, hence two matters of vital concern to the social welfare of the nation: good housing and adequate recreation. First, we shall discuss some of the aspects of the housing question, which is essentially a home question, although the two words are, of course, by no means synonymous. 

It is easy to realize that four walls and physical environment do not make the "home," and that luxurious surroundings are not essential or even desirable for a family's welfare and happiness; too often these things impart a hotel atmosphere, and the home spirit is lacking. The word home describes something difficult to define but the meaning of which all understand; it should never be a place in which merely to eat and rest, but rather the inner sanctuary of the family, the abode where all members of the family delight to be, where they can meet in privacy, enjoy one another's company, and develop a normal family social group which is the most intimate and most important of all social groups in the world. It should be the most comfortable and satisfying abode for the parents and adult members, and the happiest of all places for children. If the home is a failure, the family is disorganized; and if the families of the state are not united in peace, harmony, and contentment, that state will never prosper. Although we ought not to overestimate the importance of environment, nevertheless social and psychological environmental influ- 163

ence is very closely bound up with one's physical surroundings. 

Joy, domestic contentment, industrial efficiency, high standards of morality, good health, all depend in large measure upon favorable housing conditions; that is, upon the provision for each family of a modestly comfortable and comely home. 

122. The minimum housing accommodation necessary for each family.

The ideal to be aimed at in housing is that every family should live in clean and decent surroundings, with accommodations providing at least the essentials for health, family life, and the preservation of morality. Every family should have a house which furnishes protection against the elements of nature, with a kitchen, and separate sleeping accommodation for the sexes. There should be windows in every room, so that there is enough light and air; and no families should be allowed to sleep in cellars or dark rooms without windows. Water should be obtainable within the house, and in tenement buildings it should be in an easily accessible and public place if each family is not provided with its own private supply, which is, of course, desirable. Every family should have its own bathroom and toilet, and, preferably, its own garden also. To insure good air and recreational facilities for children and adolescents, open spaces and, if possible, supervised recreation grounds, should be easily accessible. 

123. The housing conditions of the poor in all civilized countries need serious consideration. 

There still exist in all civilized countries thousands of poor families who live under dreadful housing conditions. Often they inhabit tenements where, perhaps, each room is made to house an entire family and serve the triple function of kitchen, parlor, and bedroom for all; where there are filthy yards, halls, stairways, and public toilets; and where the water supply is a long way from each "home." Sometimes rooms which house families have no windows; other rooms look out on narrow, sunless courts; frequently public authorities add injustice to the lot of these slum dwellers by bad provision of street lighting and cleaning; odors arise from all sides; there is often incessant noise in the streets

and in neighboring rooms; and since the rooms themselves are poorly ventilated, overcrowded, and dirty, the air in them is vitiated and the surroundings drab and cheerless. The serious overcrowding makes it impossible to provide for the proper separation of the sexes, to keep the home clean, or to have any privacy in those times when a short period of solitude would prevent the incessant close association of others getting on one's nerves. Land overcrowding results not only in lack of air, but also in inadequate parks or open-air playgrounds, so that the street is the only alternative to the unhealthy and uncongenial atmosphere of the home. 

And even single-family houses in the country may have conditions as bad as those we have just described, with serious overcrowding of rooms, insufficient protection against the elements, the extremes of heat and cold, impure water supply, and inadequate drainage and sanitation. 

124. The social results of bad housing are very grave morally, physically, and from the economic viewpoint. 

Because of these conditions few precautions can be taken against contagion, and the very atmosphere makes for a poor physical condition, so that it is small wonder that disease of all descriptions is rife in slum areas, and ill health is the rule and not the exception. The worker in poor health, without the relaxation from his labor which would be provided by a peaceful home, and unable to get refreshing sleep, becomes inefficient at his work, and thus costs the country money not only in reduced economic output but also in poor-relief. It is little wonder, too, that many adults turn to drink, gambling, and narcotics as a temporary distraction from their surroundings; that parents, with jarred nerves, often quarrel, treat their children cruelly, or resort to divorce or separation; that since those of opposite sex must sleep in the same room, immorality is frequent; that children, with no joyful home to attract them, with the street as their playground, and bad companions as their playmates, often grow up undisciplined, become rebellious against law and order and those better off than they, and may develop habits of delinquency, crime, and immorality. Indeed, the cost to the state for the upkeep of hos-

pitals, prisons, reformatories, and the relief of the poor, in consequence of bad housing, is appalling. 

125. The causes of overcrowding are various, but scientific remedies must be sought.

The introduction of the factory system was one of the earliest causes of bad housing conditions, leading as it did to a desire for accommodation near the place of work and hence to a scarcity of building space. The poorer workers seeking to live near their handelier

The old and the new in tenements. A striking contrast is shown in the old-fashioned houses at the left, and modern "model tenement" at the right. The apartments in the "model tenement" are airy, properly lighted, and safe in every way. There is a roof garden and proper fire-escape facilities. The rents are reasonably low. This picture was taken in New York City. place of employment in order to save time, and traveling expenses as well, could afford only a minimum of rent in return for a roof over their heads. The desire for accommodation in these factory areas, and for many years the great influx of immigrants to the poorer quarters of large cities, led to competition for room space and hence to greatly increased rents. These high rents charged to workers resulted not only in what is known as land overcrowding but also in room overcrowding, families even being obliged to share their one room with a lodger to help them pay the rent.

Workers tolerated these conditions either because they were ignorant that better conditions might be found elsewhere, or because they were unwilling to move to the suburbs and thus cut themselves off from the friendliness of neighbors and the brightness and distractions provided by the crowded city streets; sometimes they were unable to pay the cost of removal or the daily travel-

Acme

One of the many streets in the two square miles of tenement district on New York's lower East Side. Many children have only streets such as this in which to play. ing expenses entailed by residence in another district of the town. 

The greed of landlords is another frequent cause of bad housing conditions. The provision of housing has nearly always been left to private enterprise, and the landlords who built for profit were anxious to make as much money as they could. They therefore charged the highest possible rents, and usually gave in return a minimum of improvements and repairs. In the rural districts, where only low rents were obtainable because of the poor wages paid to the farm laborers, landlords did as little repairwork as

they could in order to get what they considered a fitting return on their capital.

The payment of a living wage and better housing legislation would largely eradicate these two causes of the housing evil. If all workers received a living wage, they could pay the higher rents necessary for better accommodation, and could also afford to take advantage of the now cheaper traveling facilities to the suburbs of all our large towns. 

The absence of improved housing legislation, which is, indeed, a third cause of bad housing, is chiefly due to the indifference of the voting public. Citizens should see to it that town planning and zoning are undertaken by all cities, so that overcrowding will not take place in the future. This will be possible if, together with these zoning plans, they likewise insist on legislation to make landlords conform to city development plans, setting forth minimum requirements for all new buildings, and specifying improvements which must be made in existing slum areas. Citizens should also see that competent and reliable building inspectors are appointed, and they should foster coöperative building-and-loan societies which enable workers to buy their own homes. 

While the foregoing causes are the main reasons for bad housing, the tenants themselves are sometimes responsible for the conditions. The way to overcome their deficiencies is principally by education of children in the schools, though some educational work can be done through parish societies, settlement houses, or other agencies. Particularly should a girl be shown that since the home is the woman's workshop, and she spends more time in it each day than the rest of the family, it is to her own advantage to keep it clean, healthy, beautiful, and comfortable. She should likewise be made to see that it is not only to her advantage to do this, but also her duty, for she is the central pivot of the family, and only if there is a bright and cheerful house for her children and husband to come to, will her family be united, satisfied, and safe from the dangers of recreation sought outside the home. 

Courses in homemaking should be provided for girls in all our schools, and all students should be taught that in the home the

tenant has actual duties and responsibilities, not only toward himself and his family, but also toward his neighbors and his landlord.

Landlords, whose tenants are to blame for much of the dirty and unsightly conditions of slumland, might perhaps find it to their advantage to employ intelligent women rent collectors. These should be specially trained not only to instruct the tenant in homemaking, but also to observe any necessary repairs which the landlord himself should perform. Visiting housekeepers in poorer districts can perform a similar service, but they do not collect rents.

Another remedy of the housing question lies in the provision of cheap residential clubs for poorly paid unmarried men or women, where they can live in congenial surroundings at a minimum of cost.

Finally, where overcrowding in an area is beyond improvement, city authorities may find it necessary to formulate a settlement plan, and help families from slum areas to settle in the suburbs or in rural districts elsewhere. 

126. Recreation is essential for our well-being, and there is a growing amount of leisure time at man's disposal. 

The problem of providing good recreational facilities for young and old is intimately connected with the housing question, with the family, with social virtues, and with good citizenship. 

To maintain physical, mental, and moral fitness, our life must certainly not be a mere round of work, eating, and sleep. It is essential that we have some leisure time to employ as we think fit. Children, of course, have much more free time than adults, but there are few people who do not have at least an hour or two a day to spend at their own devices. Indeed, the amount of leisure at our disposal grows each year because of the increasing use of labor-saving devices in the home to lighten the wife's daily tasks, the large amount of unemployment in the world, and the agitation for shorter hours of work. 

The manner in which both children and adults spend their leisure time is of vital importance for their own welfare and for

that of the nation, for not only is it true that the "devil finds work for idle hands to do," but recreational society comes next in importance to educational society. It forms one of the most significant social groups to which we belong, for there is a tremendous power both for good and for evil in leisure-time activities. 

127. Recreation has physical, mental, and moral value. 

Good, wholesome recreation is of immense value physically and spiritually, for it affords relief from the strain and monotony of daily tasks, creates mirth and happiness, and develops body, mind, and character. Play, that is, physical recreation, is particularly useful, for not only does it develop nerve and muscular control, and the heart, lungs, and digestive organs, but it teaches emotional control also, and such social habits and behavior as observing the rules of the game, obedience to authority, respect for the rights of others, and the value of teamwork or coöperation with others; it likewise develops mental alertness, confidence, courage, resourcefulness, self-control in defeat or victory, insight, justice, and charity. In other words, physical recreation in particular avoids defective physical and moral growth, cultivates positive good habits, and develops a virile character and a spirit of social service and good citizenship. Wholesome recreation of all types, however, both passive and active, is a necessity and of great value. 

128. There are many forms of recreation, for individuals vary greatly in their tastes.

Recreation, of its very nature, must be the expression and satisfaction of our own desires by the employment of our time as we think fit. Not all have the same tastes or wish to spend their leisure time in the same way. Some people like the passive relaxation offered by art, music, and literature, and prefer to spend their leisure hours in reading, in listening to lectures, concerts, or the radio, in travel, visits to art galleries or museums, or by going to the theater or motion pictures. Some enjoy playing billiards, bridge, or other games, or the activities of a debating or dramatic society; or they may even find sufficient relaxation in conversing with congenial companions. Others, again, consider a change of work as their recreation, and have a hobby which they sufficiently

enjoy to continue without the main motive, at least, being financial gain. As a diversion from their usual employment, therefore, they occupy themselves with carpentry, hunting, fishing, or gardening; they study some special subject, write books, or help an organization such as a sodality, a parish club, the Society of St. 

Vincent de Paul, or a civic group, for example, a League of Women Voters. Still others wish to indulge in athletic activities, to play ball, tennis, or golf; to skate, swim, or hike. While this active play is essential for children, it is a form of activity in which all adults can indulge, the cultured as well as the uneducated, for although many people confine their relaxation to amusements of various kinds or to a change of occupation, they also need the physical and social value of sports. 

129. The home is the most important recreational center. 

The ideal place for recreation is the home, and every family should systematically provide for the recreation of all the members. The home will then be a joyous and beloved place, where each member can enjoy the diversion he likes best, and where all can have some active and wholesome fun and recreation together. 

The community of interests thus established will make the home spirit thrive; and parents, by playing with their children, will learn more about them, be able to correct the character defects they show in play, and teach them games for future use with outside recreational groups. There are, of course, innumerable recreational activities in which the family can engage. The members can play games and have family outings, picnics, and longer vacations together; they can listen together to music or the radio; work together in the garden, or have some mutual hobbies. Reading and one's personal hobbies are also best done in the home. 

Members should respect one another's interests, however, and particularly should children be made to feel that they are welcome to play about the house, and make a normal amount of noise, and they should not have their games interrupted so frequently that they become dissatisfied and lose their desire to play at home. 

Particularly to be encouraged is the Home-Play Movement which aims at instructing parents how to turn their back yard into play-

grounds for their children, and in teaching them a variety of children's home games. 

130. The home needs supplementary outside agencies, but the attraction of bad commercial amusement must be counteracted.

The poor, who are most in need of recreation and refreshment, have least opportunities of employing their leisure time well. Too often bad housing conditions make the home so unattractive, with no facilities in or near it for healthy play, that members of the family seek all their diversion outside the home, and frequently can resort only to the street or to cheap commercial places of U.S. Dept. of Agr. 

Camps make for leadership. amusement. The tremendous power of commercialized play at the present time, where financial returns are of paramount consideration, with a total disregard of what is best and most helpful for the citizen, should indeed be fully realized. Some commercial amusements are, of course, harmless or even good, but many of them appeal to man's baser instincts, work upon his gambling propensities and the universal instinct of sex, and offer amusements which are attractive but harmful to children and adults alike. Many a man and woman has been driven to drunkenness and immorality, to gambling dens, low-grade dance halls, and

other places which bring out the baser passions in man solely for want of attractive substitutes. 

Nor is it only those who live in slum areas who need provision for recreation outside the home. All recreation cannot take place within the family circle, for people need the companionship of others of their own age, and often have interests which are not shared with other members of the family, or which cannot be undertaken in the home. Many of the richer population, even, live in apartment houses which offer far too inadequate space for play. As with the poorer members of the community, they also must needs turn to outside diversion and become largely depend- A city playground. Here the children of the neighborhood can play in good surroundings and under capable supervision. ent on commercial amusements. They are more able than the poor, however, to afford to belong to private recreation and sports clubs.

Outside recreational facilities are, therefore, essential to supplement what the home may have to offer, and although a certain amount of commercial amusements is necessary, and richer people may form their own private recreation and sports clubs, other agencies, notably the church, school, and local community group should furnish the greater proportion of needs. 

131. Supervised play for children and adolescents is essential, and this should be provided by the school and other agencies.

The play needs of children and adolescents in particular should

be well taken care of, not only because they have more leisure time at their disposal, but also because they are in the formative stage physically, mentally, and morally, and all social groups have a more important influence on them at this time than in later life. In the classroom or the workshop they have to do what they are told, and their activities in the home are more or less supervised, but at play outside the classroom and the home they 

Small children at play in a public park. give freer rein to their own personalities. The recreational groups to which they belong, and their activities within these groups are, therefore, of vital importance, and they need good companionship and systematic provision for their leisure hours. Boys will form gangs unless they can find an outlet for their energies in competitive games and athletics, and many people have been permanently influenced for good or evil by their early play companions. Statistics have, indeed, proved that much juvenile delinquency and adult crime have been caused by lack of adequate recreational facilities, particularly for the young who have only the street in which to play. Although the play instinct is strong in man, and children should be allowed to express themselves as much as possible in playing their own games, and adolescents allowed to form their own committees for dramatic and other

social functions, nevertheless both children and adolescents need the help, encouragement, and suggestions which can be given them by a trained and tactful supervisor. This direction will not only help them to learn games which will prove useful in their recreational activities when older, but will also aid their character formation to the greatest possible extent. The school should therefore have a sufficiency of outside activities to provide children with athletics, clubs of all kinds, and social functions, while whatever is lacking in school programs should be supplied by the parish, by other private agencies, and by the community. 

132. Social settlements provide for the needs of the poor in certain districts.

Some districts are fortunate in having what is known as a social-settlement house, sometimes called a neighborhood club or community center. A social settlement is a group of people with cultural and social advantages, who settle in a suitable house in a poor neighborhood for the purpose of sharing their advantages with the people in the district. The social workers who form these settlements maintain that their work is not "charity" in the strict sense of the word, for in return for the help they give, they receive a development of personality and the friendship which their poor neighbors have to offer. The activities of settlements are many, for the aim is to discover all the needs of the community in which they live. Some settlements chiefly provide cultural education, in the belief that other agencies successfully supply recreational facilities and the poor man needs a broadening of his mental vision if he is to live a more complete life. Probably the most important function of settlement houses, however, is club work, that is, the provision of a home, as it were, where the people of the neighborhood can congregate for a few hours to forget their troubles by some form of recreation, or to discuss their needs with understanding social workers who endeavor to find suitable remedies.

It is to be noted, however, that although some social settlements sincerely strive to help the religious needs of the community, and the Catholics they serve do not have their faith injured

in any way, nevertheless it is best that where parish clubs and activities are not sufficient to meet the people's needs, the parish should establish its own settlement house, or have a joint settlement with one or more other parishes not too far away from the neighborhood to be served. 

133. Next to the family, the most important agency which should furnish recreational facilities is the Church. 

The need for recreation and its influence is so great, and so much ill-afforded money is spent on commercial amusement which is often positively harmful, that the Church is filling a real need The Catholic Boy's Brigade, New York. These boys learn to play and work for God and country. in providing social and recreational facilities for its members. 

Leisure time spent under the Church's auspices cannot but be morally healthful, and the facilities offered have the additional advantage of being near the homes of the people, so that they are not obliged to travel too great a distance or to spend an undue amount of time away from the family. Moreover, these activities will help to keep the parish a united social group, and will give pastors and others the opportunity of showing that religion is not divorced from life, and that true piety does not exclude happy and healthful fun. They will also give Catholics the opportunity of meeting their fellow Catholics, which will lead to fewer mixed marriages and fewer Catholics coming under the influence of nonreligious people, to the loss of their Faith. According to the needs of the different parish groups, therefore, there should be plays and concerts, dances, lectures, social affairs, clubs

for games, and dramatic, social, literary, and other societies. 

Wherever possible the parish should have its own athletic grounds as well. In these various ways the parish can thus become the center of the social life of its people. 

In many parishes lack of funds prevents the offering of adequate recreational facilities to parishioners, but in others a variety of activities has been established, and adolescents are particularly taken care of by the Catholic Boys' Brigade, Catholic boy-scout and girl-guide troops, the baseball and basketball leagues of the 

Junior Holy Name Society, or similar undertakings.

Among national Catholic organizations in connection with recreation, two agencies especially must be mentioned: First, the 

Catholic Theater Movement of New York, which was founded in 1912 and issues its Bulletin to give the names of plays which do not offend against morals or in any other particular; second, the similar work of the Motion Picture Bureau of the International Federation of Catholic Alumnae, which publishes "white lists" of films in the Catholic periodicals. 

134. Local community groups of the state must supply whatever is lacking in private provision for the recreational needs of its citizens.

The facilities offered by the family, by the Church, by private organizations of all kinds—including commercialized amusements, settlement-house activities, and the provision made by some industrial concerns for the benefit of their employees - do not take care of all the recreational needs of the people. Whatever is wanting must be supplied by the state, whose duty it is to foster the public well-being in every way. Local community groups, therefore, should provide parks, public playgrounds and playing fields, swimming pools, libraries, art galleries, museums, musical entertainments, and community social centers where such amusements as games, dancing, dramatics, and physical exercises may be engaged in when outdoor recreation is not feasible. 

To help municipalities and other civic communities to establish year-round recreational systems for the wholesome recreation of their citizens, the Playground and Recreation Association of

America, whose offices are situated in New York, was especially founded. The Association is at the service of individuals and groups of all kinds who may need its assistance. It answers inquiries, publishes pamphlets, handbooks, a yearbook, and a monthly magazine, and maintains an employment department for trained recreation leaders. It has a special bureau for the promotion of recreational facilities for Negroes, for this section of the community in America has been woefully discriminated against in this, as in other matters, a fact which has probably been the cause of much of the delinquency among colored people.

Chapter XIV  

136. It is necessary to distinguish between the terms "poverty" and "destitution"; "paupers" and "dependents."

Before going into the problem of dependency and relief it is necessary to have a clear understanding of the distinction between terms which are frequently used in connection with this subject. Poverty means the deprivation of something necessary for subsistence and physical efficiency. It is a vague and relative term, for some people consider themselves poor if they are unable to afford an automobile, or the education of a son or daughter at an expensive private school; others, and with more cause, regard themselves as poor because they cannot buy the clothes necessary for their station in life, or because they can afford little amusement or diversion, and cannot take a yearly vacation; others, again, are poor because they lack the resources to provide themselves with sufficient food, clothing, or shelter to maintain full bodily vigor.

Destitution, on the other hand, is a positive term. It means the entire deprivation of the means of subsistence. A person without the means to purchase food, or clothing, or shelter, may be termed destitute, and since man has a right to life, it is the duty of society to take care of him. 

A pauper is one who, being poor and destitute, seeks public aid, that is, aid from the state or local civic community. The word pauper, however, is now little used, and the much kinder name of dependent is usually given to all who are unable to provide themselves with the necessities of life and who seek assistance of others. The word dependent, therefore, is not used in this chap- 179

ter in the sense of one dependent upon another, such as a child on its parents, but in the more specific connotation of one who receives private and even public-charity assistance from others than the immediate family. 

It must be remembered, of course, that a very large number of the poor are neither paupers nor dependents. They manage to subsist without outside relief, frequently to the detriment of their physical well-being. Keystone

Many an aged person is happy to be independent of charity. The old ladies' sewing shop, conducted for several years in New York, has provided work for many and has been a blessing to them. This is an important form of indoor relief. 

137. Poverty and dependency is one of the greatest social problems which still remains unsolved.

It is a sad blot on our civilization that there are still people in the world who have to subsist without what may be termed even the necessities of life, while others have much more than enough for their needs, or are even fabulously wealthy. The causes of poverty, however, are so many that a discussion of them is almost impossible. Invariably a person is not poor for one single cause alone, but as a result of complex social and economic conditions. 

Some are poor largely through their own fault, because they are shiftless, extravagant, incompetent, immoral, drunkards, given to betting or gambling, or because they have some other moral

handicaps or character defects, due either to self-indulgence or to faulty training. Others are poor because of physical or mental disabilities which make it impossible for them to compete with wage earners who are mentally and physically normal; or because they have an ailing wife or children or other relatives who need medical and hospital attention which takes up all their resources. 

Others, again, are poor because of unemployment, or because they have only irregular, insufficient, or poorly paid work; because one or other of the contingencies to which the worker is liable came upon them and they were unprovided to cope with the emergency. Some people who are born poor have little or no chance to overcome the disadvantages of malnutrition, bad surroundings, poor housing, or undesirable associates; also, they may have no training for skilled work of any kind, because they had to earn their living from too early an age. Since poverty almost invariably undermines physical and mental ability, and frequently leads to such moral results as a lack of self-assurance and initiative, discouragement, depression, and even habits of vice and crime, it is important that society should try to abolish it. 

Ewing Galloway 

A blind beggar. The aged poor should not be forced to resort to begging to gain a livelihood. 

138. Poverty is not necessarily an evil, but the causes, both social and individual, should be eliminated so far as possible. 

Poverty will always exist because there will ever be inequalities among mankind, and man will always be subject to unforseen contingencies in the material world, while in the spiritual domain some will always succumb to temptation. "For the poor you have

always with you," said our Lord Himself. It is not possible for us to say that poverty is an unmixed evil. Certainly, it is no disgrace for a person to be poor provided he has not become dependent upon others for causes which he himself could have remedied. 

It is, indeed, a fact that the "respectable poor" are often far more virtuous than many of the rich, and although there have been holy men in all stations of life, wealth brings with it many temptations, and the means of satisfying evil desires. A large proportion of canonized saints have been very poor in this world's wealth. 

Yet, as was stated in Chapter X, the rich must make right use of their property and see that at least some of the surplus over and above that necessary to maintain themselves and their families according to their station in life, accrues in some way to the benefit of the less fortunate; and all men must strive for the betterment of society. The extensive poverty in the world would be largely eliminated with the universal payment of a living wage, the improvement of working conditions, the provision of social insurance against all risks to which the worker is exposed until such a time as he receives a living wage, the extension of vocational training for every citizen, the amelioration of inadequate housing, the provision of adequate hospital, medical, and health facilities for all, and a return to true Christian family life with more competent moral training at home and in the schools. In other words, a reorganization of our industrial and social system is needed if widespread poverty is to be abolished. 

In the meantime, the poor are among us, and it is society's duty to see that all citizens possess at least a minimum of material goods necessary for well-being, for apart from the universal law of charity, no state can prosper with large numbers of unprovided poor in its midst. 

139. It is the duty of the state to assist the unprovided poor; and citizens are bound to contribute their fair share of these expenses.

While many claim that private charity or almsgiving is the most effective way of relieving poverty, and this is, indeed, fre- 'Cf. John xii. 8; Matt. xxvi. 11; Mark xiv. 7.

quently the case, nevertheless the needs of all the poor are rarely covered in this manner. Since, therefore, it is the duty of the state to provide for the well-being of its citizens, the state is bound to supply whatever is wanting in private charity, and it may levy contributions from the citizens to cover the expenses thus incurred. Every citizen, therefore, should contribute his fair share of the public burden for charity relief, and a man should not refuse to pay his share of the public fund on the plea that he has given private alms. 

140. The application of scientific methods of social service is the best way of relieving poverty. 

The practical application of the principles of sociology in the relief of the poor and in provision for the sick, the handicapped, the defective, and the delinquent, is called social work or social service. As regards the relief of the poor, which is our immediate concern in this chapter, since there may be much waste of resources in the giving of private alms, and the poor may not be helped in the most effective manner by this method, many private and public charity organizations have adopted what is known as scientific case work. Modern social case work comprises much more than a mere dispensation of alms or provision for immediate needs; while relieving needs, the social worker tries to prevent them by removing the causes from which they rise, so that they will not recur in the future. As stated, poverty is due to a variety of causes, and each "case" must be treated in accordance with the poor person's need. It is not always necessary to give money; sometimes the only help needed may be sympathy and patience in changing a character; on the other hand, it may be a complete change of environment, an expensive operation, the break-up of a home, or the finding of employment. Social case work consists first in the discovery of persons in need of help; nor is it always advisable to wait until the poor themselves apply for alms, for then they may already have reached a state of destitution, too late for constructive assistance, and so become a costly burden to the community. The second step consists in interviewing the person needing help, to investigate the causes of his poverty. If

necessary, his family and other individuals and groups must also be interviewed, so that all possible information is secured. Third, the case must be diagnosed, taking into consideration not only that need which is most visible, but also all the multiple contributory wants, so that a complete understanding of all needs is arrived at. Lastly, comes the treatment of the needs as a unit, using the individual's own resources where possible, and also all Keystone

The visiting nurse. A form of outdoor relief. available community resources which may be necessary.

While all this is proceeding, temporary relief of chronic poverty is, of course, often necessary. 

141. Poverty is relieved by both outdoor and indoor relief, according to circumstances. 

The terms "outdoor" and "indoor" relief are technical expressions to denote two methods of relieving the poor. 

Outdoor relief means the care of the sick and the poor in their own homes or in foster homes. Indoor relief is the care of the sick and needy in institutions. As a general rule it may be said that, wherever possible, outdoor relief is the more preferable; the home is not broken up, and the needs of the family can be more carefully ascertained and provided for; it is also generally considered that this form of relief can be administered more economically.

In the United States, indoor relief is generally given only to the aged poor and to vagrants, in "homes," almshouses, or public lodging houses; to the sick poor in public hospitals and convalescent homes; to orphans in orphanages; to the handicapped and

defective in homes and institutions. Many of these classes of dependents, however, receive outdoor relief; for example, the aged are entitled to pensions in certain states, and "mothers' aid" is often given to widows with children.

In many localities, the aged, certain defectives, orphans, and sometimes juvenile delinquents are taken care of in foster homes. 

Families who are willing to take care of them are paid a fixed sum by the charitable organization, and social workers call on 

Taking care of newly born baby and mother in the home; another form of outdoor relief. them regularly to insure their proper treatment. They thus enjoy some sort of home life, and are generally happier than when cared for in an institution. This method of providing for dependents is considered a type of outdoor relief. 

142. Well-organized charitable agencies do not duplicate the services given by other agencies. They are usually aided in this by a Social Service Exchange. 

To prevent unnecessary duplication of work and overlapping of social service, private and public agencies for the relief of the poor in any given area frequently establish what is known as a Social 

Service Exchange-sometimes called a Confidential Exchange or a

Central Index. This system is organized as follows: Whenever an agency which is a member of the Exchange gives assistance to an applicant or investigates a "case," it records at the Exchange the name and address of the person investigated or helped, to indicate that it has information to supply, upon application, to other members of the Exchange. Records are confidential and are open only to authorized representatives of Exchange members, so as to protect the poor from the curiosity of those not immediately concerned with giving them relief. If a new case comes to the notice of an agency, its first step is to inquire at the Exchange to learn if the person or family to be assisted is known to other organizations. Then, if a record of the case is found, the inquirer communicates with the agencies noted, obtains all available information from these sources, and thus makes another investigation unnecessary. Through the Exchange, an agency can also sometimes collaborate with other organizations in rendering assistance to the needy; for instance, a city relief agency might supply food and pay the rent, the Society of St. Vincent de Paul might give clothing, and a hospital provide necessary free medical attention. 

This union of forces leads to greater service efficiency and prevents duplication of assistance.

Members of the Exchange often form what is usually known as a Council of Social Agencies. This organization holds meetings at which members can exchange views and the result is usually a much greater unification and coördination of charitable assistance in the district which it serves. 

143. Many districts have also organized the obtaining of finances by means of Community Drives and Chests. 

One of the greatest problems of social workers is the obtaining of funds to carry on their work, and group financing of private charities has proved an effective way of removing this disability. 

Councils of Social Agencies led the way to Community Chests, which is the name given to the central money-raising organization for financing nearly all the social work in a community. In 1940 there were 552 Community Chests in the United States. The smallest one was that in Hawthorne, California, which raised

$373 in 1940 in a town with a population of 15,000, and the largest was that of Boston, where the population of 1,928,361 raised $4,674,724.2 The participating agencies in a Community Chest usually appoint representatives to the board, which also includes prominent bankers, business men, and others, to serve as representatives of the local contributors to the fund. Prior to the yearly campaign for money, each participating agency is required to prepare a budget for the coming year. This involves planning ahead not only for current expenses, but also for necessary extensions; it generally involves also the adoption of uniform accounting systems. Next come extensive preparations for the systematic solicitation of donations from all citizens, use being made of up-to-date publicity methods to insure as large contributions as possible. The 

Chest then makes an active campaign for funds during a fixed period of one or two weeks. The goal aimed at is the amount of the consolidated demands of the participating agencies, and an extensive drive is made to reach or even exceed this figure. Proceeds are divided among the participants in the agreed amounts. 

Usually contributors to the Chest are promised that during the ensuing year they will be immune from further solicitation by agencies within the federation. 

By this method, the participating agencies usually obtain more funds than they would by private endeavors to secure monies, and as the Chest board insists that they maintain high standards of operation, the sum contributed usually is used to good purpose, and the poor often benefit by better service. Occasionally, however, the administrative boards of the Chests have favored the strong and powerful members, at a sacrifice of the interests of smaller agencies; and in some districts there have been complaints that the board unduly interfered with the work of the individual agencies. For these and other reasons, Catholic charities have not always participated in local federated finance drives, although they have done so in many localities, to their entire satisfaction. 

2Directory of Community Chests and Councils of Social Agencies, pp. 60-63.

144. The Catholic Church has ever been foremost in relieving the needs of the poor, and in most dioceses of the United States Catholic charities are excellently coördinated. 

From the very beginning of the history of the Church we have records of her charitable activities, and the care of the poor by the good will and friendliness of neighbors, by parish priests, by monasteries and convents, and later, if they were workers, by the gilds. Nor was the Church's help to the poor suspended after the 

Reformation, for all readers must be aware that she dispenses millions of dollars every year in charitable donations through the parish priests, and such organizations as the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, the Third Order of St. Francis, the Knights of Columbus, the Ladies of Charity, the Christ-Child Society, and similar groups. We might also call attention to the fact that as early as the fourth century St. Basil had done away with many of the conditions to be found even now in the "indoor" relief of certain poorhouses, by the housing of the sick and dependent in separate buildings; and even in the seventeenth century some of the Sodalities of Our Lady were just as scientific in their methods of giving "outdoor relief" as any modern relief agency.³

The natural unit for Catholic social service is the parish. There are, however, certain services, such as the provision of orphanages, hospitals, special institutions for defectives and delinquents, which the parish cannot possibly establish and maintain. These inter-parochial charities are often undertaken by the Society of St. Vincent de Paul, which is the principal relief agency in most parishes. Of the larger enterprises many are provided by religious communities, who undertake charitable works as they appear necessary, or in accordance with the traditions of their Order, sometimes with little regard for overlapping of service provided by similar communities in the same district. Many dioceses have obviated this difficulty by a central diocesan charity organization, which does invaluable work in the organization of diocesan charities. The annual meetings of the National Conference of Catholic 

Charities, begun in 1910, also provide an important clearing house Cf. H. S. Spalding, S.J., Chapters in Social History.

for exchange of views between the foremost Catholic social workers from all over the country, and the heads of the important Catholic institutions. The monthly Catholic Charities Review, published in Washington, is the official organ of the Conference. 

By the publication of surveys, study outlines, and other valuable aids, the Department of Social Action of the National Catholic Welfare Conference also assists the national outlook of Catholic charities in the United States.* "Cf. Rev. John O'Grady, An Introduction to Social Work, Chap. XV. for further informa tion regarding diocesan and national organization of charities in the United States.

Chapter XV THE DEFECTIVE 

146. The defective is a general term to describe the physically handicapped and the mentally deficient, who need very special training and care. Catholics have a particular duty in regard to the Catholic defective. 

The word defective is a general name used in social work to describe the mentally deficient, the insane, and also those who have some physical handicap, such as the blind, the deaf, the crippled, and the permanently sick. 

Physical and mental deficiency prevents people, through no fault of their own, from taking their normal place in the world as independent social beings. It is, of course, the duty of the families of such people to try to fit them to become as normal as possible, by special hospital treatment, care and education, and, if necessary, by providing them with vocational guidance and training which will enable them to be as economically independent as possible in adult life. A specially constructive program must therefore be formulated for all defectives, and sometimes their families cannot help them, either through disinterest or, as is frequent, through lack of facilities, and particularly through poverty. When this is so, it is the duty of the state to provide all necessary aid for their welfare, if private agencies do not themselves supply the deficiencies of the family. 

Catholics have a particlar duty in regard to the defective, not only because most of our Lord's miracles were performed for people of this sort, but because they usually need what is known as "indoor relief," and unless there be a Catholic environment provided for the Catholic handicapped and deficient, many will 190

THE DEFECTIVE 

191 necessarily lose their faith. Defectives also, cut off from a full normal life in the world, need the solace of religion more than those who are physically and mentally sound, and assuredly it is a true apostolate to fill this need. 

147. The Blind.

The Social Work Year Book for 1941 estimates that there are 200,000 to 250,000 blind persons in the United States, and the Keystone The blind can now earn their living by broom making and other fairly light occupations. 

White House Conference Preliminary Committee Report gives the estimated number of children who are either blind or with only partial vision at 60,000.¹ The official 1930 census report gave a total of only 63,593 blind persons.2 Blindness is, therefore, a serious problem as regards number, as well as regards the special handicap which the blind must surmount in becoming economically independent, and in living in society. 

The causes of blindness are varied. Some blindness is congenital, as a result of heredity, or some other cause. Inherited blindness may appear either at birth, or as a progressive defect, for 

¹P. 447. 

2Statistical Abstract of the United States, United States Department of Commerce, 1936. p. 79.

example, nearsightedness. At least 80 per cent of blindness, however, results from lack of attention to the eyes at birth, or from injuries, accidents, inflammation following measles or other diseases, or degenerative changes due to old age. 

It is now generally recognized that the blind need special training and care, which can be secured only in schools set apart for that purpose; and that this training should not begin at the end of the school period, but at the very beginning, with kindergarten work, for best results are obtained from those who have had their senses of touch and sound developed in their early years. This scientific training cannot be provided at home, and although day schools for the blind are a real boon for many (and there are still too few available), it is often best that patients should not be left with their own families. Early training may be neglected at home, or they may be pampered and otherwise spoiled for fighting their own way in the world in later years. Yet institutional life is almost equally detrimental for later life in the world, for there is the danger of "institutionalization.”

In training the blind, particular attention is now paid to physical education, and instruction in music and in industrial arts, such as basket making, weaving, and metal work. For many years, special books have also been provided for the blind, so that blind persons are no longer dependent on the kindness of others for their entertainment or instruction. These books are expensive, however, and free libraries for the blind are often sadly in need of funds. Many blind persons have made excellent success in life, though they generally need the help of an institution to find them work, as they are particularly handicapped in obtaining employment.

But modern care of the blind does not end with the provision of schools and libraries. The National Committee for the Prevention of Blindness, organized in 1915, is doing all possible to investigate causes of blindness or impaired vision, to advocate measures for their elimination, and to extend preventive work. The increase of skilled medical attention for all children of school age, and the free testing of eyesight and provision of glasses, help

THE DEFECTIVE 

193 greatly to diminish preventable blindness. Appropriate factory laws have greatly diminished eyestrain, also, and the number of accidents at work. Babies' sore eyes (opthalmia neonatorum) has been almost eliminated by compulsory dropping of a weak (1 per cent) solution of nitrate of silver into every infant's eyes at birth.

For the Catholic blind, Catholic literature is provided in the United States through the Xavier Free Publication Society for the Blind, in New York City. There is a great need, however, for the extension of these services, for translations of suitable books, for the establishment of more Catholic schools and societies for the blind to provide the necessary Catholic environmental influence, and also for a greater number of Catholics to take a friendly interest in blind persons living in their neighborhoods. 

148. The Deaf. 3 It is estimated that there are at least ten million people in the United States who are "hard of hearing," and that there are 

90,000 to 120,000 deaf-mutes in the country. Although deafness is a severe handicap in life, it is not one that is insurmountable.

There are various causes for deafness. One of them is heredity, particularly as a result of marriage between close relatives. 

Heredity, however, is usually considered a minor factor, and the principal causes are meningitis, brain fever, primary ear disease, and various contagious or infectious diseases, such as measles and diphtheria.

The deaf who are taught lip reading or a combination of lip reading and sign language, can usually take their place in the world and suffer little from their handicap. Special schools, however, are needed to give them this necessary help, and usually special instruction is provided in those arts and industries where deafness is least a handicap. As with blindness, increased facilities for the medical inspection of children is greatly diminishing deafness, particularly when due to primary ear disease. 

The deaf and dumb are, of course, in a much more serious posi- 

3Cf. Social Work Year Book, 1941, pp. 167, 168.

tion, but it has been discovered in modern times that mutism is sometimes due merely to lack of adequate training. If the deafness occurred in childhood and there was no special training, the deaf person was necessarily mute, for we learn to speak largely from hearing others.

The national organization for social work among the deaf is the American Federation of Organizations for the Hard of Hearing. There are several Catholic schools for the deaf in the United 

States, and there is also the Catholic association of the Knights 

International Newsreel 

Testing the hearing of school children. de l'Epée, which has branches in most large cities. In addition to providing chaplains for work among the deaf, this association maintains a fund for the education of the deaf, and for the relief of the deaf poor and the widows and orphans of deceased members. Unable to hear oral instruction and often forced to attend special schools under non-Catholic auspices, the deaf are in great need of Catholic missionary workers. 

149. The Crippled, and those with permanently broken health.

The White House Conference Preliminary Committee Reports estimate the number of crippled children in the United States as

THE DEFECTIVE 

195 between 289,000 and 365,000. The causes of crippling are many, and range from hereditary defects, to deformities due to tuberculosis, rickets, paralysis, arthritis, prenatal injury, birth accidents, and accidents after birth. Happily, the number of children who are crippled as a result of malnutrition and other effects of poverty is now greatly diminished, due to the more widespread medical and material assistance offered to mothers and prospective mothers. 

The treatment of cripples is, of course, provided mainly through hospitals. Yet more than medical treatment is needed to make These crippled children are enjoying themselves under the best of care. a cripple financially independent in adult life. Vocational training can often rehabilitate adults to take their place in industry, though not necessarily in the occupation they formerly pursued. 

Handicapped children, however, require not only industrial train-. ing to fit them to earn a livelihood whenever possible, but also such education as is compatible with their state of health, and often must be helped to become accustomed to whatever orthopedic appliances they may have to wear. Sometimes day schools are sufficient for their needs, possibly with the provision of special transportation and suitable desks and other equipment; but often it is best for a child to become a resident in a special institution, despite the danger of the "institutionalizing" effect. In this respect Catholic cripples are better provided for than other Catholic 

SP. 453.

defectives. There are numerous Catholic hospitals and institutions in the United States to care for them. Yet Catholics can assist cripples not only by seeing that every Catholic patient in a non- Catholic institution has adequate provision for the practice of his religion, but by visiting those afflicted and helping to make their lives a little brighter, and by providing them with suitable employment whenever possible. To be able to assist in the cost of his own support has brought joy and a new interest in life to many a cripple. Then, too, some cripples would be equally well cared for in a foster home as in an institution, and Catholic families who would volunteer such assistance would assuredly be performing a true act of mercy.

The same remarks apply to help which may be afforded to those people who are permanently sick and disabled, though not necessarily crippled. 

150. The Mental Deficients. 

Mental deficients, or feeble-minded, are those who have never been of average intelligence. Their bodies may have normal growth, but their brains are either underdeveloped from birth, or development ceases at a very early age. Mental deficients are usually classified into three main types: 

1. Idiots, whose mental age never goes beyond that of a child 

2 to 3 years old, and who are unable to preserve themselves from ordinary physical dangers. 

2. Imbeciles, whose maximum mental age is that of a child of 7 years, and who consequently are never capable of managing their own affairs. 

3. Feeble-minded or morons, whose mental development is above that of imbeciles, but who never exceed the mentality of a child of 12 years.

Since there is no sharp dividing line between the normal person and the high-grade moron, it is difficult to estimate the number of mental deficients in the United States. In 1938 there were 

114,470 mentally deficient and epileptic patients in state institutions, and the Social Work Year Book for 1933 gives as a con- Statistical Abstract of the United States, United States Department of Commerce, 1940, p. 73.

THE DEFECTIVE 

197 servative estimate at least one million definitely mentally deficient persons. Other estimates state that probably the total number of mental deficients and insane people together does not exceed one in every 250 of the population, with not more than one moron to every 500 of population, and only about one idiot and imbecile to every 2,000 of population.s 

Heredity undoubtedly plays an important part in mental deficiency, but there are many other causes also, such as prenatal injuries or defects, undernourishment or mental worry of the mother, alcoholic or drug degeneration of the mother, postnatal accident, or bad environment which is not conducive to mental development, for example, if the mother were herself mentally deficient and could not give proper attention to her child's mental needs.

Mental deficients need protection from harmful influences all their life, for they rarely attain the measure of prudence necessary to take care of their own affairs. Special schools are often provided for them by local education authorities, sometimes they are put into foster homes, and sometimes they are placed in colonies or in institutions. If given education in some suitable employment, such as agricultural work for boys, and domestic service for girls, and if trained in good habits, high-grade morons are capable of earning their own living, and of leading useful lives in the world. 

Supervision is, however, necessary, for they never attain normal prudence and social stability. 

151. The Insane.

The insane are those who developed mentally in a normal manner in early life, but whose mind later became diseased. In 1936 the total number of patients in United States hospitals for mental disease was 432,131. There are many types of insanity, some mild, and others dangerously violent.

Insanity may be inherited, that is, one may be born with a predisposition to become insane at a certain age. In many cases it is 'P. 288. 

8Cf. Dr. Charles Bernstein, The Social Care of the Mentally Deficient, p. 5, National 

Catholic Welfare Conference, 1930; also Stanley P. Davies, Social Control of the Mentally 

Deficient, p. 56; G. B. Mangold, Social Pathology, pp. 369-370. "Patients in Hospitals for Mental Disease, 1936, U. S. Dept. of Commerce, Bureau of 

Census, 1938, p. 2.

caused by sudden shock and violent emotion, or results from illness, worry, overwork, immorality, or excessive indulgence in drugs or alcohol.

It is now realized that insanity is a disease to which anyone is liable, and for which there is often a remedy, provided the patient is put under skilled observation at an early stage. It is a true sign of modern progress that the stigma which once attached to insanity is gradually disappearing, and the time seems to be approaching when a doctor will be as loath to pronounce a person incurably insane, as he is now reluctant to state that any other illness baffles him.

Effective treatment for insanity can usually be provided only in a hospital and not when the patient lives at home. Once the hospital psychiatrists have discovered the cause of the malady, frequently only time and careful treatment are needed to effect a cure. 

152. Institutions versus Colonies.

Since so large a proportion of defectives must live in institutions, it is appropriate to discuss briefly what is known as the colony or cottage system.

When persons have to be taken from their family circle, the nearer the substitution approaches family life, the happier and more natural will be their mode of living. For this reason, when indoor relief must be given to dependents, the foster home is considered best for their welfare, whenever this method of taking care of them is feasible. Similarly, placement in a foster home is sometimes the best method of caring for the handicapped, the mentally defective, and the juvenile delinquent. 

If more supervision or attention is required than could be given in a foster home, then the cottage system or colony usually is preferable to the institution. In the cottage system the inmates live in groups of thirty or less, each group in an individual house. 

Each house is in charge of a matron or house mother, who supervises the whole establishment. The houses are placed in colonies, close enough to each other for centralized management, but the life led in each cottage is as near a normal family life as is

THE DEFECTIVE 

199 possible. Each member is encouraged to take a personal interest in his or her particular cottage, and at the same time individuality is allowed full play. A spirit of loyalty to the group is fostered, as also mutual kindness and self-helpfulness. Necessary work in the cottage is performed by the members according to their individual capacities. Some do housework, others, gardening; others work in the colony workshops, so that they learn a skilled trade. 

The work of adult defectives in colonies frequently results in their being almost wholly self-supporting financially.10

This system has been found most successful for orphans, the aged, and for such cripples, deaf, dumb, or blind who cannot find foster homes but who are in need of the special medical care which only an institution could provide. It is also the most effective way of training many juvenile delinquents and those suffering from the milder forms of mental deficiency and insanity. The value of the system has been realized only lately, but an increasing number of institutions are adopting it, and it will probably become much more widespread within the next few years. 

10Cf. Bernstein, op. cit., p. 11.

Chapter XVI DELINQUENCY 

154. A delinquent is one who, by committing a felony or a misdemeanor, transgresses a civil law. 

To enable men to live harmoniously in civic society, the state must pass laws conformable to the natural law, and to insure good order it is the duty of citizens to obey these laws, and a complementary duty of the state to prevent transgressions by punishing the transgressor. Those who, by willful act or omission, violate the just laws of the state, commit either a crime or a misdemeanor; they do not fulfill their duties as social beings and become, in the language of social service, "delinquent." This is not to say, however, that all offenses against law are sins: a law may be necessary to maintain good order for example, a parking ordinance and the transgression of it will be an offense but not sinful. Similarly, there are many sins against which the state has no enactment.
