The word crime must be interpreted in accordance with the gravity of the offense, and delinquents are not necessarily criminals. Serious crime—such as burglary, robbery, homicide, brutal assault, treason, and arson—is known as a felony, and delinquents who commit felonies may be termed criminals, for they have willfully and seriously injured or done violence to another's person or property. Delinquencies which are lesser violations of the law are called misdemeanors.

We must not forget, also, that many an innocent person is charged with crime; and many a criminal goes free and undetected by society. 200

DELINQUENCY 

201 1932 1933 1934 1935 1936 1937 1938 

Number of Inmates in Prisons and Reformatories in the United States¹ 137,516 137,997 136,810 138,316 144,180 145,038 152,654 

155. The prevention and punishment of delinquency is one of the state's biggest social problems. 

In the prevention and punishment of crime, the primary obligation of the state is that of justice, for the protection of society, that is, for the common good; secondarily, there is the obligation of charity, for the state must also consider the good of the individual delinquent as a member of society. The state, therefore, must not merely make wise enactments, and punish those who transgress them, but in meting out punishment, while its first aim must be to restore order and prevent hurt to society, it must also seek to reform the transgressor.

Crime should be punished with three objectives in view. First, punishment must be retributive, but expiation should not be excessive nor necessarily in proportion to the crime. Second, it should have a deterring effect on the prisoner himself and on society. Punishment should be as unpleasant as is compatible with justice and the other objectives of punishment, so that the offender himself will be inclined to make a better choice in the future and will refrain from committing the same crime a second time; and society will be protected because others also, knowing the punishment, will be deterred from committing it. Third, punishment should lead to the reformation of the offender. When once a man comes before the court, the problem should cease to be the general one of elimination of crime but the particular problem of how to prevent that individual offender from repeating the same offense, while punishing him in a fitting manner. The court should make the punishment fit the criminal rather than the crime, and sometimes reformation is effected more readily without imprisonment or by a more lenient sentence. 

1 Statistical Abstract, 1940, p. 77.

156. The state has a right to punish delinquents, and may even exact capital punishment when a heinous crime has been committed and the offender's guilt is clearly established. 

To insure good order and the carrying out of the natural law, the state has power to make just laws and the duty to see that these laws are executed; and it has direct authority to punish any acts which are injurious to society. 

As regards capital punishment, there can be no doubt that where a crime of sufficient gravity has been committed, and where the offender's guilt is clearly established, the state can legitimately administer the death penalty. The fear of death is universal. Where it is the practice of a state to have judgment swiftly following upon the apprehension of an offender, if the judgment is that of death, the sentence should be speedily carried out, without any emotional "glory" often attendant upon the exploitation of "news" in the press. The death sentence is an adequate punishment for a heinous crime, and punishment meted out in the manner just described would be very effective in deterring others from committing a similar offense. In seeking to achieve the third objective of punishment, and to reform the delinquent, we must be fully aware, first, of the chief occasions of crime and also of the remedies for them; second, of the causes of the particular offenses; and third, of the best remedies which may be applied in the individual case. 

157. Delinquency may be due to one or more of the following causes: 

1. Defective religious and moral education. Most crime is due to a defective moral or mental attitude, resulting from lack of true notions of rights and duties toward God, toward one's neighbor, and toward oneself, and to a neglect of training in habits of virtue and self-restraint. Such a knowledge and training are primarily acquired in the home with the assistance of the school, as explained in Chapter XII. If the home life of a child is defective, or if the educational system neglects to realize its duty to promote religious and moral education, that child runs a serious risk of becoming delinquent.

DELINQUENCY 

203 2. Defective home life. Children are particularly susceptible to the influence of environment. Where parents are shiftless, ignorant, lacking in self-restraint, emotional, immoral, or delinquent, and for these reasons neglect to train their children in habits of virtue, but give them instead the disadvantages both of bad example and bad environment, their children are particularly likely to become delinquent. Bad housing conditions, uncongenial home life, working mothers, the break-up of the home on account of divorce, are all home factors, too, which may lead to children growing up undisciplined, associating with bad companions, and ultimately adopting a criminal career. 

3. Industrial conditions and lack of vocational training. The very visible discrepancies between the rich and the poor, in combination with present-day materialism and overvaluation of wealth, often lead to temptations to steal, embezzle, and commit frauds. A child who takes up a "blind-alley" occupation is without a trade when he grows up, and is usually forced to adopt some occupation which will give him less than a living wage, and often subject him to the temptations just mentioned. 

4. Commercial amusements and the press. Much crime is suggested by newspaper reports, cheap magazines, moving pictures, and plays. Gambling, intemperance, drug addiction also lead to crime by weakening morale and conscience, and inciting to anger, lust, and thieving to obtain money to continue the vice. Most of these circumstances can be largely eliminated by a sane censorship of the theater, the screen, and the press, and by a better provision of wholesome noncommercial recreational facilities. 

5. Public attitude toward crime and the penal system. The social viewpoint of a community, its moral attitude toward crime, and its conception of the sanctity of life, property and other rights and social obligations, naturally influence the prevalency of crime. 

The integrity or corruption of police systems and just or lax court procedure, are also determining factors. Political conditions are partly to blame for the inefficiency of police methods in the United States, for a change is usually made in the head of the police force whenever a new city administration begins. This con-

dition should be remedied, and the judicial system should be made entirely free from the taint of bribery and corruption. 

Then, too, while the greatest care should be taken to prevent delinquency and to remove the causes which lead to it, transgressions should be followed by immediate arrest and speedy prosecution. Prisoners should always be separated according to age and sex; first offenders should never be placed with habitual criminals, nor should those who have committed only small offenses be associated with greater criminals, nor again, should the unconvicted be placed with the convicted. Acme 

Sing Sing football team playing against United States Naval Militia team.

Prisoners, in the foreground, watch the game. 

6. Physical and mental disabilities. If defectives are neglected or discriminated against, they sometimes develop an antisocial character and a distorted view on life, and commit crimes. 

7. Climatic influence. There are more crimes against property in the winter, and more crimes against persons in the summer. 

Humidity, intense heat, electrical conditions in the air, lead to nervousness and irritability, and hence sometimes to crimes of violence. As explained in Chapter III, however, the influence of geography not only on crime, but in other respects also, has been greatly exaggerated.

DELINQUENCY 

205 8. Inheritance of criminal predispositions (crime itself is not inherited).

Sometimes crime is attributed to heredity, but our theory of life, which recognizes the Creator's design for man and man's possession of free will, is against this view, and all real scientists reject it. Yet some predispositions to criminal action may be inherited, and these may be developed by bad environment and example, as has been mentioned. These tendencies, however, can be overcome by correct environment and education. The mentally deficient are often supposed to have inherited criminal tendencies, yet studies of the mental status of prisoners have shown that only about 7 or 8 per cent are mentally deficient.2 

BI 

Ewing Galloway

Sing Sing Prison. A classroom for illiterate prisoners. 

158. How delinquents are dealt with in the United States. 

There are three kinds of penal institutions in America for older offenders: the county jail, and the state and federal prisons. Those awaiting trial go to county jail, as the local prison is called, where women are separated from men, but where first offenders usually come into contact with habitual or incorrigible criminals, and where unconvicted and convicted are also not as a rule separated. 

2Stanley P. Davies, Social Control of the Mentally Deficient, p. 175.

The social history of each prisoner is usually examined, and an endeavor is made to discover the cause of the crime, so that fitting remedies may be applied. Often physical, psychiatric, and psychological examinations are also given before trial to determine causes other than social. In this way feeble-minded and insane persons are separated from prisoners of other categories, and are given special treatment.

Upon conviction, those sentenced to small periods of imprisonment, up to nine months or so, usually remain in the county jail, Ewing Galloway

Radio provides entertainment for convicts. A Sing Sing prisoner listening on an ear phone. Loudspeakers are not used since they would annoy those who do not care to listen. whereas others either pass to a state penitentiary, or if they have broken a Federal law (a law binding on all the citizens of the United States) they go to a Federal prison. Sometimes prisoners are placed on probation, a term we shall explain later. — - Best modern penal methods aim at reforming the delinquent not merely hardening him in building up his morale, instead of breaking it down. Attention is usually given to the physical training of prisoners, so that they are put into good physical condition; to their education and industrial training, so that they can earn an honest livelihood when freed; and to their moral and religious instruction. This modern idea has led to the establishment of prison camps and farms, to probation, parole, and the indeterminate sentence. 

159. Prison camps and farms do much to build up the health and morale of delinquents.

Prison camps originated as a result of overcrowding in prisons

DELINQUENCY 

207 and the necessity for finding additional accommodation. In these camps offenders live in huts and work on the construction of new roads, the reclamation of land, or afforestation. This system costs the community much less than the expensive upkeep of prisons, and is much more helpful than ordinary prison conditions for building up morale and physical strength. A similar plan is that of the farms which are sometimes attached to prisons, where prisoners live in houses after the cottage or colony plan detailed in the preceding chapter. Most women prisoners are now looked after in colonies of this nature, particularly when they come under the Federal administration. Here they are taught to take care of a home and how to make home life attractive. 

160. Probation is a system which has usually been very successful.

Thirty-two states in the Union and the Federal Government have adopted what is known as the probation system in dealing with certain types of adult delinquents and all the states but one provide for juvenile probation. When a delinquent is placed on probation he is allowed to remain free, but is under the supervision of a court official called a probation officer. The probationer reports to this officer at regular intervals, and usually restrictions are placed on him as to his recreation, companions, and retirement from the streets at night. If he fails to carry out the probation regulations, he is taken before the court again, and usually committed. The probation officer's duty is to help the offender to reform by taking a friendly interest in him, finding him work where necessary, and looking after his material and mental welfare to the best of his ability.

Since the primary consideration of punishment is to protect society, and only secondarily to help the delinquent, probation should not have exclusive application where there has been positive injury to person or property, for here retribution is required; nor when the delinquent is a positive, active menace to society, a habitual offender, for example. Probation is only successful with certain types of delinquents, and when the probation officer is conscientious in the performance of his obligation to the court,

to society, and to the delinquent. In many cities, the St. Vincent de Paul Society, the Knights of Columbus, and other organizations provide Catholic probation officers for Catholic delinquents. 

161. Parole and the indeterminate sentence are also features of modern penology which may be successful with certain types of prisoners. 

The indeterminate sentence is one where, instead of a definite term of imprisonment being passed, a sentence is given stating the minimum and the maximum number of years the delinquent is to be imprisoned. After the expiration of the minimum term, the prison administration board or the court judge has the option of releasing the prisoner on parole at any time up to the fixed maximum term of imprisonment, when, of course, he must be released. In this way prisoners can be kept in custody long enough to make scientific treatment of individual needs possible and useful, and immediately the delinquent shows signs of reform, provided the minimum term of imprisonment is accomplished, he may be released. This method thus gives a delinquent a powerful motive for good behavior in prison and sincere attempt at reformation. His behavior is generally measured by the merit or progressive-stage system, which allows a prisoner to obtain certain privileges or to lose them, depending on his conduct. 

Parole, however, is not pardon. A prisoner on parole is subjected to the same supervision as a person on probation, and he receives the same assistance. If he does not behave properly, the parole may be withdrawn at any time before the maximum sentence has expired, and he is again put into prison to serve his full term of imprisonment.

The dangers inherent in both the indeterminate sentence and in parole lie in the way such systems can play into the hands of dishonest politicians. 

162. Habitual delinquents are sometimes given imprisonment for life.

Habitual delinquents, or recidivists, as they are called, are a special problem, for they appear incorrigible, and society needs

DELINQUENCY 

209 greater protection from them than from the occasional offender. 

Some states deal with recidivism by enforcing imprisonment for life after a certain number of felonies have been committed. 

Where each of the felonies have been singly of relative unimportance, this sentence would seem to be excessive punishment, particularly as the judge has no power to mitigate the sentence. 

But the repeated serious violations of the law may in certain cases involve either a contempt for it, or a neglect of reformation after repeated opportunities, or both; and the menace to society by injury to its law or to its members may be such as to justify the permanent removal of the menace. Such laws have, then, a great deterrent value and are a most efficient protection if adequately enforced. Our modern conditions emphatically call for them. 

163. Prisoners should be paid at least a small amount for prison labor.

Convicted prisoners are usually given some form of work to keep them occupied during the greater part of the day. Where a man is not a skilled craftsman, under best prison conditions he is trained in work which will fit him to take his place in the outside world when he is released. It would seem that prisoners should have an incentive to work at a suitable occupation by payment of a small amount in wages which they might be allowed to spend as they please; and that a certain portion of their earnings should either be sent to dependents who might otherwise become a charge on public charities, or be allowed to accumulate against their release from prison. The question then arises as to what should be done with the product of prison labor. The needs of the prison should be provided for, but any other work might be profitably sold to outside agencies, provided it is not disposed of at prices which would undercut private manufacturers. The work of prisoners will often enable a prison to be self-supporting, but it should be allotted as part of a scientific program of reformation, and not to make the prison "pay."

164. Juvenile delinquents should be given special care and attention by the courts.

Since most criminals begin their delinquent career before reaching maturity, if it is possible to reform young delinquents permanently, a great number of potential crimes will be obviated. 

Juveniles are still in the formative stage, and whether or not they turn permanently to a life of crime largely depends on the habits to which they are trained. 

Ewing Galloway

Juvenile court. The probation officer (lady) stands beside the boy, while the judge has a heart-to-heart talk with him. Compare this scene with the court for adults, shown on page 66.

Special courts, judges, and probation officers are usually provided for juvenile delinquents, a term which includes children up to the age of 16 or 18. If possible, an attempt is made to adjust the child to normal behavior by placing him on probation, with special probation officers who make it their duty to be real friends of the child, to visit him regularly, and to do everything possible to help him. Where it is found that a child will not respond to this treatment, or where home conditions are not satisfactory, the delinquent is placed in a special reformatory school, usually run on the colony plan. Delinquents older than juveniles, but under 21 years of age, are often treated separately as well as "young persons."

DELINQUENCY 

211 The St. Vincent de Paul Society, the Knights of Columbus, the 

Catholic Women's League, and similar societies help Catholic juvenile delinquents in most cities by providing "Big Brothers" and "Big Sisters" who act as probation officers, visit Catholic inmates of institutions, and generally do all that a real elder brother or sister would do for an erring younger member of their family.

Chapter XVII THE RACE PROBLEM AND THE IMMIGRANT 

166. Many nationalities compose the American nation. 

No other country in the world is composed of people of so many different nationalities as our own. We all know that the Indians are the native Americans, but from the advent of Columbus in 1492, right down to the present day, people of all races have been continually arriving on our shores. First came the English, Scottish, Irish, Spanish, and French; Negroes were brought in as slaves, though occasionally members of this race also entered as freemen. Then, as the country was opened up, an invasion from all nations of the world began. Between 1901 and 1910 alone, nearly 9,000,000 immigrants came to the United States, and although this was the highest figure for any decade, there were over 4,000,000 immigrants in the 1921-1930 period, and the Report of the Commissioner General of Immigration shows that the total number of immigrants between the years 1820 and 1930 was 

37,762,012 — 32,276,346 coming from Europe; 4,241,429 from 

Canada and South America; 903,195 from Asia; and 341,042 from other continents. 

167. Causes of immigration are various, but chiefly economic.

Immigrants came to the United States for many reasons. Some had been banished from their country for political, religious, or other reasons, and took up their social life again in the New World; others came voluntarily because of differences at home regarding politics or religion, or because they had a roving disposition and a spirit of adventure, or, as was most frequently the case, because their country did not offer them a livelihood, or be- 212

cause they thought that there were greater opportunities for selfadvancement in the United States. 

168. Despite restrictions which have been placed on immigration, there is a large number of foreigners and colored people in our midst.

At first the country welcomed everyone who wished to come, but the number of immigrants of all types, races, and characters increased so rapidly that since the year 1882 several laws have been passed to prevent certain types of immigrants from entering the country. As the law now stands Asiatics and Malayans are excluded, and the number of people of any other nationality who can enter the country as immigrants each year is restricted to 2 per cent of the number of that nationality which was recorded in the census of 1890. This "quota law" entirely excludes immigration from certain southeastern European peoples, and discriminates to some extent against the Latin races, the Slavs, and the Irish, because the greater proportion of peoples in the United States in 1890 were of British and German extraction. Further restriction makes it necessary for every immigrant to present a health certificate and a certificate of good character, to be able to read and write and, finally, to be in possession of a definite amount of money upon arrival. The latter requirement has only recently been made, and at the present time few people are entering the country other than for visits of short duration. In fact, the total immigration for 1935 was only 34,956, and in that year 9,329 more people left the United States permanently than entered it. In 1932, as many as 112,786 more people left the country than came here.

Despite immigration restrictions, the 1930 census shows that 

34,703,183 inhabitants, or about 32 per cent of our total population, are white people of foreign parentage. Over 21 millions of these were born in the United States, but 13,366,407 were foreignborn, although the 1930 Report of the Commissioner of Naturalization stated that 58.5 per cent of the latter category were already naturalized, and an additional 9.3 per cent had taken out "first papers." 

'Handbook of Labor Statistics, 1936, p. 95.

Besides these white people of foreign parentage, there were living in the United States in 1930, according to the census report, nearly 12 million Negroes, about 139,000 Japanese, and 75,000 Chinese, as well as several thousand other colored peoples. The total number of foreign-born peoples, both white and colored in the United States in 1930, was 14,202,149, and of these 864,865, or 6.6 per cent, were unable to speak English. 

169. The presence of so many foreign peoples and colored races in the country presents serious problems, but immigrants have done much for the advancement of the 

United States.

The majority of the Negroes in our own country are descended from those who have been here for generations, and since their number is so great also, we shall discuss separately the problem they create, and confine our attention for the moment to the immigrant. We have neglected the problem of Oriental immigration because the relative number of Orientals in the country is small, and also because they are largely confined to the Pacific Coast.

Although immigration is now practically at a standstill, it will, nevertheless, be useful to discuss the difficulties which arise from the influx of foreigners to the country, because the immigration laws will probably be relaxed again in the future, and also because many of those who came within recent years are still a social problem. It must not be forgotten, however, that many of our best-known public men have been immigrants; that many immigrants have been professional people and highly skilled craftsmen; and that even the poorer and less-endowed classes have supplied the demand for labor and have done necessary work which native-born Americans did not wish to perform. 

170. The first problem of the immigrant is that of providing him with a home and a livelihood.

The first difficulty in connection with the entry of immigrants into the United States is the fact that many have been without resources and have spoken little or no English. Upon arrival they have usually settled in the poorer quarters of large cities, and

because most of them were rural peoples, or unskilled, they have welcomed any occupation they could find, and have taken up the most menial and underpaid occupations. Ignorant of the language and the law, people of their own race have frequently exploited them under the guise of friendship, finding them miserably paid employment and exacting fees entirely out of proportion to the services they rendered. Eventually many immigrants took advantage of better conditions elsewhere, moved to other districts, and found employment for which they were more suited by training and ability. However, sweated labor, woman and child labor, સામાજી 

U.S. Treas. Dept. 

Immigrants entering the United States. Many arrive poverty stricken and bewildered. an increase in slum population, juvenile delinquency, disunion in family life, and destitution, have been the inevitable results of the immigrant's need of earning a livelihood immediately upon arrival. 

171. The Americanization of the immigrant is the next big social problem.

The assimilation of immigrants into the life and customs of the country is the next problem to be considered. If the people of a country are to form a unified social group within the state, they must be at least somewhat homogeneous in habits and outlook. 

Yet while many immigrants have been of an educated class, have spoken our language, and have been able to adapt them-

selves readily to our ways, very many others have been illiterate, or have come from countries whose language, laws, customs, and ideals are very different from our own. They have formed colonies, where those of the same nationality live in close association, speak their own language, eat their national foods, and follow their own social customs. Their conservatism and clannishness has been a great obstacle to their absorption and Americanization. Orientals provide the worst problem, for their notions of God, of marriage, the home, and the state are all different from our own concepts: they lose the social-control restraints of their own country, and they frequently fail to understand and to conform to ours. 

Yet immigrants should be encouraged to live together in these national groups, for they provide them with the companionship of those whom they understand, and make the transition from their own way of living to ours much less painful. Moreover, immigrants should be encouraged to preserve the best in the culture which they have brought with them from their native lands: their folklore and dances, their language, and their distinctive occupational pursuits, for it is precisely the mixture of peoples in the United States which makes our culture so rich. 

172. Among the immigrants themselves, a domestic problem is frequently created. 

A third problem of immigration is a domestic one. The children of immigrants attend American schools, and thus come into contact with American ideals and customs much more quickly than do their parents. Naturally wishing to conform to the school and play groups to which they belong, they frequently come into conflict with their social life at home, and even lose respect for their parents because of their differing social habits. Moreover, they speak English as well as any child of American parents, and often fail to learn the language used by their family. Often, therefore, they grow up undisciplined outside school hours; the disrespect for their parents leads to disrespect for the law, and the children of immigrants very frequently become delinquent in this manner. 

Then too, the quota immigration laws have led to much domestic distress because of the separation of families. Relatives of immigrants already here have been unable to come because they

could not conform to quota laws, or could not furnish the necessary certificates. Other members of immigrant families have been deported on arrival, and even if a deportation is for a purely technical reason, perhaps due to the fault of American officials in other countries, a deportee is never allowed to enter the country again. Such family separations lead to bigamy, desertion, and cases of nonsupport of families left in our midst. 

173. Immigrants should be helped by friendly assistance and by special facilities to hasten their Americanization. 

Immigrants are particularly in need of friendship on their arrival in our country, people who will find them a suitable dwelling place and occupation, who will teach them, help them to understand our ways, and will find them friends among their own countryfolk.

The Immigrant Publication Society, founded in 1914, collects and distributes practical information, and has greatly helped immigrants through such books as the Guide to the United States for the Immigrant, and Makers of America. Settlement houses, the Knights of Columbus, the Kolping houses, the Y.M.C.A., and the Y.W.C.A., the foreign literature sections of many of our public libraries, and the special classes for foreigners in citizenship and in the history of the United States, have also given them invaluable aid. Catholics are well provided for, further, by the N.C.W.C. 

Bureau of Immigration. This bureau establishes working contacts with responsible Catholic welfare societies in many countries abroad, is notified of the date of arrival of Catholic immigrants, and aids them from the time they begin their journey until they are happily settled over here. All immigrants are free to call upon this bureau for information or help in connection with deportation troubles, reëntry into the country after a holiday abroad, or any other matters in connection with their life in the United States. 

174. Catholics should help Catholic immigrants in every possible way.

Because Catholic immigrants sometimes settle in non-Catholic or anti-Catholic environments, are ignorant of the whereabouts of the Church, cannot understand the language, and are some-

times given the impression that to be good citizens, they must abandon their religion, Catholics should do all possible to help them. A few Catholics in each parish should make it their special duty to help all immigrants on their arrival in the district, to give them advice, introduce them to the parish priest, acquaint them with the hours of church services, find them suitable Catholic friends, and show them that it is certainly not necessary to Keystone

Alien (center) taking the oath of allegiance. The two witnesses must swear that they have known him for the required length of time. 

abandon their religion in order to be American citizens rather that their religion will help them to become good citizens, and that many of the best Americans are zealous Catholics as well. 

175. Immigrants may attain the full privileges of American citizenship by naturalization.

Provided his immigration papers are in order, every immigrant may remain in the country as long as he wishes, whether or not he attains citizenship by naturalization. In order to vote, however and obtain the privileges of a citizen of the United States, he must renounce his allegiance to his own country and become a naturalized subject of ours. Any time after arrival he may go before a court and take out his "first papers," provided he is at least 18 years of age, pays a fee of $5, and promises to renounce his allegiance to any other government. After he has been here five

years, but not less than two years after taking out "first papers," the immigrant may again go before the court and ask to be declared an American citizen. His request must be accompanied by a fee of $10 and an affidavit given by two citizens who certify that he is 21 years of age, or over, that they have known him to be a resident of the state where application is made for one year or more, and that he has been a resident of the United States for at least five years. He must also pass a short test in American history and citizenship. Ninety days after this request has been filed, the immigrant may again appear before the court and receive his naturalization papers, making him an American citizen. 

Foreign-born children automatically become citizens upon the naturalization of their father, and any child who is born in the United States, even though his father be an unnaturalized foreigner, is considered an American citizen by birth. 

176. American Negroes have been very unjustly treated in the past.

It is not within the domain of sociology to trace the advent of Negroes to the United States, or to give the history leading to their final emancipation from slavery in 1865. Suffice it to say that the whole institution of slavery was, of course, an injustice against man's right to liberty, and that conditions of slavery stifled in many of the Negroes any habits of industry and thrift which they may have possessed. That at the time of the emancipation many Negroes were uneducated, and lacking in industrial training, and utterly incapable of earning a livelihood, is not to be wondered at. Frequently they had been forced to labor under such cruel taskmasters that they naturally worked only when driven; the work they were given to do was mostly of the unskilled kind; they could possess nothing of their own, and as the masters were supposed to provide them with everything, they were not required to use foresight in providing for emergencies; their morals were rarely safeguarded in any way; and from the year 1740 onward the various states had passed laws to forbid the instruction of slaves and to impose penalties on those who taught them.

At the time of the emancipation, of course, not all Negroes were

in this condition. Some had always been free men; others had been taught secretly by their masters; others had natural gifts and endowments which they had fostered. But ever since that time, Negroes have been gravely discriminated against in the United States. In nearly all places they have been deprived of opportunity to avail themselves of adequate public education, have had little or no facilities for recreation to recuperate their forces, have been denied the possibility of earning a decent livelihood, obliged to accept conditions that are unfair and unjust, and discriminated against in favor of recent immigrants. Many Negroes have also been unjustly denied the right to vote as American citizens. This discrimination has frequently led to a bitterness on their side, which, of course, is blameworthy but almost to be expected.

We must, however, take into consideration that despite these disabilities, many Negroes have made notable achievements. The long list of Negro inventions and literature is very impressive, and indicative of the false impression many of us have as regards 

Negro ability.

Comparison of Negro and White Population in the United States2 Negro White Other

1930 11,891,143 108,864,207 2,019,696 122,775,046 1920 10,463,131 94,820,915 426,574 105,710,620 177. Negroes have been discriminated against economically, in education, in public places, in housing, and in recreation.

Since no provision was made for the Negro slaves upon their emancipation, we must become reconciled to the Negro continuing to inhabit the same territory as white people, and moreover we must remember that they are as much citizens of the United 

States as any other Americans.

There is much economic discrimination against Negroes. Some employers refuse to give them work at all; others employ them 

2Statistical Abstract, 1936, p. 15.

only for work of an inferior nature; others again pay them a lower rate of wages than is given to white men for similar services. 

Yet the Negro must earn his living as must the white man, he has a right to proper opportunities of advancement, and a living wage for a fair day's work. This economic discrimination has led to the poverty-stricken condition of many of our Negro citizens. AS WE

Ewing Galloway

Statue of Booker T. Washington, founder of 

Tuskegee Institute, a great university for the colored.

Negroes have also been seriously discriminated against in education. In many places in the South they have been entirely excluded from the public schools, and where separate schools are provided for them out of public funds, there is often a great disparity between expenditure on white children and on those of Negro blood. Since the Negro and the white man pay equal taxes,

however, it would seem unjust that they should have unequal educational advantages. In the North they are received in the public schools, but are frequently denied admission to colleges and universities. When opportunities for higher education cannot be easily obtained elsewhere, it must at least be said that the action of such institutions is lacking in charity. 

In the South the Negro is segregated in such public places as theaters, restaurants, hotels, street cars, railroads, and even in churches. Probably the worst offense in this regard is the "Jim 

Crow" car of the Southern railroads. The occupants pay the same as the white people, but are given inferior accómmodation. 

The railroad company, in other words, profits by local prejudice. 

Discrimination is also shown to Negroes in the matter of housing and recreation. Everywhere in America Negroes are restricted in their choice of residence, and the housing that is available for them is mostly dilapidated and grossly overcrowded, and the rents are frequently unfairly high. Where houses made available to 

Negroes are in a state of dilapidation, they are often difficult to put or keep in repair, and the lack of housing accommodation for the race makes for rapid congestion which necessarily increases the normal rate of dilapidation. Apart from the great need of houses for the Negro worker, however, it is obvious that the restriction of Negroes in their choice of residence may at times imply hardship to many of the professional and other classes, who naturally desire the same social environment as middle-class white people. This is a problem which needs serious study. There is also a woeful lack of recreation facilities for the Negroes. Several large cities, such as Detroit, have taken definite steps to solve the recreation problem for the Negro by the provision of special playgrounds and playing fields. Most clubs, golf courses, and tennis courts are barred to colored people, however many of those which are publicly owned. Much of the crime attributed to Negroes is due to this lack of provision for leisure hours, as also inadequate housing facilities, racial discrimination in work and wages, and the widespread antagonism against the race. 

even

178. The chief difficulty in regard to the Negro problem is the racial prejudice of the white people. The Negro has rights which should not be violated, and charity requires that he should be treated with the courtesy due to a human being. 

A class in plain sewing. Tuskegee Institute.

The fact that there are 12 million Negroes in the United States, all of whom are citizens with exactly the same rights as white people, makes the unjust discrimination against the Negro a very 

Theory class in the electrical division, Tuskegee

Institute. 

grave problem which needs serious study. That the problem is complicated, no one will deny, particularly on account of the undesirability of interracial marriages, but the unjust discrimination against Negroes must certainly be abolished.

The racial prejudice against the Negroes was aroused and fostered by the slave trade, as an attempted justification of the evils and the inherent injustice of this economic system. It was continued by the bitterness aroused by the Civil War and the Southerners' loss of their human chattels; and it was spread 

North by southern pride, which resented meeting emancipated slaves on an equal footing. Built upon a natural aversion of white people to the color and features of the Negro, this racial prejudice was made more intense in the economic competition they afforded in the labor market. All unfair discrimination resulting from this prejudice, and the attitude of many toward the Negro race, is lacking both in justice and in charity. 

First, in justice, Negroes have the same natural rights as any other race of men, and these rights must be safeguarded and protected. All mankind, white, yellow, brown, or black, is essentially the same and comes from the same stock that of Adam. Then all men, not those of one color only, were redeemed by Jesus Christ.

Second, it is a violation of charity to use opprobrious epithets to describe the Negro or his acts, to foster feelings of antagonism and aversion, and to humiliate him publicly by odious forms of segregation. A certain degree of separation will, however, usually be recognized as reasonable by both races, and often voluntarily accepted.

Much more serious is the discrimination against Negroes in education, and since canon law requires a Catholic education for the Catholic Negro, every effort must be made that an opportunity for this be equally offered to Negro and White. It would surely be strange if Catholics should refuse Negroes admission to their schools in districts where public and Protestant institutions are willing to receive them. Everything possible should be done to offer proper educational opportunities for the Negro, as well as to treat him in a Christian manner in all social intercourse. No 

Christian can uncharitably discriminate against a Negro without violating the teaching of Christ.

There are so many Negroes in the South that on account of the danger of miscegenation (interracial marriages), and for the maintenance of peace and well-being, continued separation may seem advisable. No one has a right, however, to foster feelings of antagonism and aversion against any human beings on account of their color alone, and separation does not necessarily imply unfair discrimination, for, as just explained, this should never be tolerated.

Yet it must be noted that an absence of racial aversion, or the control of such feelings, does not mean that we are bound to treat every Negro as our dearest friend. We have the right to choose our friends where we will. No man is lacking in charity who does not invite another to share his more intimate life, and the white man can feel charitably disposed toward his colored fellow citizens, and can respect their rights, and act always without unfair discrimination, without giving an extreme demonstration of good will. 

179. A sincere effort must be made to solve the Negro problem.

It would appear that Negroes need sympathy and assistance in their efforts to overcome prejudices, and a keener realization that it will be to their best interests not to imitate the white people in every particular, but to develop their own distinctive culture and manner of living. Their achievements along this line will inevitably bring them recognition of their ability and worth by all civilized peoples.

There can be no doubt, however, that Negroes have made enormous progress in the past seventy years. They have their own churches, colleges, universities, and professional societies, and as we stated previously in this chapter, many Negroes have achieved wonderful success in the fields of literature, art, and mechanical inventions. There are also three influential societies for the promotion of Negro welfare: the National Association for the Advancement of Colored People, which has been particularly active in the defense of Negro rights; the National Urban League, which

has rendered signal service in the solution of problems of recreation, employment, and housing; and the Federation of Colored 

Catholics of the United States, which has 70,000 members. 

Catholics, however, have been singularly negligent in their care of the religious needs of the Negro. Of the 12 million Negroes, only about 200,000 belong to our Faith, and missionaries who have devoted their lives to our colored citizens are as yet wholly inadequate in numbers. The needs of the Negro in America economic, social, and religious — provide a fallow field for Catholic Action. 

---

Chapter XVIII CATHOLIC ACTION 

181. Our need of social life gives us, as social beings, an obligation to further the progress of society as far as we are able.

We now come to the culmination of our study of sociology. We have learned that God created us social beings, but that since He did not endow us with self-sufficiency, we cannot get along without the help of others and we have been created with a positive need of social life.

Had our parents not married and formed a domestic society, we ourselves would not exist. Had we not lived in this domestic family and received a good early training, and continued to have the love and care of our parents, we should probably not enjoy many of the comforts and advantages we have today. Had our family not lived in civil society, and enjoyed the protection of the army, navy, air force, and police, and also the constructive aid of the state in the school facilities it provided for us, the health measures, pure-food laws, laws against monopolies, and the like, we should in all probability have been murdered, or have died of starvation long before this time. And without recreational society, and the school group, and the occupational society from which our family draws its income, and all the other societies to which we belong, we should certainly not be as fortunate as we actually are.

Now, if society helps us in all these things, as social beings we ourselves owe a duty to society to try to further its progress insofar as we are able. 

227

182. The best means by which we, as Catholics, may fill our duty to society is by furthering Catholic Action in every possible way.

Our previous study has shown us that most of the world's evils are due to a lack of attention to social rights and duties and the dictates of the moral law, and it must now be evident to us that no true social progress is possible without giving consideration to God, and therefore to religion. As Catholics, therefore, we must realize that the best and only way by which we can fill our duty to society is by forwarding what is known as "Catholic Action." 

The words Catholic Action provide a new term to express the extension of the Kingdom of Christ, and if we extend Christ's Kingdom, we shall be indeed working for social reform. 

A lengthier and more explicit definition of Catholic Action has been given us by the Holy Father himself, in a letter to the Archbishop of Breslau in 1929, where he said: "Catholic Action is nothing other than the participation of the laity in the apostolate of the hierarchy. In effect, Catholic Action consists not merely of the pursuit of personal Christian perfection, which is, however, before all others its first and greatest end, but it also consists of a true apostolate in which Catholics of every social class participate, coming thus to be united in thought and action around these centers of sound doctrine and multiple social activity, legitimately constituted and, as a result, aided and systained by the authority of the Bishops."

In a letter to a Catholic society in Belgium, Pope Pius XI again amplified for us the meaning of Catholic Action, saying: "Its first consideration should be to make good Christians. But the Christian once trained, must spend outside of himself the life that he has received. He ought to carry everywhere this treasure of Christianity and make it live in every field of life, in the family, and in public life, not excluding politics. For what We wish is that Christ rule on earth as He rules in heaven and that

229 183. We may fill our duty to society in many ways, the most important of which is prayer for ourselves, and trying to become as good as possible in order to give good example to others.

We may fill our duty to society in many ways, each of which provides matter for much thought, and for a much more detailed

The most important duty which we owe to society is that of good example, for since, as was shown in Chapter III, social environment, public opinion, and convention do wield such a mighty influence, we ourselves must inevitably exert influence on others. CATHOLIC ACTION 

231 Holy Father stated in one of the quotations above, "the pursuit of personal Christian perfection" is the "first and greatest end" of Catholic Action. We must therefore pray and make use of all means for our own sanctification. We should, moreover, try positively to perform all our actions as well as possible. In this way we shall be giving good example to our companions, and thus shall be working toward a more perfect social life. 

184. Catholic Action also implies active apostolate. 

If we turn again to the Holy Father's description of Catholic Action we shall see that this "also consists of a true apostolate in which Catholics of every social class participate." And in this statement Pope Pius is but following the words of Scripture, where we read that "Faith without works is dead";³ that we must "do the truth in charity"; and that "he that hath the substance of this world, and shall see his brother in need, and shall shut up his bowels from him: how doth the charity of God abide in him?”5

And again, not only have we the positive command of our Lord Himself to "love our neighbors as ourselves," but we also have 

His terrible warning: "Depart from Me, you cursed, into everlasting fire which was prepared for the devil and his angels. For 

I was hungry, and you gave Me not to eat: I was thirsty, and you gave Me not to drink. I was a stranger, and you took Me not in; naked, and you covered Me not; sick and in prison, and you did not visit Me. . . . Amen I say to you, as long as you did it not to one of these least, neither did you do it to Me."

It would seem, from these quotations, that all who would be Catholics in deed as well as in name, must see to it that they actively promote Catholic Action in every possible way. 

185. The first form which active Catholic Action should take is prayer for others.

In addition to helping our neighbor by good example, therefore, we must consciously strive to do all possible to help him, and the 'James ii. 20. 

'Ephes. iv. 15. 

"First Epistle of St. John, iii. 17. "Cf. Matt. xxii. 39. "Matt. xxv. 41-45.

Eucharistic Congress at Buenos Aires, Argentine. A celebration such as this is really a great public profession of faith, and is a form of Catholic Action.

CATHOLIC ACTION 

233 first way in which we may do this is by praying for him. We can pray to God to have mercy on our relatives, our friends, our rulers, the people in our neighborhood, in our native land, and in the whole world. Those who feel especially called to devote their lives to this form of Catholic Action, of social duty toward others, usually adopt the religious life in a contemplative order. 

186. The second form of active apostolate is some external social duty.

Probably, while we are still at school, there is little we can do to help the world socially other than by prayer and good example, and by the normal friendly help we can give to our family and our friends. We should, however, think of the future, and the ways by which we can help mankind externally are so many that we cannot do more than just indicate a few. Any of these works, if done under the auspices of a bishop, usually as a member of a society authorized to conduct Catholic Action activities, is a means of carrying out Catholic Action. 

1. We can give spiritual help to people, even as lay persons, by moral encouragement and advice when we find this necessary, by fostering religious literature and art, and by financial support of religious institutions. Those who feel especially called to minister to the religious needs of others and perform Catholic Action in this way can become priests, brothers, or nuns. 

2. Education is an important field for Catholic Action. In the school and in later life we should study those subjects which will give us a knowledge of the needs of others, or enable us to answer difficulties. This form of apostolate may involve an interest in religion, sociology, economics, psychology, and other branches of knowledge. We ought, in particular, to foster the study of subjects from the Catholic viewpoint, and this we can do by joining Catholic study clubs when we leave school, by supporting Catholic libraries and radio programs, and by subscribing to Catholic periodicals, newspapers, and books, so that these things may continue to exist and thus promote Catholic education. 

3. The relief of poverty and destitution is another important field. If we would make the care of the poor our lifework, we can become voluntary or paid social workers. The many who will not

wish to do this, however, may be able to sew for the poor, to collect cast-off clothing, or to give them money either directly or by means of the poor-box in the Church, subscriptions taken up at home or in the school, or through a Society such as the Society of St. Vincent de Paul. 

4. Aid to the sick, the disabled, and the defective is also an excellent work of Catholic Action. Catholic doctors and nurses are especially needed to take care of Catholic sick people, those who become disabled either temporarily or permanently, the handicapped, and the mentally deficient. There is likewise need

As part-time workers in this form of Catholic Action, one might become a member of the Red Cross Society, or, remembering that to visit the sick is a corporal work of mercy, one might arrange to visit a hospital, or to read to the blind, or to take some sick person for an outing. 

5. Adults can help the delinquent by taking up work as a lawyer, a probation officer, a policeman, and kindred professions.

CATHOLIC ACTION 

235 They can undertake to visit the local prison, to provide prisoners with entertainment, books, and lectures, to join a Big Brother or a Big Sister society, and in other ways give the delinquent a sincere friendship, and help him to take his place again in society. The Catholic delinquent who has neglected his religious duties needs special friendship and encouragement, for only by returning to the practice of religion will he be truly reformed. 

6. Another sphere of Catholic Action is paid or volunteer recreation work as playground leaders, settlement-house workers, and the like. We have discussed the value of such work in 

Chapter XIII. 

187. The Church is interested in social problems because they concern man not merely from the viewpoint of justice but also from that of charity. 

Some people have been tempted to ask why the Church interests herself with social principles and institutions, since these pertain to men's temporal welfare, and the Church is concerned only with the spiritual. Apart from the question of justice, which is constantly involved here, she is interested in these matters for the following reasons: 

1. The Church's members are not yet enjoying eternal bliss, they are struggling on this earth. Naturally, then, it is her wish that they should enjoy this present life, although in such a way that they do not place an exaggerated value on worldly joys, nor forfeit their eternal happiness for excessive mundane interests. 

Man, in fact, normally needs a certain amount of temporal wellbeing in order to have health, time, and opportunity to look after his spiritual welfare. Ever since the time of our Lord, the Catholic Church has recognized this truth, and the Apostles, monks, priests, and holy men and women have always relieved the poor and those in distress. 

2. To relieve man's social needs is a definite command of Jesus 

Christ, as we have already shown, and the Church is bound to take an interest in His commands, and to enjoin her members to fulfill them. 

3. Social problems are concerned with man's temporal welfare, it is true, but man's body cannot be divorced from his soul. It is

the Church's duty, therefore, to insure that the needs of a man's soul are not jeopardized when his bodily needs are relieved. For this reason the Church is not only interested in laying down principles for social reform, but she likewise fosters Catholic charities, and urges us to support these charities rather than the non- Catholic. For similar reasons it is important that trained Catholic social workers should form part of the personnel of non-Catholic organizations. 

The Paulist Circulating Library, San Francisco. Groups in many city parishes are establishing libraries such as this for their people. By providing good literature they are carrying on an important form of Catholic Action. 

188. Catholic Action is nothing new, it is merely a term to describe a movement which aims at the extension of the Kingdom of Christ, and this Catholics have always had at heart.

Although the term Catholic Action is of comparatively recent origin, a second's thought will convince the reader of the foregoing paragraphs, that all which comes under the term Catholic Action is none other than Christian duty and Christian charity. Men have striven for perfection from the time of Christ - indeed, for long before His coming. People have always been found to pray

CATHOLIC ACTION 

237 for others, and history gives us ample proof of how earnestly the Church has always tried to carry out Christ's command to teach "all things whatsoever I have commanded you," and to help all people in distress, as He illustrated by the parable of the 

Good Samaritan, exhorting them: "Go, and do thou in like manner." Despite the fact that man's rights and duties are so frequently disregarded nowadays, or their violation is considered "clever" rather than offensive, the Church still shows men that to neglect a duty, or to violate the right of another is indeed a sin; and she still continues to give lavish example of charity toward all those in need. In numerous encyclicals also, particularly in the social encyclicals of recent years, 10 the popes have urged us to make a study of social questions. 

In the United States the central organization for Catholic Action is the National Catholic Welfare Conference at Washington. 

Working directly under the bishops, its chief duty is to unite Catholic lay organizations throughout the country. Among its numerous activities it sponsors study clubs, arranges conferences, maintains a world-wide press association for the dissemination of Catholic news and a nation-wide Catholic Hour on the radio; it publishes many educational books, pamphlets, and study outlines for colleges and study groups, in addition to the monthly review, Catholic Action; it gives all possible aid to the immigrant, and it conducts the National Catholic School of Social Service for the education of women social workers. All organizations, however, which forward any of the numerous activities outlined in this chapter are, of course, fostering Catholic Action.
