Appendix I THE POSTULATES OF SOCIOLOGY 

Note: In Chapter I we set down certain postulates which a study of man and his social relations from the Christian viewpoint must necessarily take for granted. Since it may be useful to the reader to realize the exact import of these postulates, and to know why these particular truths have been taken as necessary foundations to our study, and not facts of another kind, we give below a brief discussion: first, of the necessity of postulates in general, and then of each of the truths which we have, in particular, postulated for the science of sociology. 

A postulate is a truth or fact which is necessary for the study of a subject, but which is taken for granted because it is already proved by other sciences. 

To make headway in the advancement of knowledge, most sciences have to take as postulates certain truths or facts which are either evident in themselves or which, having already been satisfactorily established in other fields, it would be superfluous to prove again.

A postulate, however, is not a mere hypothesis or theory. If we use such suppositions as a basis for reasoning or as a starting point for investigation, we must be careful that we verify them before drawing conclusions. Unverified theories in another branch of knowledge must never be taken as postulates, for they would certainly not lead to certitude, and they might easily result in error.

As with other sciences, there are certain facts or truths which are necessary as a foundation for the study of sociology, and which, as stated in Chapter I, are either self-evident, or can be 239

amply proved if we wish to study them in detail. That we should believe in the particular postulates set forth in this book makes a great difference to our comprehension of mankind, as will be easily understood. Many non-Catholic sociologists do not believe in them, and for this reason many of their ideas about man's social relations are false, and often dangerous for man's well-being. 

We shall now take each of these postulates in order, and attempt to show why a belief in them is essential if we are to regard man and his social relations in a true light and a correct perspective. Proofs will be found in books of philosophy, psychology, ethics, history, and theology.¹ 

1. God exists, and He is the Creator of all things, man included.

To deny God's existence is to deny all purpose in life. We could not understand man and the universe did we not believe that God created all things. There would be no explanation of the world's wonders; no fundamental reason why we should be moral, why we should be self-sacrificing at times for the good of our fellow men; no hope in an eternal reward in heaven to compensate us for pain and sacrifice here below. To refuse to acknowledge God's existence, and that we are created for His honor and glory, would be well calculated to lead us to despair. 

It might be well to discuss briefly the question of evolution under this heading, for it is a matter on which there has been much misunderstanding, and regarding which much that is pure hypothesis has been taken for granted. 

Those who deny the existence of God deny that the world and the things in the world are created. They affirm that life evolved progressively from inorganic matter to the present state. They believe that originally the earth was nonliving ether dust which, 1For supplementary reading the following might well be consulted:

Catholic Encyclopædia; articles on Ethics, Evolution, Free Will, Immortality of the 

Soul, Law - natural, Rights. Also Ross, E. J., Fundamental Sociology, Chap. 7 (Milwaukee: 

Bruce Publishing Co., 1939).

Haas, Rev. F. J., Man and Society, Chaps. I, III, IV, V (New York: Century Co., 1930). 

Muntsch, Rev. A., S.J., and Spalding, Rev. H. S., S.J., Introductory Sociology, Pt.

2, Chaps. II, V (New York: Heath & Co., 1928). 

Ross, E. J., A Survey of Sociology, Chap. II and Appendix I (Milwaukee: Bruce Pub lishing Co., 1932).

APPENDIX I 

241 in the course of ages, gradually evolved, first into vegetative life; then, from mud and decayed vegetable matter came insects and small animals; later these lower animals gradually developed into higher forms; and eventually the highest of all animals, man, evolved. In this evolutionary theory the last stage before man is reached is usually considered to be ape life. 

Catholics may believe in the evolution of the body, with the following provisos, which are provable by philosophy: 

First, they must believe that God exists. Second, they must believe that God created all things (for it is obvious that there must be a reason for life and movement, and that there must be a power behind the marvelous order in the universe: the order of the seasons, months, days, and nights; the order in the structure of plants and the bodily frame of men). Third, they must believe in some special divine intervention for the formation of Eve's body, based on the authority of Scripture. Fourth, they must believe in the separate creation of each man's soul. There is no space to enter here into the many proofs of these four postulates. 

With these four provisos, therefore, Catholics may hold the theory that God could have confined Himself to only one initial act of creation for all existing things: that He could have created inorganic matter — giving this matter the power within itself to evolve and ultimately to become alive. All things would, in this theory, have been fundamentally created by God, although indirectly with direct creation reserved only for the first initial act, and for each man's soul, and with some special intervention for the formation of Eve's body. Such a theory would be wholly in accord with our notion of God's almighty power: for it would indeed be an almighty act if, æons and æons ago He gave an atom the power to evolve into the whole inorganic world, into all forms of plant life as we know them today, and into all species of animal life, including the wonderfully developed animal we call man. 

If we wish to hold the evolutionary theory, however, besides postulating at least an initial creation of matter by God, we must, as we have just stated, likewise postulate a second act of creation for man's soul. Man's intellect, that is, his thinking principle, is

a faculty (an essential power) of his soul, and distinguishes him from the rest of creation. His soul is a spirit, hence it cannot have evolved and must at some time have been infused into his body, even if the evolution of the body could ever be proved. And all the human race must have descended from the first man into whom a soul was infused, because otherwise the whole human race could not be held accountable for the sin of one man: Adam.

On this fact stands the whole doctrine of our redemption by Christ. Through one man we all lost our supernatural rights to sanctifying grace, to the sonship of God, and to heaven; therefore one Man was to redeem us: Christ.

The whole human race is descended from Adam and Eve, and this unity of human origin is absolutely conformable to all scientific evidence. All human beings, whether they be white, black, yellow, or brown in color, whether they come from the north or the south, from North America or from South America, from China, Africa, or Europe, are all essentially the same in bodily formation, in reasoning powers, in ability to exchange ideas by means of speech, and in certain fundamental character traits, hence they must have descended from the same original parents of the human race.

It must be noted, however, that this idea of evolution of man's body from inorganic matter is a theory only, for it has in no way been proved. No class of animals or plants has ever been known to evolve into another. There are no "missing links," the being in a halfway stage between purely animal life and manhood. All the skeletons averred to be "missing links" have been conclusively proved to belong to one group or the other to animals or man. Between man and the ape, moreover, while there are many apparent similarities there are also many differences, the most striking of which is that man is the only truly erect animal. His pelvis is basin shaped outward, to permit erect carriage, while that of the ape is more like an inverted basin, which makes erect posture impossible. 

2. Man has a spiritual soul which is immortal, hence man has an eternal destiny.

APPENDIX I 

243 Philosophy proves for us that man has a soul, and that his soul is immortal. The importance of taking these truths for granted in the study of man's social relations lies in the fact that if man had no eternal destiny, then the materialists would be justified in working for merely human standards of happiness, and in striving to better man's condition on earth, the question of his future happiness would be of no account. This false assumption would, of course, lead to many abuses and, in fact, does so at the present time.

Then again, if man had not a soul, he would be no better than the brute creation; he would have no rights and no duties toward his fellow men. It is argued that he would undertake social obligations in order to preserve harmony in social life. It is inconceivable, however, that all men would follow their nobler yearnings and be moral in this life when, in so many cases, to do so would be against their natural inclinations and entail hardship on them. 

At least an implicit belief on the part of man in future reward for good done on earth is necessary for social coöperation. And without this implicit belief man would find the inequalities of this present life, with the pain and sufferings that some endure, and the great temporal happiness of others, wholly insupportable. 

3. Man is endowed with a free will. 

Our belief that man has a free will is very important. Many men refuse to consider the proofs furnished by philosophy, and deny man this freedom. They tell us that man has no control over his thoughts, and they take from him all responsibility for his acts, be they good or bad. They presuppose that, given a certain heredity and environment, a man is inevitably bound to become a criminal, or to be a good citizen. If a man does not fulfill his duties, they state that he is not to blame, for undoubtedly his heredity or environment is the cause and inevitably inclined him to disregard those duties. If these ideas were true, it would be useless to strive to better man's temporal condition, which is the main object of social service; and it would be useless to expect anyone to overcome the bad habits he may have acquired. Both morality and law are built upon the doctrine of free will. Without

freedom of will there could be no reward for doing good, and no punishment for crime; there would be no heaven and no hell. We are certainly born with predispositions to good and evil, and our environment usually has some influence on us. As we showed in 

Chapter III, however, our conduct, that is whether we are actually good or bad, depends to some extent on our ideals and attitudes, on the habits we have acquired and ultimately on our will; and this psychological influence is more important than heredity and environment.

ETERNAL LAW CREATION 

4. Man is subject to laws, both physical and moral. 

Every created thing, living and nonliving, irrational and rational, has its own finality; that is, it was created for a particular purpose. 

Everything that exists is willed by God to exist for a particular end. If a thing has no purpose to justify its existence, if God does not will that it should exist, then it would cease to exist. This "divine reason directing in advance the actions. and movements of all existing things" (to use St. Thomas Aquinas's definition) is called the eternal law. The eternal law is, therefore, in God. That part of the eternal law in which creation participates, however, is called the natural law, and this natural law may be divided into the physical law and the moral law. 

PHYSICAL LAW NATURAL LAW MORAL LAW DIVINE HUMAN POSITIVE POSITIVE LAWS LAWS CONSCIENCE 

The laws to which man is subject.

The physical law is the creature's natural inclination (instilled into it by 

God) to its proper end and act. All the things of nature, irrational creatures, and also the physical side of man, are subject to this physical law. Reason discovers these laws by observation, experiment, and induction; for example the laws of physics, chemistry, astronomy, biology, the law that we need food and sleep in order to live, that certain acts will inevitably lead to

APPENDIX I 

245 definite physical results, and by observation the law is known. 

Man, however, is subject to the physical law only on his physical side. He differs from the animal in that he is subject also to the moral law. His reason tells him that certain things are right and others inadvisable or wrong, but he is not forced by a physical law into the observance of that order: he is, owing to his free will, capable of accepting or rejecting it. The moral law, therefore, is the natural law in its application to rational beings; that is, to man. Conscience interprets the natural law for us. We are under its sovereign authority, and our mental integrity is impaired if we refuse to do what, after due reflection, is presented to our mind as right, and therefore necessary for us to do. 

God has deigned to reveal this natural moral law to us more explicitly through the divine positive laws of the Ten Commandments, the teaching of Christ, and the laws of the Catholic Church which Christ founded.

To promote good order in society, man has also made positive laws, such as traffic laws, and laws in regard to voting or the payment of taxes. These are human positive laws, and they must always be in accord with the natural law, physical or moral, else we are not bound to obey them.

If we did not believe that man was subject to moral law there would be chaos in the world: certainly no social harmony. 

5. Man has certain rights and duties which are common to all mankind.

If we are subject to law, we have a duty to observe it; and if we have a duty to observe a law, we have a right to expect that others will not hamper us in the exercise of this duty. We have likewise a duty to respect the rights of others. 

A right is an inviolable moral or legal title and claim to have, to do, to demand, to omit, or to acquire something. A duty is a moral obligation to do whatever the law commands and to omit what it forbids. As stated in Paragraph 16, man's principal rights are to life, liberty, justice, truth, and the ownership of private property; also to live socially, with directive authority (since groups of men cannot come together in harmony and sustained

unity of purpose without a leader and a guide); and the paramount right of man is to perform his duties to his Creator, which means the right to follow his conscience. 

Many subsidiary rights are included under these brief headings, and are discussed in ethics. They are too comprehensive to detail here, but to give a few examples we might state that under man's right to life comes his right to a living wage, and to rest and recreation, in order to maintain life at a reasonable standard of living. Under the same heading comes also man's duty not to commit suicide and not to kill another. Under his right to liberty comes his right to fulfill all his duties, his right to his own opinion, if this opinion is not opposed to truth or to the moral law, and his right to marry or remain single, as he pleases. His right to justice demands that his lawful rights be not taken away from him; that he be not unfairly discriminated against on account of color, race, or creed; that he be given a living wage for work honestly accomplished, and that his good name be preserved. His right to truth includes his right to receive a Catholic education in order to have his mind nourished with truth. His right to property denies the tenets of socialism. 

In accordance with these rights, ethics also teaches us that we have several duties, which may be classed under the following headings: a) Toward God we owe the duty of worship, the acceptance of His Will, and the fulfillment of all our religious obligations. b) Toward ourselves we owe the preservation of our life and our health, both physically and morally. We also owe to ourselves the proper utilization of our spiritual and material powers and possessions. We must be jealous of our honor and reputation. And before all else we owe ourselves the duty of self-respect. Selfreverence demands the recognition of the dignity of oneself as a creature of God with an eternal destiny, and of our body and powers as the instruments for the attainment of that destiny. It demands the performance of all our actions as perfectly as possible. c) Toward our fellow men we owe first, justice, that we do not

APPENDIX I 

247 encroach on their rights; second, we owe charity, according to the divine precept: "Thou shalt love thy neighbor as thyself."2

The importance of recognizing these rights and duties in a study of man's social relations must be obvious. 

6. Christ is the Son of God and He established the 

Church, to which He gave divine authority to guide men in matters related to their natural destiny. 

These facts are proved primarily by the historical records we have of Christ's life, by His revelation, and by miracles. If we believe in the divinity of Christ and His Church, we shall abide by their teachings. If we abide by their teachings we shall not only strive for personal holiness, and to perform perfectly all the duties involved in our social relations, but we shall also try, by prayer and positive good works, to help those in need: this is 

Catholic Action, to which a special chapter has been devoted. 

2Mark xii. 31; cf. also Matt. xxii. 39.

Appendix II THE NATIONAL RECOVERY PROGRAM 

Note: Since the first printing of this book, in September 1934, the 

Supreme Court came to the decision in May, 1935, that the N.R.A. program was unconstitutional. Nevertheless, we retain the following brief history and outline of the program for handy reference. 

The inauguration of a planned economic order was of vital necessity in 1933.

As all readers are aware, the prosperity of the after-war years in the United States came to an end in 1929. All through the 

1920-1929 period speculation had been rampant. Business was booming, and even small investors were speculating in stocks and bonds in the hope of increased demand and higher prices, due to the expectation of larger profits to be divided among stockholders, and in the purposed investment of such profits in further security holdings. Most people bought more goods than they could pay for, making their purchases with borrowed money or on credit, and hoping eventually to meet their debts out of future profits or future earnings. Prices reached unprecedented levels, and were at least 50 per cent higher than in prewar years, as is shown by the accompanying graph of the retail prices of major foods. 

Already by the middle of 1929 signs were evident that this unreasonable credit purchasing had reached its limits. Unable to cover their former commitments, many people became still deeper engulfed in the mire of debt; current purchases were therefore curtailed, and the lessened demand for goods meant that many employees were dismissed. These unemployed then had to reduce their expenditure also, decreasing the demand still more, and thus leading to yet greater unemployment. In the meantime, however, 248

APPENDIX II 

249 abnormal credit transactions on the stock exchanges still continued. Then with the loss of fortunes involved in the stock exchange crash of October, 1929, the depression years arrived; and thousands of bank failures increased the financial distress. 

The Reconstruction Finance Corporation was founded 

January, 1932.

In an effort to save the financial structure of the country, the 210 200 190 180 170 168.3 185.9

To avoid a threatened nation-wide bankruptcy, President

Roosevelt drew up the National Recovery Program. 

Despite the creation of the R.F.C., bank and business failures, with the subsequent unemployment and business distress, increased throughout the country. Immediately after President 

Roosevelt assumed office he began a series of social legislation 1933

calculated to save the country from ruin. Briefly, the chief legislation passed has been as follows:

I. The Federal Securities Act, May, 1933. 

The Federal Securities Act of 1933 was intended to protect the investing public by insisting that, with a few exceptions, all securities must be registered with the Federal Trade Commission before they can be sent through the mails or issued in interstate or foreign commerce. This Act makes the sellers of securities responsible for misrepresentation in the sale of stocks and bonds. 85 80 90

II. The National Industrial Recovery Act, June, 1933. 

This Act was drawn up in order to check the "widespread unemployment and disorganization of industry," by inducing and maintaining "united action of labor and management under adequate governmental sanctions and supervision." There was much similarity between many of the provisions of the Act and Pope Pius XI's scheme outlined in Chapter X. The three parts of the 

Act (called "Titles") were as follows: 

Title 1. All trades, industries, or subdivisions of trades or in dustries were urged to draw up voluntary codes of fair competition. The trade or industrial associations or groups drawing up these codes were to be truly representative of their class, and in the best interest of all employers, workers, and consumers. The 

"National Industrial Recovery Act, Title I, Section I.

APPENDIX II 

251 codes were not to promote monopolies, neither were they to eliminate nor oppress small enterprises. They were to set up reasonable standards for hours, wages, and conditions of employment, with due regard to retail prices. Hours of labor were to be less than were the average working hours at the time of the passing of the Act, with the idea of giving employment to as many jobless as is practicable. Standard minimum wage payments set up by the various codes were to be sufficient to provide the worker with a "living wage." Even a minority in any trade, industry, or subdivision, who might have contested the setting up of a code, was to be held by its provisions once the President was satisfied that the particular code in question was just and equitable. Violations of any duly approved code was to be punishable by law. 

If a trade, industry, or subdivision of a trade or industry, did not draw up its own code voluntarily, then after due notice and a public hearing, the President had power under this Act to inpose a code on that trade, industry, or subdivision. Violations of an involuntary code of this nature were also to be punishable by law.

To assist in the operation of the Act special advisory boards were set up by the President, one each for employers, workers, and consumers. A Labor Mediation Board was likewise limited, to act as arbitrator in the event of disputes between employers and labor, with subsidiary local committees working under this national board in various parts of the country. 

The Act did not cover professional people, executives, domestic servants, agricultural workers, and engineers. It must be mentioned, too, that codes drawn up under this Act, whether voluntary or involuntary, were to take effect for two years only, so that mistakes discovered in them could be rectified.

Title 2. The second part of the Act dealt with the power given to the President to appropriate $3,300,000,000 for public works, to give temporary relief to such unemployed workers as could not be absorbed by the additional employment brought about by the effects of the industrial "Title" of the Act, outlined above. 

Title 3. The third section provided for federal emergency re lief to the unemployed.

III. The Farm Relief and Farm Credit Acts. 

Separate legislation, too detailed to discuss here, was also passed in the summer of 1933 to relieve the unprecedented depression in the agricultural industry. Its main object was to increase the purchasing power of farmers, to refinance farm mortgages at lower rates of interest, and to bring about a rise in commodity prices by limiting production. The rise in the retail prices of foodstuffs, as indicated by the accompanying table, is a reflection of the increased price of farm produce.

IV. The Glass-Steagall Bank Act, June, 1933. 

This Act was passed to protect the small depositor by providing for government insurance of bank deposits, and better governmental control of the banks. The Federal Deposit Insurance 

Corporation was created to carry out the provisions of this Act. 

V. The Home Owners' Loan Act. 

Under this Act the Home Owners' Loan Corporation was created by the government with a capital of $200,000,000 to be many persons of moderate means from losing homes bought on used to refinance home mortgage indebtedness, and thus save the installment system during the boom years, or on which mortgage payments were overdue.

The National Recovery Act seemingly proved unsuccessful. 

We cannot go into the many reasons for the seeming partial failure of the N.R.A. Dishonesty of many employers who professed to be members, yet did not keep their word; the great difficulty of supervision, and many other causes of the failure may be given. 

Abbreviations used for the names of the best known administrative bodies formed under the National Recovery Program.

AAA. Agricultural Adjustment Administration. Founded for the administration of agreements between producers and distributors of agricultural products, and to bring relief to farmers. 

CAB. Consumers Advisory Board. Founded to investigate complaints about prices, under the N.R.A. administration. 

CCC. Civilian Conservation Corps. The corps of civilian workers on reforestation and conservation projects. 

CCC. Commodity Credit Corporation. Founded to have charge of loans for commodity purchases.

1. Federal Farm Board. 

2. Federal Farm Loan Bureau.

LAB. Labor Advisory Board. Founded to appeal, when necessary, to labor to arbitrate in strikes and lockouts. 

NCB. National Compliance Board. Founded to attempt adjustments of complaints regarding code violations; to recommend exceptions from code provisions; to order the withdrawal of the Blue Eagle from violators of the N.R.A.

NLB. National Labor Board. Founded to deal with labor problems, such as strikes.

NLRB. National Labor Relations Board.

NRA. National Recovery Act. Drawn up to check unemployment and disorganization of industry.

UNIT TWENTY-EIGHT (§§ 60-62) 

1. In what way did the domestic system of industry foster a more natural home life than the present factory system? 

2. Can you name five families in your neighborhood who carry on a home industry, and where no member works away from home? 

3. Why did workmen under the gild system usually take a greater interest in the perfection of their task than the present-day worker? 

4. What is meant by (a) the industrial revolution, (b) laissez faire? 

5. In what respects was the lot of the laborer in the days of the manorial system (a) better, and (b) worse than at the present time? 

6. What led to the improvement in the workers' condition after the industrial revolution? 

7. How did the industrial revolution enable large fortunes to be made

by some; yet lead to the utter destitution of others? Were all fortunes made by unjust means?

American farmer?

2. Prove that capital and labor are mutually interdependent. What service does the capitalist perform for society? 

3. Although the factory system has taken the worker away from home for the greater part of the day, and has made his work more monotonous than in former times, has he benefited in any way from the mass production of goods by machinery? If so, how? 

4. How is industry financed at the present time?

Questions 

UNIT THIRTY-TWO (§§ 67-70) 

1. What conditions under which workers labor need special consideration by social workers? 

2. What is the social importance of a living wage for the worker? Do wage payments in any way affect good citizenship? 

3. What is meant by the terms: living wage; family allowances; minimum wage law? 

4. Would all workers be properly treated if there were a standard wage for all, sufficient to enable everyone to live in comfort? 

5. What can be done to remedy bad working conditions? 

6. What laws has your state made as regards wages? 

7. What are the provisions of the Federal Fair Labor Standards Act of 1938 as regards (a) minimum wages; (b) payment for overtime? 

8. Why does the worker need legal protection against the employer? 

9. Why is the payment of a living wage to be regarded as a "minimum"? 

10. Is the worker better off as regards the average number of working hours than at the beginning of this century? If so, to what does he owe the improvement? 

11. Why do workers need a certain amount of rest? Would home life be fostered if the working day were only five or six hours? 

12. Visit a local factory and find out what is being done there for the workers' health, safety, and general welfare. What part of these provisions are compulsory by law?

UNIT THIRTY-THREE (§§ 71-72)

3. Is the child worker better off in the country or in the city?

4. Why does the trade cycle bring more frequent depression periods than a half century ago? How can the state help to remedy the unemployment distress which is caused in this manner? 

5. What is meant by technological unemployment? Can it be remedied in any way? 

6. Which are the most important causes of unemployment — personal or economic? Why? 

7. When choosing a career for future work, can you find some in which there will be less chances for unemployment than in others? How do (a) lack of education, (b) lack of vocational training, (c) child labor affect the employment of the worker in later years?

6. Discuss the various features of bad housing which may result in quarreling within the family. 

7. What is the connection between bad housing conditions and (a) ill health, (b) juvenile delinquency?

UNIT FIFTY-EIGHT (§ 125) 

1. Explain the terms "land overcrowding," "room overcrowding"? 

2. Name the four most important causes of bad housing conditions. 

3. How could a city prevent the industrial causes of bad housing? 

4. Why do slum dwellers frequently prefer to stay in their present surroundings rather than accept better conditions in the suburbs at the same rental? Can you propose any remedies for this situation? 

Would the universal payment of a living wage be sufficient? 

5. Could the government legislate for the protection of workers against greedy landlords? In what ways? 

6. What are the responsibilities of a citizen as regards bad housing conditions in his district? 

7. Will voluntary efforts on the part of citizens suffice to remedy the evil? If not, why? 

8. What part can the school play in remedying the slum problem? 

9. Do slum tenants frequently need to take greater interest in their homes? In what way?

RECOMMENDED READINGS AND QUESTIONS 281 

10. Do landlords or tenants resent the interest of agencies working for better housing conditions? What might be done to remedy this situation? Are there any such agencies in your district? Do you think you should belong to any of these when you leave school? If so, why? 

11. Is it a duty of Catholics to establish residential clubs for workers? 

Discuss this question.

UNIT FIFTY-NINE (§§ 126-128) 

1. Show how the recreation group plays an important part in connection with social and psychic environmental influence on man. 

2. Discuss the statement: "The play impulse is nature's way of developing body, mind, and character" (Bogardus). 

3. Draw up a schedule to show how much leisure time you have each day, and exactly how you spend it. 

4. What is meant by passive and active recreation? Are both useful? Why? 

5. If you took little interest in passive amusement when you were young, would this have any effect on your life if you became an invalid in middle age? Discuss. 

6. Discuss the connection between the economic system at the present day and recreation. 

7. Why is it important for the state that all the citizens should employ their leisure time well? 

8. What is (a) the mental, and (b) the moral value of recreation? 

9. Why is it more essential for children than for adults to have opportunity for physical recreation? 

10. How many forms of recreation are actually available to you? Do you take advantage of them all? Why not? Would it be better for you if you extended your recreational interests, and if so, why? 

11. Why is it useful for everyone to have a hobby in addition to interest in other recreation?

UNIT SIXTY (§§ 129-131) 

1. What forms of recreation are provided for you in your home? If you think an extension of these home recreation facilities desirable, how can you tactfully try to improve the situation? 

2. How has modern city life affected home recreational activities? Is there more or less home recreation now than a few years ago? 

3. Discuss the value of commercialized recreation. 

4. What is meant by commercialized play? Do you spend much money in this form of amusement? If so, give all the reasons you can think of to justify your habit, and state what remedies you think might be needed if the amount of time and money you now spend is excessive.

5. What forms of good amusements would citizens have to forego if all commercialized play were abolished? Would this be advisable? 

6. Discuss the value and necessity of organized recreation (a) for children, (b) for adolescents, (c) for adults. 

7. Which do you prefer: to be left to your own devices, or to have your recreation organized for you? Why?

UNIT SIXTY-ONE (§§ 132-134) 

1. What is meant by a social settlement? Visit a settlement in your district, if there is one, and make a report on what you saw there. 

2. Why is it advisable that Catholics should have, if possible, their own provisions for recreation? 

3. Could the family and the Church provide for all the recreational needs of a person, or is the assistance of private organizations and the state advisable? In what ways? 

4. Discuss the recreational facilities offered by your parish. Does your family take sufficient advantage of them? Ought it to take more interest in them? Ought the parish to supplement the facilities already provided? In what way? 

5. Discuss the value of parks to the community. 

6. Before you go to a play or a movie what precautions ought you to take? 

7. List the benefits of summer camps. How might the number of camps available to children be extended? 

8. Write to the Playground and Recreation Association of America for a list of their publications, and discover whether you think any of them would be useful to your school.
